Re-Implementing :nth-child() With Modern CSS

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The title sounds a bit strange, right? Why re-implement an existing feature, that’s completely useless! I have two reasons for that. The first reason is to be able to adjust the :nth-child() arguments on the fly.

And you decide to update the 5 or the 8. The only way is to manually rewrite the selector, which isn’t ideal. We cannot have something like the below where you can change the variables as you want:

With my implementation, you will be able to do it!

The second reason is to have fun and play with some new features. You will probably never need to rely on a dynamic :nth-child() selector, but I am sure you will learn one or two useful tricks along the way.

We will rely on the same code structure in all the examples: a container with a bunch of

elements inside it.

And the container controls the variables of :nth-child():

I am not saying that this structure is mandatory for the implementation. I just considered a simple one to keep things easy to follow.

Let’s start with the simplest case, a selector that selects only one element:

I don’t think I need to explain what this selector does. The 5 is nothing but the index of the selected element. So, if B is equal to the index of the element, that element is selected.

We can translate this into the following CSS code:

I am using the new if() syntax that allows me to define a condition within style(). If that condition is true, the background color is set to red. Otherwise, nothing happens.

The if() syntax is not that complex. In our case, we only have one condition, so you can see it as follows:

The tricky part is how to define the condition within style(). I wrote a small post explaining this part, so do yourself a favor and go read it before we continue: “The Hidden Trick of Style Queries and if().” It’s a quick reading with crucial details that helps you better understand how if() works. From there, you will notice that our condition can be written several ways that are all valid:

We have our first implementation! You can easily apply it to any property as well:

The code looks a bit redundant, which is probably one of the drawbacks of this method, but it’s still cool to implement the selector and be able to adjust the value of --B on the fly. Don’t forget that our goal is to learn new tricks, and you already have two of them in your pocket (sibling-index() and if()).

Here is an interactive demo where you can adjust the value of --B and see the changes:

Let’s move to our next selector. It selects more than one element.

Think for a moment about how to implement such a selector. It’s almost the same as the previous one.

If the index of the element is greater than or equal to the value of --B, the element is selected. As simple as that!

What about this one? Easy, right? We simply use <= instead of >=!

We can also easily implement a range selection, which is the combination of both selectors:

The code should be self-explanatory. It’s even more intuitive than the :nth-child() notation, which requires a few minutes of deciphering if you are not used to it.

Now let’s deal with the A argument. For example, the following selector:

…selects the 3rd, 6th, 9th, etc. elements. Seen differently, the index of the element needs to be a multiple of 3. To do such testing, we can use the mod() function. If mod(index, 3) returns 0, it means the remainder of the division of index by 3 is equal to 0, hence, it’s a multiple of 3.

It works! Another selector with a simple implementation.

We can identify two special cases. When --A is equal to 1, we have :nth-child(n), which selects all the elements and when it’s equal to 2, we have :nth-child(2n) which is also :nth-child(even).

While this works fine, there is a small gotcha. When A is a negative value, the selector should select nothing which is logical as all the indexes are positive, but if you try using negative values with my implementation, some elements still get selected which is wrong.

The mod() function works with negative numbers and can still return 0 in such cases, so to be more accurate, we should test the sign of A.

See that? I simply added another condition inside style() using the and keyword. The syntax of if() may sound confusing at first glance, but at the end it’s the same as any other language: a bunch of conditions defined inside style(). You can have as many as you want combined with and and or.

Now let’s combine all the tricks we learned so far to implement the generic form of :nth-child(). In this case, the index is equal to An + B, and we can express n like this:

n should be a positive integer (zero included). For the positive part, we can use:

For the integer part, we need to make sure index - B is a multiple of A, and for this, we use mod():

We translate this into CSS to get the following:

We are almost good except for the case when --A is equal to 0. In that case, the selection is simply wrong. The mod() results in NaN since its second argument is equal to 0, and this will mess with the condition. Actually, that condition is not meant to work with --A equal to 0.

We either consider that this implementation is only valid for a value of --A different from 0 (which is probably what we want) or we try to find a solution to include that case.

We are here to learn and discover new stuff, so let’s try something else. There are probably a lot of ways to fix the case of 0, but here is my idea:

How does it work? Well, this will be your homework!

As you can see, I have introduced a new variable that should be equal to 1 if the element is selected by :nth-child(An + B). Try to dissect the code to understand how it works. If you still struggle, check the following post: “Dynamic nth-child() using sibling-index() andif().”

And here is the final interactive with the complete implementation of :nth-child(An + B):

We can easily get all the previous selectors by simply adjusting the variables:

I hope you enjoyed this small exercise. It was a good opportunity to explore the new if() feature, and the :nth-child() selector was the perfect candidate for this. After all, selectors are nothing but a set of conditions to filter elements and select the needed ones.

What about :nth-last-child()? If you are up for another challenge, try to see how you can update the previous implementation to make it work with :nth-last-child(). (Hint: you may need to rely on sibling-count().)

FEATURE | Werder Bremen are moving in the right direction under Daniel Thioune as unbeaten run continues

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Werder Bremen’s remarkable transformation under Daniel Thioune continued with a convincing 2-0 home victory over Hoffenheim, extending their unbeaten Bundesliga run to seven matches and providing further evidence that the club is moving in the right direction.

After spending much of last season battling against relegation, Bremen are now playing with confidence, determination and a clear tactical identity. What initially appeared to be a campaign focused on avoiding another relegation battle is gradually developing into something considerably more exciting.

Perhaps the most impressive aspect of Bremen’s recent form is their ability to win matches in different ways.

Their dramatic comeback against Augsburg demonstrated resilience, while the draw against Borussia Dortmund showed they could compete with stronger opposition. Victories over Mainz and Hoffenheim have now added another important element: consistency.

Thioune deserves considerable credit for creating an environment in which players understand their responsibilities and are willing to fight for one another.

Bremen are no longer a team that appears vulnerable whenever opponents increase the pressure. Instead, they have developed the confidence to manage difficult situations and remain competitive throughout matches.

It is impossible to discuss Bremen’s resurgence without mentioning Niclas Füllkrug.

The German international scored his ninth Bundesliga goal of the season against Hoffenheim, opening the scoring in the 57th minute before Eren Dinkçi secured the victory with an excellent finish in the 82nd minute.

Füllkrug has now scored in seven consecutive Bundesliga matches, matching a remarkable achievement last accomplished by Werder legend Rudi Völler during the 1982/83 season. No Bremen player has ever scored nine goals in the opening eight Bundesliga matchdays.

His influence extends beyond goals. Füllkrug gives Bremen a reliable attacking focal point, brings experience to a relatively young squad and provides the leadership required during difficult moments.

His return has transformed Bremen’s attack, but Thioune deserves credit for creating a system that allows the striker to thrive.

Bremen’s improvement becomes even more impressive when considering their recent history.

For several seasons, the club have struggled to establish themselves as consistent Bundesliga contenders. Relegation in 2021 and subsequent battles near the bottom of the table highlighted how far Werder had fallen from their successful years under Thomas Schaaf.

Thioune’s work is gradually changing that narrative.

The victory over Hoffenheim also represented an important defensive improvement. Bremen had conceded 14 goals in their opening seven Bundesliga matches, making their clean sheet particularly encouraging.

It would be premature to describe Bremen as genuine European contenders after only eight matches. However, their unbeaten run, Füllkrug’s extraordinary goalscoring form and the confidence developing throughout the squad suggest that something promising is happening at the Weserstadion.

The challenge now is maintaining that momentum.

For the first time in several seasons, Werder supporters have reasons to look towards the upper half of the Bundesliga rather than constantly worrying about the relegation places.

Thioune has not simply improved Bremen’s results. He is beginning to restore the belief that this historic club can once again compete for something more meaningful than survival.

FEATURE | Five standout players from Bundesliga Matchday 8

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Bundesliga Matchday 8 delivered another entertaining weekend of German football, with Bayern and Borussia Dortmund sharing the points in Der Klassiker, Stuttgart producing an impressive away victory against Bayer Leverkusen and Werder Bremen extending their unbeaten run under Daniel Thioune.

Freiburg also completed a comeback against Augsburg, while newly promoted Elversberg continued to impress with a convincing victory over Mainz. With Bayern and Dortmund level on 18 points at the top of the Bundesliga, the competition is already developing into an intriguing campaign.

Here are five players whose performances made the difference for their teams.

Ermedin Demirović delivered one of the outstanding performances of Matchday 8 as Stuttgart secured an impressive 4-2 victory away at Bayer Leverkusen.

The Bosnian striker opened the scoring in the 14th minute before adding his second goal in the 65th, helping Stuttgart secure three valuable points against a direct competitor for the European positions.

Jamie Leweling and Bilal El Khannouss also scored for the visitors, while Patrik Schick and Ibrahim Maza found the net for Leverkusen.

Demirović’s brace demonstrated his importance to Stuttgart’s attacking system. His movement inside the penalty area and clinical finishing helped his team respond to a difficult start to the Bundesliga season.

Niclas Füllkrug continues to demonstrate why his return to Werder Bremen has been one of the most successful transfers of the season.

The German international scored the opening goal in Bremen’s 2-0 victory over Hoffenheim, breaking the deadlock in the 57th minute before Eren Dinkçi secured the result late in the second half.

Füllkrug has now scored nine goals in eight Bundesliga matches, continuing his remarkable goalscoring form since returning to the Weserstadion.

His contribution extends beyond finishing. His physical presence, ability to hold up possession and leadership have become essential components of Thioune’s attacking system.

With Bremen now fifth in the Bundesliga on 15 points, Füllkrug’s performances are helping transform the club’s ambitions from avoiding relegation to potentially challenging for European qualification.

Konrad Laimer delivered an impressive performance in Bayern Munich’s dramatic 2-2 draw against Borussia Dortmund at the Allianz Arena.

The Austrian opened the scoring in the 38th minute, reacting quickly after Gregor Kobel saved his initial effort. It was Laimer’s first Bundesliga goal of the season and gave Vincent Kompany’s side the advantage in Der Klassiker.

Beyond his goal, Laimer provided an important attacking outlet from right-back, repeatedly advancing into dangerous positions and creating problems for Dortmund’s defence.

Although Bayern eventually conceded a late equaliser through Fábio Silva, Laimer’s contribution earned him the Bundesliga’s official Man of the Match award.

His performance demonstrated the importance of having versatile players capable of contributing both defensively and offensively in Kompany’s system.

Felix Keidel was instrumental in Elversberg’s impressive 4-1 victory over Mainz, helping the newly promoted side secure their fourth Bundesliga win of the season.

The midfielder opened the scoring in the seventh minute with an excellent free-kick before delivering the corner that led to Maurice Krattenmacher’s goal shortly before half-time.

Francis Onyeka and Cole Campbell also scored for Elversberg, while Benedict Hollerbach netted a late consolation for Mainz.

Keidel’s contribution was particularly impressive because of his influence from set pieces. His delivery, technical ability and composure helped Elversberg establish control during an outstanding first-half performance.

With Elversberg now seventh in the Bundesliga on 13 points, Keidel’s display highlighted the growing confidence of a team that continues to exceed expectations in its first top-flight campaign.

Igor Matanović changed the momentum of Freiburg’s encounter against Augsburg, helping Julian Schuster’s side secure an important 2-1 away victory.

With Freiburg trailing 1-0 at half-time following Michael Gregoritsch’s opener, Matanović was introduced at the beginning of the second half.

The Croatian striker equalised in the 65th minute, converting from inside the penalty area after Keisuke Goto’s involvement in the build-up. Cyriaque Irié subsequently completed the comeback with an 85th-minute header.

Matanović’s goal was his fourth of the Bundesliga season, while his impact from the bench earned him the official Man of the Match award.

His performance demonstrated Freiburg’s attacking depth and Schuster’s ability to influence matches through substitutions. With Freiburg sitting third after eight matches, contributions like Matanović’s could prove essential in their pursuit of another European qualification.

Matchday 8 provided another collection of outstanding individual performances. Demirović’s brace inspired Stuttgart, Füllkrug continued his remarkable goalscoring run, Laimer impressed in Der Klassiker, Keidel helped Elversberg secure another memorable victory and Matanović transformed Freiburg’s match from the bench.

Each player contributed in a different way, demonstrating the individual quality and unpredictability that continue to make the Bundesliga one of Europe’s most entertaining competitions.

Fridges and pigs and scoring goals

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

We live in strange times. Sorry to start your day with this shocking revelation, I realise this is a bit of a bolt from the blue, but it’s fact. All across Europe supermarket fridges are being hacked. What a time to be alive. They used to hack, I dunno, mainframes and banks and shit, but now they’re hacking fridges.

Maybe they just got bored taking billions of made-up money from CryptoSloth and BitWomble and needed a challenge. I feel like fridges ought to be the kind of thing you just can’t hack – beyond unplugging it – but what do I know? Anyway, it’s an example of how the inter-connectivity that brings us all together every day is being weaponised and now the sliced meats in your local store are gone off because Neo pwned some large cold cupboards. As if we don’t have enough to deal with.

Such as all the people who hate the way Arsenal play. Let me be very clear, my days of caring what other people think about this football club, or pretty much anything else that’s important to me, are gone. It’s a waste of time. It’s a bit like quote that’s often attributed to George Bernard Shaw, which says ‘Never wrestle with pigs. You both get dirty and the pig likes it.’

I don’t think he actually said that, but I think we can all connect with the meaning. It applies so much to social media, and to many of the ways we’re exposed to punditry and ‘expert’ analysis in football these days. Like one of those shows where you have a couple of ex-pro pigs bleating on about this and that, and then a collection of other little pigs who support all the clubs argue with them and amongst themselves, and it’s just a pigstravaganza of pointless, inane bollocks but it does #numbers on socials so is seen as valuable #content.

I understand why opposition fans go on and on about how much they hate how Arsenal play. It’s because we win a lot of games. That’s the thing that really irks them. That’s just so basic though, you can’t appear to be clever and an aficionado of the game if you just say ‘I don’t like it when they win’, so it’s dressed up as dismissal of style or something else.

And look, here’s where the strange world we live in comes back into focus. You can, and really should, be able to hold more than one thought in your brain – about football or anything else – that holds true. I absolutely love how good we are defensively. LOVE IT. The commitment with which we are determined not to give the opposition anything is truly something to relish. Players laser-focused on their one on ones; putting bodies on the line to block shots and crosses; 11 players moving like a murmuration of birds because they’re so well drilled and organised; off the awareness and discipline; brilliantly timed tackles and headers and clearances; lads sticking up for each other when the opposition get a bit snide, as we saw on Sunday with Jurrien Timber and Noni Madueke refusing to allow their captain to be treated that way; it’s all part of a brilliantly defiant defensive attitude that runs right through this team.

Creating that is not easy. As Arsenal fans we have endured defences that were porous and feeble and, at times, very, very stupid. I do not long for a return to those times. Over the years Mikel Arteta has assembled a collection of players who cannot wait to go out there and do the dirty work of defending, and it runs from front to back because that’s what he demands. If it was that easy, everyone would do it, so to castigate a team’s brilliance in one department because you just lost to them without a shot on target just comes across as sour grapes.

Here’s the other thought though. I’d really like us to be a bit better from an attacking perspective, and when I look at this team and what it’s capable of, there’s no question the forward line remains the area where you can see the room for improvement. We defend as well as we do because, more often than you or I might like, we have to – because we live through games of fine margins. A 1-0 to the Arsenal is part of our history, hard-wired into our supporter DNA (particularly if you’re of a particular vintage), but I can’t lie and wish there weren’t a few more 3-0s to the Arsenal.

I’m absolutely certain Mikel Arteta would like that too, and I think it’s a bit too easy to say that this is a team that plays precisely the football he wants it to play. He is, as we all understand, an arch pragmatist, so what we get is a team that leans into its strengths, as laid out above. He is characterised as a defensive coach, which I get, but a few seasons ago we found an attacking cocktail that drove a title challenge, and saw us score more goals that year than the Invincibles did when they won the league in 2004 (91 to 73).

I think he wants both. He wants that parsimony at the back and the firepower up front. Unfortunately, forward line recruitment is the most difficult part of transfer business, and while he as manager has a part to play in that, as a club our track record is quite poor in that regard. This summer’s window was a complete bust from an attacking perspective, not a good look when you’re pursuing players like Morgan Rogers, Vinicius Junior and Julian Alvarez. When you then let three forwards go and add just one, there are questions to be asked of the people in charge. Arteta among them, of course, but he’s not the one whose job it is to get the deals done.

It’s why there are so many conversations around January, and what we might do. We see other clubs recruit forwards who produce on a consistent basis, while ours too often struggle to find that end product. Is it our system? Would these players find it hard to get those goals for us? Reasonable questions, but the bottom line in my opinion is that we have A+ options at the back and in midfield, while in that front three we’re a bit C-. That’s where the room for improvement is, that’s where the recruitment needs to be better (certainly better than no recruitment at all), and that’s how the games we currently win by a single goal margin, often by virtue of our defensive strength, become a bit more routine.

Simplistic? Yes. Easier said than done? Quite probably. But necessary? Absolutely. Until then though, expect more defensive masterclasses, more pig wrestling, and perhaps someone could hack our own Swedish fridge into scoring a few more goals. Worth a try!

Right, I’ll leave it there for now. I just have a small favour to ask. Arseblog has been nominated for Best Fan Media (Fran Merida) in the 2026 FSA Awards. We’re trying to retain our title, so if you could take a moment to give us a vote that would be much appreciated. You can do that here – https://www.surveymonkey.com/r/fsa-awards-2026

Padilla and Gramaglia earn Granadilla Tenerife 2-0 Liga F win over Athletic Club

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

To help deliver timely coverage of women's football, we may use AI tools to support content creation and editing. While we strive for accuracy and editorial quality, readers should independently verify important information.

Granadilla Tenerife beat Athletic Club 2-0 in Liga F on Monday 2 November, with Padilla and Gramaglia scoring after a goalless first half in Santa Cruz de Tenerife. Athletic had gone four league matches without defeat before the trip to the island.

Neither side found a way through before half-time. The only incident of note came in the 42nd minute, when Quiles was booked for a foul.

The deadlock lasted until the 51st minute. Zaremba provided the assist and Padilla finished to put the hosts ahead.

Athletic picked up two bookings as they chased the game. Martínez was shown a yellow card in the 53rd minute and Valero followed her into the referee’s notebook in the 73rd. Granadilla’s Muina Asla was cautioned in the 79th minute, leaving the card count level at two apiece for referee Calvo.

Gramaglia settled it in the 89th minute. Her goal doubled the lead and sealed a clean sheet for the home side.

This was another strong evening for Granadilla in this fixture. Athletic have now failed to score in the last three meetings: a 0-0 draw in August 2025, a 5-0 defeat on the island in January 2026 and this result. Granadilla have also won their last three home games against the Basque side, going back to a 2-1 victory on 2 November 2024.

The win continues a productive run for the hosts, who have taken four victories from their last five matches. Their only defeat in that stretch was a 3-2 loss to Real Madrid.

After nine rounds, Athletic are fifth on 15 points with a record of four wins, three draws and two defeats. Granadilla are sixth on 14 points, and their goal difference now stands at zero.

Both sides sit within touching distance of the places above. Atlético Madrid are fourth on 16 points and Madrid CFF third on 17, while Barcelona lead on 24 ahead of Real Madrid on 22.

Athletic face the harder assignment next. They host second-placed Real Madrid on Saturday 7 November. Granadilla are at home again on Sunday 8 November, when 14th-placed Deportivo Alavés, who have 8 points from nine games, visit.

Review: Mountain Equipment Switch Base Zip T

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The Mountain Equipment Switch Base Zip T is an ultralight, close-fitting base layer designed for high-output cold-weather activities. It is made with Octa Fleece, a warm, highly breathable ultralight fleece similar to Polartec Alpha Direct, but much more durable, and pairs well with a windbreaker or winter shell jacket.

If you’re not familiar with Mountain Equipment, they’re a highly regarded British manufacturer that has designed technical outdoor gear for mountaineering and polar exploration since 1961. They make a wide range of products, including technical clothing, sleeping bags, and mountaineering backpacks. Backcountry.com carries their products in the United States. I’ve been recommending Mountain Equipment’s men’s Ibex and women’s Chamois softshell pants for winter hiking for years, and they’re still best in class.

The top uses 20-denier, 100% Octa Fleece, made from a specialized hollow polyester fiber with eight radial projections that give the yarn a gear- or octopus-like cross-section. The hollow center and projecting “fins” create insulating air spaces that trap warm air, letting water vapor escape. Octa Fleece has a similar warmth-to-weight ratio as Polartec Alpha Direct but is more durable, which is why larger clothing manufacturers such as Arc’teryx and Mountain Hardwear use it and are developing products with it. See our FAQ for a more detailed comparison of Octa Fleece and Polartec Alpha Direct.

The inside of the Switch Base Zip-T is a fuzzy, warm fabric that is a delight to wear next to the skin or over a thin base layer, while the outer surface is made of highly breathable, abrasion-resistant mesh.

The Switch Base Zip-T can be worn as a base layer or a mid-layer over a thin base. The interior has a fuzzy fleece texture that feels warm and comfortable. Flatlocked seams improve next-to-skin comfort by reducing bulk and minimizing potential irritation. The exterior is an open and breathable mesh that is much more abrasion-resistant. Thumbhole loops help keep the sleeves in place when adding a midlayer or waterproof shell.

Nylon shoulder overlays protect an area that sees regular friction from backpack straps.  This added reinforcement improves the top’s suitability for regular mountain use without significantly compromising its lightweight design.

A deep half-zip provides practical ventilation when body heat builds, making the Switch Base Zip T more adaptable than a standard crew-neck baselayer. Similarly, the loose wrists let you pull the sleeves up your arms to vent more heat. I’ve found the Switch to be a delightful base and mid-layer, worn by itself or under a windbreaker on a chilly morning. The fabric is more wind-resistant than a mid-layer hoodie made with Polartec Alpha Direct, and I like using a pullover like it in winter more than a hoodie because it’s easier to layer with the shells and headgear (hats) I typically wear.

The Switch Base Zip T is designed for a wide range of cold-weather activities, including:

It performs best during activities that involve frequent changes in effort and temperature since you can vent it so easily or layer it with a windbreaker or shell to increase wind resistance and heat retention. It also delivers warmth well above its weight, making it a lightweight midlayer that packs an outsized amount of warmth.

The Mountain Equipment Switch Base Zip T is a well-designed cold-weather or winter base layer for active users who prioritize low weight, quick drying, and effective ventilation. Its Octa Fleec fabric provides lightweight warmth while helping manage moisture, and the half-zip makes it easy to regulate temperature during demanding climbs and ski tours. It is not the best choice for maximum insulation when activity levels are low, but that is not its main purpose. For fast-moving winter adventures, the combination of comfortable seams, reinforced shoulders, and quick-drying performance makes the Switch Base Zip T a strong and practical choice.

Disclosure: Mountain Equipment donated a garment for review.

For those of us in the New England area who visit North Conway, NH, Savage Mountain Gear also sells Mountain Equipment. I’ve picked up a couple of ME items, and they’re legit.

I Tried the New AirDoctor 1500 Tower—And It’s the Perfect Size for Eliminating Kitchen Odors

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

It happens to the best of us. You’re minding your own business as dessert bubbles in the oven or dinner sears on the stove, then—bam! The smell of burning reaches your nose, and before you know it, you’re dragging out a stepladder to silence the blaring smoke alarm.

I hate to say that this is a fairly frequent scenario in my kitchen. While I do my best to prevent pans of lasagna from overflowing and exercise caution when searing steak, accidents still occur. The only difference is that I now have a solution to clear the lingering smell from my space.

I’ve been testing the AirDoctor 1500 air purifier for two months, and it’s a portable yet powerful option for eliminating pesky kitchen odors. Here’s everything you need to know.

Banish bad odors from your kitchen with this mini-yet-mighty air purifier.

The AirDoctor 1500 is a high-performance air purifier packed into a sleek, compact package. Unlike other air purifiers that can take up as much space as a dehumidifier, the 1500 measures less than 10 inches wide and 21 inches high. It features three filters: a pre-filter for trapping larger particles like pet hair, a carbon and VOC filter for filtering odors and an UltraHEPA for capturing everything else. It’s best suited for small-to-medium spaces up to 320 square feet and comes in black or white.

I’ve been testing the AirDoctor 1500 air purifier since its launch in September 2026. I’ve used it in my kitchen, entryway, office and family room.

The AirDoctor arrived on my doorstep in a sturdy brown box. It was well-packaged to prevent damage during shipping, and setup couldn’t be easier. All you have to do is lift the unit out of the box, remove the plastic bag, peel off a few stickers and, most importantly, set up the filter.

The filter housing snaps off easily, and installation is as simple as removing the filter from its packaging and popping it back in. In all the air purifiers I’ve tested, AirDoctor has the most seamless filter access, thanks to built-in magnets in the housing. This is important not only for changing the filter but also for cleaning it.

After completing setup, I plugged in the AirDoctor and turned it on. It immediately whirred to life, starting at high speed with a red light and working its way down to a low-speed setting. The control panel is reactive to a simple touch, with options for speed (auto, one, two, three or four), ion, dim and timer. The interface also includes a light that signals when the filter needs to be checked or replaced.

As the air purifier got to work, I was impressed with how little sound it produced. On level one, you can barely hear it. It’s a slightly audible white noise that blends easily into the background. Level four is obviously the loudest, similar to a fan on high speed. If you’re very sensitive to noise, you might find any air purifier, including the AirDoctor, too loud to sleep with. But in a kitchen, the whir blends right into the din of cooking, a running dishwasher and other ambient sounds.

Within a week of the AirDoctor being in my space, it faced its first real challenge. I had a potpie galette in a 425-degree oven, and some of the filling started to leak onto the pizza steel it was baking on and burn. Within minutes, my oven filled with smoke. I managed to get the galette out without setting off the alarms, but my space was filled with an unpleasant, burning-vegetable odor.

I turned on the AirDoctor, set it to auto, and it immediately kicked into high gear. The light turned red—a sign that it senses poor air quality—and the fan worked overtime to cleanse the air. After about 15 minutes, the light turned yellow—moderate air quality—and I could already sense a difference. Within an hour, it was back to a blue light, which signifies good air quality, and it’s like the accident never happened.

Over the next few weeks, the AirDoctor helped clear the air after I burnt some toast in the toaster and cooked cruciferous vegetables. It also helps combat day-to-day odors, like a full trash can or a garbage disposal ready for a scrubdown.

What I like best is that the AirDoctor isn’t just masking these scents; it’s actually removing them from the air. As an allergy sufferer, I’ve also noticed fewer symptoms (especially sneezing) when the air purifier is running. Thanks to the 1500’s portable size, it’s easy to tote it around my downstairs living area, too.

The AirDoctor’s filters are designed to last—but the exact lifespan depends on many factors. How long you run the air purifier each day, for starters. Along with what kind of baseline air quality is in your home. Pets, for example, are an obvious culprit for added dander and hair. Luckily, the 1500 includes a built-in sensor light that tells you when it’s time for a refresh.

You can also pop off the housing and give the filter a good vacuum. I like to do this once a month or so, but again, buildup may be more or less severe depending on your home’s air quality conditions.

I’ve had the opportunity to also test the AirDoctor 1500’s larger cousin, the AirDoctor 3500. But how do these models compare?

Size is the most obvious difference. The 1500 is six inches slimmer than the 3500 and weighs roughly half as much. I find that the 1500 blends more seamlessly into my space than the 3500, taking up less footprint. The round tower design is also very easy to slip into a corner.

That said, the 1500 covers rooms up to 320 sq. ft., while the 3500 covers up to 630 sq. ft. This makes the 1500 a great option for kitchens, offices, laundry rooms and small bedrooms, while the 3500 is a better choice for large living rooms and open-concept homes.

Beyond that, performance is very similar. Both models are quiet and easy to operate. They use a 3-stage filtration system, including an UltraHEPA. They’re both high-quality machines that are built to last. And that quality does come at a premium. The 1500 retails for $399, while the 3500 costs $659.

My spouse likes to joke that I’m like a basset hound given how sensitive I am to smells. That’s why the AirDoctor 1500 is an obvious choice in our kitchen. It efficiently rids our space of odors from cooking, pungent foods and even the occasional smoke-induced accident. Not only is this machine reducing odors that would otherwise linger for days, but it’s also cleansing our air of other contaminants.

This way to cleaner air! I love this air purifier's slim footprint, low noise level and triple filter design.

The AirDoctor 1500 air purifier is available from the brand’s website or Amazon. It retails for $399.

This Olive Oil Discovery Set is a Surprisingly Thoughtful Gift for Foodies

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

This highly versatile olive oil gift set includes five distinct flavors meant to spark home cooks' imaginations.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

If you’re shopping for a true foodie, nothing can top a gourmet gift basket. But if you go that route, you have to do your research. After all, not all food gifts are the same. The last thing you want to do is send your epicurean friend or family member yet another disappointing fruit basket or a block of shelf-stable, gummy cheese!

Instead, for something ready-to-eat, opt for beloved foods with an established fanbase—perhaps one of the editor-loved finds on Goldbelly. Or, if you want to really impress them, high-quality ingredients are sure to inspire their culinary creativity. That’s why my go-to food gift is an olive oil gift set from Enzo’s Table.

The Signature Selection gift set offers a diverse lineup of flavors to impress your favorite foodies.

Folks at Enzo’s Table have been farming their vineyard in California’s fertile San Joaquin Valley for nearly a century. This gives their gourmand products a farm-to-table feel that’s backed by decades of know-how. In fact, over the years, the brand has proudly received over 200 awards for its signature olive oils, jams and biscotti!

The Signature Selection olive oil gift set is a fleet consisting of four of Enzo’s famous olive oils, plus their signature balsamic vinegar. All five 100ml bottles are organic, having been produced in the San Joaquin Valley vineyard. The gift set contains the following:

The Delicate EVOO offers a buttery flavor and incredibly mild yet peppery finish, making it a perfectly smooth pick for dipping bread or incorporating into salad dressings. I love using it for roasting wintery vegetables and crafting homemade tomato sauce for spaghetti, since it doesn’t overpower acidic or umami flavors found in fresh tomatoes and mushrooms.

Enzo’s Bold EVOO is best for imparting lasting flavor. I love it for roasted meats in particular, like the rotisserie chicken my husband makes on his grill. It leaves quite the impression with its peppery, spicy profile, especially on milder meats like chicken breast.

The Clementine Crush EVOO is probably my favorite of the five. This one goes well in salad dressings, cakes—anything that benefits from a fruity, acidic punch. Because it’s milled with fresh organic California mandarins, it’s a good EVOO to add a little brightness to dishes. Personally, it’s also my favorite option for delicate fish and sautéing shrimp.

Enzo’s Basil Crush EVOO is, as you’d expect, herby and earthy. It’s another option that’s particularly good for roasts, especially red meats. I also love adding a splash on caprese salad.

The Traditional Balsamic Vinegar is incredibly versatile in all the ways you love to see with balsamic. Because it’s crafted from sweet, organic Lambrusco and Trebbiano grapes, it shines brightest on summery dishes like grilled fruits and balsamic Brussels sprouts. I’ve tried a lot of balsamic over the years, and this one might be the thickest, richest blend I’ve enjoyed.

Delight your favorite foodie with a full fleet of flavors when you gift them an Enzo Olive Oil gift set!

Folks seeking the perfect gift for foodies can’t go wrong with the highly versatile Signature Selection gift set. With so many unique flavors inside and generous portions of each offering, it’s a wonderfully simple way to spark inspiration in the kitchen!

If your recipient finds any fast favorites while they work through their discovery set, another great gift idea is one of their olive oil subscriptions. This is also a good way to save on Enzo olive oil if you’re a big fan yourself. Over the years, food subscriptions have become increasingly popular, and they remain some of our readers’ favorite holiday gifts by far!

Ditch Your Fleet of Coffeemakers—The Chefman Coffee Station Pro Handles Everything

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Can't decide between a drip coffee maker, Nespresso, Keurig or an espresso machine? The Chefman Coffee Station Pro does all four—and takes up very little room.

Our editors and experts handpick every product we feature. We may earn a commission from your purchases.Learn more.

As Kevin Malone famously said in Season 8, Episode 2 of The Office, “Why waste time say lot word when few word do trick?” In my opinion, the same concept should apply to countertop appliances. After ditching my microwave, countertop oven, air fryer and toaster for the all-encompassing Panasonic HomeChef, I began to wonder what else I could condense into a singular appliance.

After musing over my fleet of five(!!!) coffeemakers consisting of Keurig and Nespresso machines, an automatic espresso maker, a manual espresso maker and a classic drip coffeemaker, I realized that it was time to address the barista elephant in the room. I had to reclaim my coffee bar counter space—but how? The answer, it turns out, is the Chefman Coffee Station Pro.

Here’s how I tested it over several months and why it’s become my go-to coffee machine.

Can't decide on a brew method? The Chefman Coffee Station Pro tackles them all. Yes, really.

In the age of the all-in-one appliance, maximizing countertop space just makes sense. In small kitchens especially, cramming half a dozen different-yet-similar appliances into limited space feels like such a waste. And sure, you don’t need both a blender and a food processor, but it’s nice to have the choice between the two. That’s why combo machines like the Ninja CrushBoss came out and started changing the game. That’s also the idea behind the Chefman Coffee Station Pro.

Unlike other x-in-one machines that incorporate a couple of different brewing methods (looking at you, Keurig K-Duo), the Chefman Coffee Station Pro combines 10 different functionalities into one machine. Craving a quick single cup? You can use it with both K-cups and Nespresso pods. Need to fill up a full carafe for a party? It does that, too. But what if you want to leisurely enjoy an iced latte or a shot of espresso on the weekend? Well, that’s also an option with the built-in manual espresso machine and automatic milk frother.

In terms of functionality, the Chefman Coffee Station Pro offers espresso, cappuccino, latte, single-serve coffee, 12-cup carafe, cold brew, cold-pressed espresso, coffee over ice, hot milk and cold foam. To try it out, I packed up all my other coffee makers and made it my primary coffee machine for two months.

You’d probably expect a machine with so many capabilities to be enormous, heavy, complicated, unwieldily. Not so with the Coffee Station Pro. It’s so simple to set up and use, I didn’t even need to read through the provided booklet before I was whipping up drinks. It also takes up barely more space than a dedicated espresso machine.

Right off the bat, I brewed a pot of drip coffee. First, I’m super impressed with the way the machine can read what I’m brewing based on the basket insert I choose. Pop in the drip basket, and it’s prompting me to choose the number of cups. Swap it out with the K-Cup pod holder, and it’s now asking how many ounces I’d like. It’s truly like magic!

Then, there’s the customization. First, pick between a single cup or a whole carafe. Then, choose to enjoy your coffee either hot or over ice. You also get the option of brewing a cup or a whole pot of cold brew for summertime get-togethers. There’s even a delay brew function, which makes it easy to prep your poison of choice the night before.

As I mentioned, the machine changes its touchscreen prompts based on which brew basket you choose. When you pop in a K-cup, you’re given the option of having it hot or over ice. There’s also a choice of 4-, 6-, 8-, 10- and 12-ounce cups. Then, you can adjust the strength and temperature, with three different settings for each. Finally, there’s a small flip-down platform to rest your cup on so that it doesn’t splash everywhere as it brews.

Want to use a Nespresso capsule instead? A pod insert pops into the K-cup adapter. Again, the machine will intuit this automatically and adjust accordingly. Overall, this aspect of the machine is incredibly intuitive. Plus, you can save specific settings as one of three “favorites,” if you want to speed things up with one-touch operation.

As for espressos, this thing does work. It boasts a 20-bar pressure system that brews espressos topped in a beautiful, silky crema. Pick from a single or double shot, or opt for a cappuccino or latte, both with a choice of single or double. If you choose a milk-based drink, ensure the milk reservoir is filled and angle the metal wand into your cup—the machine does the rest.

If you’re familiar with a standard manual espresso machine, you already know how to operate the portafilter. If not, no worries—there are helpful videos from Chefman to guide you through the process. You can also opt for hot milk, or warm or cold foam on its own by choosing the “froth” setting if you want to add any of those to your cup of regular coffee.

On the top of the machine next to the brew basket is an area with accessory storage for the portafilter baskets, Nespresso adapter and combination scoop/tamper. On the side of the coffee station is a place to stow the portafilter. While there’s no built-in storage for the brew basket, K-cup basket and carafe when they’re not in use, I do love that the smaller accessories have a dedicated space so they won’t be misplaced.

In terms of cleaning, this might be one of the easier machines I’ve ever used. All of the inserts simply rinse clean. But the part I really love is the milk frother. Instead of nooks and crannies that collect smells and residue, the entire thing comes apart into several pieces, so you can thoroughly rinse and wash every single part that touches milk. Win!

Chefman isn’t the only brand to claim that they’ve created an “all-in-one” solution for flexible baristas. In our research, we also found three other similar machines: The De’Longhi All-in-One, the Ninja Prestige Dual Brew Coffee Maker and, of course, the classic  Keurig K-Duo.

The only issue? We couldn’t find a single other appliance that offered K-Cup compatibility and Nespresso pod capability and a carafe and a manual espresso machine and automated milk frothing/latte settings. All of the options above offer at least two functions. Maybe four. But not all five. That crown belongs to the Chefman Coffee Station Pro alone.

Hopefully, we see more options pop up in the near future. But for now, the Chefman Coffee Station Pro is in its own category of coffee maker.

In my home, I used to have five different coffeemakers, including a Keurig, a Nespresso, an automatic espresso machine, a manual espresso maker and a classic drip coffeemaker. In fact, there were so many appliances that they began spilling into my dining room after I ran out of space on my dedicated coffee bar. After converting to the Chefman Coffee Station Pro, I’ve pared that number down to just one. One!

The only changes I’d make after using this machine since August 2026 are minor gripes. I do wish that there was a built-in coffee grinder, since having a separate bean grinder means having yet another appliance. And while I appreciate that there is a small storage cavity for tinier accessories, I don’t love having to find a place in my cupboard for the carafe and basket inserts when I’m not using them.

But honestly? Even with these small annoyances, I wouldn’t even knock off a full star on my final rating. The size, ease of use and cleanliness of the machine make it more than worth it—especially for flexible home baristas like me.

Condense five coffeemakers into just one with this ingenious appliance.

Want to clear up your counter space and join me in the future? You can snag this new appliance on the Chefman website or on Amazon. At $400, the Coffee Station Pro is absolutely an investment. And for some folks who won’t use all the different settings, it may not be worth the cost. But for coffee lovers who want to brew based on vibes (or who already have a long lineup of machines like I used to), the price point feels totally fair. After all, you’re getting five different machines in one!

The easier question is what can’t it make? It’s capable of espresso, single-serve coffee, a 12-cup carafe, cold brew and coffee over ice. While it’s not programmable to make specific drinks such as a cafe crema or Americano like some automatic espresso machines, the manual espresso component and automatic milk frother still offers extreme flexibility for budding baristas.

As Taste of Home’s shopping editor, Emily has covered everything from Pioneer Woman kitchen launches to finding the best pizza ovens. She also has four years of experience in shopping editor roles at Reader’s Digest, Taste of Home and Family Handyman. Her specialties lie in food and drink categories, as well as appliance testing and deal sourcing. Before writing this piece, we conducted three hours of research and tested the appliance for two months.

China’s Largest Startup Deal Ever Lifts Global Venture Funding To $59B In October

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Global venture funding in October totaled $59 billion, a 45% jump from a year earlier, per Crunchbase data, in large part due to a record-setting deal for China’s DeepSeek. The company’s $15.9 billion funding round marked the largest-ever venture deal for a Chinese company ever, and came in the same month that two other frontier labs raised blockbuster Series A rounds totaling at or close to $1 billion.

The bulk of October’s venture funding was invested in the two leading countries for startup investment, with companies based in the U.S. and China raising $28 billion and $20.6 billion last month, respectively. The last time more than $20 billion was invested in China in a single month was June 2018, when Ant Group raised $14 billion in its Series C funding.

Funding concentrated in seven companies that raised billion-dollar rounds, accounting for just over 40% of October’s venture funding. These large amounts were invested in frontier labs, manufacturing, energy, semiconductors, prediction markets and spend management software. Private equity led much of this funding, along with a few multistage venture firms including Sequoia Capital, Index Ventures and Lux Capital.

DeepSeek’s $15.9 billion raise at a valuation of $90 billion marked the largest private funding by a Chinese tech company on record. The deal places DeepSeek among the Top 10 most highly funded private companies. It also makes it the second most highly valued Chinese company after ByteDance, and the third most highly valued private frontier lab after U.S. companies Anthropic and OpenAI.

DeepSeek wasn’t the only Chinese frontier lab to raise big in October. Moonshot AI, a rival open-source lab, reportedly reached a $50 billion value in October, making it the 11th most valuable private company on The Crunchbase Unicorn Board. The startup has also reportedly confidentially filed to go public on the Hong Kong stock exchange.

Massachusetts-based manufacturing startup Re:Build raised the second-largest funding deal last month, a $2.1 billion round. The company partners with industrial companies and innovators from the product design phase to large-scale production and says it has 2.4 million square feet of manufacturing capacity.

Other large rounds last month went to Axiom, which raised $2 billion for power and grid capacity build out for data centers; spend-management platform Ramp, which raised $1.85 billion and said its ARR has reached $2 billion; open-source semiconductor company Tenstorrent, which says it  raised a $1.5 billion Series E funding and recently moved its  headquarters to Silicon Valley from Canada; prediction market Kalshi, which raised a $1 billion Series E; and frontier lab Flapping Airplanes, which raised a $1 billion Series A.

A further eight companies raised rounds north of $500 million last month. They spanned industries ranging from frontier labs, robotics, legal and chips, to agentic AI and healthcare. Among the most notable was TypeSafe AI, which says it raised an $870 million Series A funding on the heels of rapid developer adoption of its decision model Jev.

A total of 66% of global funding was in AI-driven companies in October, with semiconductor companies gaining market share this past month.

The U.S. IPO market was slow in anticipation of Anthropic’s November IPO. The offering could surpass even SpaceX’s record-setting June listing that raised $85.7 billion.

On the M&A front, at least nine venture-backed companies were acquired for more than $1 billion last month, including Uber’s acquisition of 19-year-old ezCater for $2.3 billion.

With two months remaining, 2026 has already set records for acquisitions, IPOs and funding rounds across major venture markets. October extended that momentum, but also highlighted how concentrated this cycle has become: Seven companies accounted for more than 40% of global funding, while AI-driven businesses captured two-thirds.

The question heading into year-end is how widely that momentum will spread, as Anthropic prepares to list in what could be the largest IPO of all time.

The data contained in this report comes directly from Crunchbase, and is based on reported data. Data is as of Nov. 2, 2026.

Cohere is targeting a $5 billion funding that was announced in October, without reporting an initial or final close. This funding was not included in the October monthly totals.

Note that data lags are most pronounced at the earliest stages of venture activity, with seed funding amounts increasing significantly after the end of a quarter/year.

Please note that all funding values are given in U.S. dollars unless otherwise noted. Crunchbase converts foreign currencies to U.S. dollars at the prevailing spot rate from the date funding rounds, acquisitions, IPOs and other financial events are reported. Even if those events were added to Crunchbase long after the event was announced, foreign currency transactions are converted at the historic spot price.

Seed and angel consists of seed, pre-seed and angel rounds. Crunchbase also includes venture rounds of unknown series, equity crowdfunding and convertible notes at $3 million (USD or as-converted USD equivalent) or less.

Early-stage consists of Series A and Series B rounds, as well as other round types. Crunchbase includes venture rounds of unknown series, corporate venture and other rounds above $3 million, and those less than or equal to $15 million.

Late-stage consists of Series C, Series D, Series E and later-lettered venture rounds following the “Series [Letter]” naming convention. Also included are venture rounds of unknown series, corporate venture and other rounds above $15 million. Corporate rounds are only included if a company has raised an equity funding at seed through a venture series funding round.

Technology growth is a private-equity round raised by a company that has previously raised a “venture” round. (So basically, any round from the previously defined stages.)

Spread the Love: Now’s the Time to Edit Your Garden and Share with Friends

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

All Gardenista stories—from garden tours and expert advice to hand tools and furniture roundups.

Starting from scratch or upgrading an outdoor space? Look here for advice on plants and hardscape materials. We have 200 guides on everything from fences to foxgloves.

Heading somewhere? Plan your trip with our destination guides to our favorite public gardens, hotels, restaurants, and shops.

Gardenista’s members-only directory of landscape architects and garden designers.

See our obsessively curated catalog of favorite products sourced by the Gardenista editors.

This is part of a series with Perfect Earth Project, a nonprofit dedicated to ecological gardening, on how you can be more sustainable in your landscapes at home.

Fall used to be a time of endless raking and cutting back perennials, but now that we’re leaving the leaves and stems the way nature intended, we have more time to do things we enjoy in the garden, like sharing the native plants we love with friends. What can be better than giving plants you know have been grown organically, coming from a gardener they know and trust—the very best provenance.

Sharing native plants also helps fill in the gap in supply now that more people want to grow them. These value—and valuable—plants are beautiful and beneficial to the ecosystem, supporting pollinators, who nibble their leaves and drink their nectar, and birds and animals, who feast on their seeds. Sharing plants is also a fun way to edit your garden, thinning out or moving, shall we say, particularly exuberant varieties—we’re looking at you, Rudbeckia, Coreopsis, goldenrod, and Aster, to new homes.

Photography courtesy of Perfect Earth Project.

“Fall is ideal because you don’t have to worry about stressors like heat and drought,” says landscape designer and Perfect Earth Project founder Edwina von Gal. Plus, in fall plants are easier to identify since their blooms or foliage are still visible. Be sure to plan your digging when your friends are available to plant them quickly, she advises. “It’s important not to let the roots dry out, so never leave recently dug-up plants out in the sun,” she says. Here’s where you can put those plastic nursery pots you’ve been saving to good use. Once you dig up the plants, pot them up to share. When replanting, remind your friends to tamp down the soil to make sure there are no air pockets around the roots and to water them well.

One note to consider: Keep a watch on warm-season plants, those that thrive during summer’s heat. Since their roots aren’t actively growing in the fall and winter months, plants that haven’t rooted in well, especially those with small root systems, can “heave” or rise out of the ground during freeze and thaw cycles. Don’t let it deter you. If this happens to your newly planted plants, don’t worry. Simply push them back into the ground. As Rosemary Verey once told von Gal when she visited Barnsley House, “The time to take a cutting is when it is offered.”

Spread the love and the joy this season and give some stellar native species to your friends and neighbors before the ground freezes. Here are von Gal’s suggestions for three different types of plants.

Plants that spread horizontally by rhizomes or stolons are especially easy to share since their roots don’t tend to grow all that deep. Using a garden fork gently tease out or loosen the soil around the plant you’re going to divide. For plants with massive root systems, a sharp spade might be needed to separate them from their community. Pull up a clump, making sure each division has both roots and shoots intact.

We know we recommend leaving stems standing over the winter for wildlife. But for some transplants “it’s important that the roots stay stable when you place them in the ground,” says von Gal. If the plant is big and floppy, like tall Coreopsis, it’s okay to cut it back, to a foot or so, depending on how stable it feels to prevent it from moving too much and potentially uprooting after replanting, she says. If you are very energetic, staking is also an option.

Use a fork or spade to break apart ferns with creeping rhizomes, like hay-scented, sensitive, and ostrich ferns. Divide the plants, making sure there are lots of roots and place them in moist shady locations. They are best planted quite shallow with lots of leaf mold or compost.

Some shrubs, like elderberry, produce offshoots or suckers. Dig them out with a sharp spade, being careful to keep the roots intact, and then plant them in a new spot. Stake any that might blow over in the wind to keep them upright.

Need the right tools for fall gardening? Browse our Shop Our Picks page for editor-approved finds.
