Oregon wildfire victims defend jury instruction in billion-dollar PacifiCorp suit

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The fires over Labor Day weekend in 2020 displaced thousands of Oregon residents.

PORTLAND, Ore. (CN) â Victims of the destructive series of wildfires that ravaged Oregon over Labor Day weekend in 2020 implored the state Supreme Court on Tuesday to revive a jury verdict in their favor.

In one of the state’s largest disasters, fires blazed across Oregon during a historic windstorm in September 2020. PacifiCorp and Pacific Power had left their power lines energized, and those energy lines ignited the flames.

Thousands of survivors of four fires â the 242 Fire, the Echo Mountain Complex Fire, the South Obenchain Fire, and the Santiam Canyon Fire â sued the power companies, accusing them of failing to reasonably maintain the power lines, clear vegetation surrounding the lines and protect against fire. They also claimed the companies negligently failed to shut down power to the lines despite warnings.

The trial court certified a class of fire survivors harmed by the four fires and structured the trial so the jury would determine liability and causation for each fire. After an eight-week trial, the jury found PacifiCorp caused the fires and harmed class members. Nineteen damages-only trials then produced verdicts and judgments exceeding $1 billion.

However, PacifiCorp appealed, challenging an instruction that told the jury the class representatives brought evidence on behalf of absent class members and jurors could âassume that the evidence at trial applies to all class members.â

The Oregon Court of Appeals sided with PacifiCorp, holding that common evidence didnât prove causation and the jury may have assumed evidence of liability for one fire meant liability for the others.

Before the Supreme Court Tuesday, the fire survivors â a class of over 2,400 individuals â argued the appeals court made a mistake.

âAt the end of the day, what they really don’t like is the jury verdict in this case,â said Nicholas Rosinia, attorney representing the class of wildfire survivors said, referring to the power companies. âThey bet the company on a class action wildfire trial, and they lost.â

âHere, the jury decided the case on a fire area by fire area basis," Rosinia continued. “That’s not to say the evidence was not common across the class as a whole, but it permitted, in this case, PacifiCorp to make fire-specific defenses to the extent it had fire-specific defenses.”

The fire survivors argue the jury simply needed to determine that PacifiCorpâs fires were a substantial factor in the resulting harm.

âThe PacifiCorp’s conflagration of fires was the common evidence that proved the ultimate harm, and that’s why all of the class members relied on that same body of evidence with respect to the conflagration to prove their case,â Rosinia said.

The wildfire survivors said there was no dispute that there was harm across the class area.

âThe only dispute was whether PacifiCorp’s conflagration of fires was a substantial factor, and the evidence that everybody relied on was common as to that question,â Rosinia said.

PacifiCorp disagreed, arguing the class action was unprecedented, that the class should not have been certified, and that the instruction invited the jury to assume the evidence presented at trial applied to every single class member.

âFor the jury, class actions are hard enough, but they’re told this is one big class. This wasn’t subclasses by fire areas â one big class, 17 class representatives, they widely differ among them, and the jury is told, âYou can consider all of the evidence as applying to all the class members,â" said Theodore Boutrous, attorney for PacifiCorp. âI’ve never seen anything like it.â

Oregon Supreme Court Justice Christopher Garrett questioned PacifiCorpâs theory.

âIt appears just from reading the briefs and the verdict form that the case was pretty clearly tried on a fire-by-fire basis and the verdict form reflects that,â Garrett remarked. âSo how real is the worry that the jury would have transposed causation from fire number one to fire number three?â

PacifiCorp argued the worry is quite real, given the sheer size of the class and varying circumstances.

âDo we really think the jury would think that a fire burning a hundred miles away proved a different fire?â questioned Oregon Supreme Court Justice Meagan Flynn.

PacifiCorp contended that yes, the jury very well could have thought so. It also argued the trial court should not have certified such a large class.

âThere hasn’t been a class action certified anywhere in the country with one wildfire. Here, we’ve got four fire areas, and there were dozens of fires within those fires,â Boutrous said. âIt was just a fundamental error to certify one big class with 17 representatives who themselves couldn’t have been in a class just alone because they were in completely different places.â

The Oregon Supreme Court did not indicate when it would rule.

Brazil expands federal powers over public security

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

The constitutional amendment passed without opposition.

RIO DE JANEIRO (CN) â Brazil approved a constitutional amendment Tuesday that expands the federal governmentâs role in public security, directs oil revenue to policing and prisons and calls for tougher penalties against criminal organizations.

The Senate approved President Luiz InÃ¡cio Lula da Silvaâs proposal in two rounds of voting, with no opposing votes, after separating out amendments that would lower the age of criminal responsibility. The lower house approved the amendment March 4, clearing the way for Congress to formally enact it without presidential approval.

The vote marks a victory for Lula after a presidential campaign in which public security figured prominently. The amendment also clears the way for a Public Security Ministry, whose creation Lula had made contingent on its approval.

The government secured approval before the new Congress takes office in 2027, when the opposition will have greater strength in the Senate.

Lula welcomed the vote in a social media post, saying the new ministry would focus on intelligence, planning and cooperation with states and municipalities to confront criminal factions and militias.

âProtection for citizens. Prison for criminals,â Lula wrote. âThe Brazilian people have the right to work, return home and live in peace. Guaranteeing that right is a commitment of Brazilâs government.â

JoÃ£o Gabriel Madeira Pontes, an attorney who holds a doctorate in public law from Rio de Janeiro State University, welcomed greater federal involvement and cooperation among different levels of government.

âUnder the original framework of the 1988 Constitution, public security was almost exclusively the responsibility of the states,â Pontes said.

The amendment adds the Unified Public Security System, which already exists under federal law, to the Constitution. It provides for joint operations, information sharing and integration of federal, state and municipal agenciesâ systems.

It restricts the governmentâs ability to freeze money in federal public security and prison funds or use it for other purposes, while directing 10% of the surplus in the oil-funded Social Fund to those programs. The transfers will begin in 2027 and reach the full percentage in 2029.

The text requires legislation establishing tougher penalties for members of criminal organizations based on their rank within those groups. These include incarceration in maximum-security or other specialized facilities, limits or bans on moving to less restrictive prison regimes and confiscation of assets linked to criminal activities.

The amendment also expands the Federal Policeâs authority to investigate militias and criminal organizations operating across state or international borders. The Federal Highway Police will be able to patrol federal railways and waterways in addition to highways.

Municipalities will be able to establish civilian police forces for patrols and community policing, provided they meet requirements for training, funding and coordination with other forces. Public prosecutors will exercise external oversight.

Pontes said the main challenge will be integrating new federal and municipal responsibilities with existing state police structures. The amendment maintains the division between military police, responsible for patrols, and civil police, responsible for criminal investigations.

âHow will this fit together with an old institutional structure that has been criticized for many years â the statesâ public security structure?â Pontes said. âI think there will be considerable difficulty integrating these changes with the institutional framework that already existed at the state level.â

Pontes also questioned the effectiveness of relying on incarceration, which he described as âsomewhat the logic behind the amendment.â

âMass incarceration has always been a phenomenon in Brazil, and it grows every year. And we havenât achieved much through incarceration. I really donât know how this could improve public security, specifically the fight against organized crime,â Pontes said.

A dispute over the age of criminal responsibility, currently 18, had threatened to delay approval. The opposition proposed lowering it to 16 generally and 14 for certain crimes, while a Workersâ Party proposal would lower it to 16 only for crimes classified as heinous under Brazilian law. Senators voted 38-36 to consider those proposals separately, avoiding another vote on the amendment in the lower house.

Senator FlÃ¡vio Bolsonaro, whom Lula defeated in the presidential election, participated remotely from the United States and voted against considering the proposals separately. He left Brazil on Oct. 27, after a Supreme Court justice ordered him to surrender his passports as part of an investigation into suspected money laundering.

Courthouse News reporter MarÃ­lia Marasciulo is based in Brazil.

Environmentalists successfully block California’s first carbon capture project

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

TerraVault was one oil and gas company's plan to inject and store up to 2 million tons of carbon dioxide underground every year.

(CN) âÂ A Superior Court judge ruled Tuesday that Kern County’s approval of California’s first carbon capture and storage project violated the state’s bedrock environmental law, the California Environmental Quality Act.

The ruling is a win for local environmental justice and conservation groups, including the Sierra Club and the Center for Biological Diversity, who argued the county had left “multiple environmental, public health and safety issues” unaddressed when it approved the landmark project.

âFor years, Kern County residents have raised serious concerns about increased pollution and the safety of millions of tons of CO2 stored beneath their community," Ileana Navarro of the Central California Environmental Justice Network said in a written statement. “We are so grateful that the court recognized what Kern County failed to see and gave community concerns the serious consideration they deserve.”

Located at the Elk Hills Oil Field in the San Joaquin Valley and built by the California Resources Corporation, the state’s largest oil and gas company, TerraVault began capturing carbon dioxide from oil wells and injecting it underground in May. Once completed, the two vaults, made from depleted oil reservoirs, are expected to capture more than 2 million tons of carbon dioxide every year âÂ the equivalent of what more than 400,000 cars would emit annually.

In an email, President and CEO of the California Resources Corporation Francisco Leon expressed disappointment in the ruling, writing: “This project is the result of years of work, including close consultation with surrounding communities where we received overwhelming support for our efforts. This order is a step in the wrong direction for addressing the impacts of climate change and reflects the weaponization of CEQA by a narrow group of special interests. We are still evaluating the order and will aggressively pursue all available options to protect and advance this important project.â

Some environmental groups have said TerraVault I will, in effect, extend the life of the Elk Hills Oil Field, the seventh most productive in the U.S. The California Resources Corporation has said it could also store carbon from a range of not-yet-built factories in the region, which could produce, among other things, cement and steel. These new industries, opponents say, would further pollute a low-income area already struggling with bad air quality.

In their CEQA lawsuit, the plaintiffs argued the environmental impact report, prepared by the California Resources Corporation and approved by Kern County, didn’t properly account for the eight possible industries TerraVault I could capture carbon for, even though the new factories were a “reasonably foreseeable consequence” of the project. Superior Court Judge Marcos Camacho agreed.

“The record evidence shows that all CO2 capture equipment, transportation pipelines, compression facilities, injection and monitoring wells, and underground pore space exist solely to receive and store CO2,” Camacho wrote in the ruling. “Thus, the source facilities and industries unquestionably change the scope and nature of the project… The Court finds that such omission indicates that the whole of the project was not considered, in violation of CEQA.”

The judge found plenty of other shortcomings in the environmental report, referred to in the ruling as the REIR.Â “The issue of atmospheric leakage of CO2,” for example, was not “fully addressed.” He also found the report “fails to adequately mitigate the project’s greenhouse gas impacts.”

“This is a lack of analysis in the REIR, not a disagreement among experts,” Camacho wrote.

The ruling means TerraVault must temporarily shut down. The California Resources Corporation will have to prepare a new environmental impact report, a long and costly process, before it can restart operations.

The fight over TerraVault exposes a deep and long-standing divide within the environmental movement. Some groups believe climate change is the biggest threat to life on earth, and to avoid the most dire consequences, we must employ any and all tactics to remove carbon from the atmosphere. One report by the Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change reads, âThe deployment of [carbon dioxide removal] to counterbalance hard-to-abate residual emissions is unavoidable if net zero CO2 or GHG emissions are to be achieved."

Other groups, including the plaintiffs suing to stop TerraVault, say the way to stop climate change is to cut emissions.

“California needs to invest in real solutions like clean wind and solar to solve our energy and climate emergencies,” said Michelle Ghafar, an attorney with Earthjustice that represented the plaintiffs, in a written statement. “We canât waste time on âHail Maryâ projects pushed by the fossil fuel industry that will only delay our progress and further entrench pollution in frontline communities.â

Modern CSS Might Make Your Front-End More Complex

📅 2026-11-03  |  Read Source →

Gabriel is a front-end developer and technical writer. He specializes in HTML, CSS, JavaScript, React, Vue, TailwindCSS, and BootStrap, with a track record of … More about Gabriel ↬

Weekly tips on front-end & UX.Trusted by 182,000+ folks.

Have you ever replaced fifty lines of JavaScript with a few lines of CSS, merged the PR, and felt like you just did the codebase a favor? You replace ResizeObserver with a container query, the diff is mostly red, and everyone approves it before lunch.

This was what it looked like before you made the change:

See the Pen [demo (before PR) [forked]](https://codepen.io/smashingmag/pen/XJMxVJq) by Shoyombo Gabriel Ayomide.

Then, a week later, a card on the dashboard has vanished. A heading has grown on tablets for no reason anyone can explain. The spacing inside a comment is off.

See the Pen [demo (after PR) [forked]](https://codepen.io/smashingmag/pen/gbmBopp) by Shoyombo Gabriel Ayomide.

None of these bugs are in the few lines you wrote. All of them arrived with those few lines of CSS.

In my last article on how CSS is growing smarter, I left a few questions hanging at the end, including: What happens when logic is split between CSS and JavaScript, and how do we debug conditional styles without a clear view of what triggered them? This article is my attempt to answer these two questions.

I think it’s worth saying clearly that I am not saying modern CSS is bad. What I am saying is that:

A CSS feature can simplify a piece of code while making the wider application harder to reason about if you don’t understand how it relates to existing code, be it CSS or JavaScript.

Think of a new CSS feature as a new tenant moving into a shared apartment. The tenant may be quiet and tidy, but they still share a kitchen with everyone who already lives there. In that case, CSS is now roommates with JavaScript, the component’s layout, the CSS Cascade, and the browser’s rendering engine. The apartment’s stability depends less on the tenant and more on how they get along with one another.

That gives us two different things to measure, and a diff only shows the first one:

I am not the only one thinking about this. In April 2026, Igor Roztropiński published an essay titled “Modern Frontend Complexity: essential or accidental?” asking:

The essay landed on r/programming, where the thread has drawn 80-plus comments and the replies pull in two directions. One of the most upvoted comments, from a user named Kwantuum, pushes back on the whole genre:

Another commenter, kayinfire, takes the other side, arguing that “there are tons of scenarios where a front-end framework introduces more accidental complexity than it’s worth.” They say this a few replies later:

I think both commenters are on to something, and this article lives in the gap between the two. Yes, CSS has improved in the last several years. And yes, “less” CSS on paper is not automatically “less” complexity in practice. That said, my claim is narrower. A native feature only reduces complexity when it actually replaces what was there before and doesn’t disrupt the calm state of everything else it interacts with.

Let’s meet the housemates in our metaphorical apartment, one scenario at a time.

The first housemate is JavaScript, and the trouble starts when the old tenant never quite moves out. Here is a card that switches to a two-column layout when it has enough room. The original version uses a ResizeObserver to toggle a class:

With CSS Container Queries, we need no script at all and can simplify our selectors at the same time:

Lovely. Now, here’s the thing: migrations rarely happen in one clean sweep. The pull request adds the @container rule and removes the .card--wide .card__body rule. The observer survives because it is registered in another file. The .card--wide .card__title rule survives because nobody searched for the class. Nothing looks broken, because both implementations agree on 480px. Then a designer asks to move the breakpoint to 600px, and a developer updates the only place they know about: the @container rule.

See the Pen [Scenario 1: The Behaviour With Two Owners [forked]](https://codepen.io/smashingmag/pen/ZYeqvpx) by Shoyombo Gabriel Ayomide.

Between 480px and 600px, the card now has a single-column body and a wide-layout title. Nobody wrote that state. To find it, you need to know that the layout has two owners and read both of them.

CSS and JavaScript can happily share a component. What they cannot share is the component’s behavior. So, instead of having a silent fight over a component’s behavior, check first. Know all the contending parties, then split the work.

And when you’re done migrating, you have to finish the job: delete the observer, the class, and every selector that mentions the class. Otherwise, you may wind up with a tenant who simply refuses to move out.

Back in 2023, I wrote about how block formatting contexts solve float issues. Back then, creating a block formatting context was something we did on purpose. Today, you can create one without meaning to. When we declare container-type: inline-size, we think we are saying, “let my children query my width.” The browser hears quite a bit more. This is the second housemate, the layout around the component, and our new tenant who just asked for one shelf has ended up rearranging the living room.

According to MDN’s container-type reference, the value “applies style and inline-size containment to the element.” And MDN’s guide to block formatting contexts lists “query containers (elements where container-type isn’t normal)” among the CSS styles that create one. An element that establishes a new block formatting context will, in MDN’s words:

That means one declaration can change three layout behaviors that have nothing to do with queries, plus a fourth that comes from inline-size containment. I built a demo where each “before” and “after” pair differs by only that one declaration.

See the Pen [Scenario 2: The Card That Vanished [forked]](https://codepen.io/smashingmag/pen/dPvgJpB) by Shoyombo Gabriel Ayomide.

A paragraph with margin-bottom: 16px sits above the card, and the card’s heading has margin-top: 24px. Before the change, the heading’s margin collapsed through the top of the card and merged with the paragraph’s margin, leaving a 24px gap. Afterward, the margin remained trapped inside the card. The two margins now add up to 40px, and the card grows from 30px to 54px tall.

The card follows a floated image. Before the change, the card’s box ran behind the float and its text wrapped around the image. Afterward, the whole card sits beside the float; it starts 120px further in (the width of the image) and is 120px narrower.

This is the one that makes cards vanish. Inline-size containment stops an element from taking its width from its contents. MDN has a nice, succinct explanation:

In the last demo, three tag-sized cards sit in a flex row and measure roughly 74px, 96px, and 114px, respectively. If we add container-type: inline-size, then all three measure 0px. I got the same 0px result when the card was displayed inline-block, floated, set to a width: fit-content box, was an align-items: flex-start child of a column flex container, and when it was an item in an auto grid track. A plain block-level card retains its full width because its width is determined by its parent rather than its contents.

In isolation, the card is a block that fills its parent. In reality, it is a flex item, follows a float, or sits under a paragraph whose margin it used to absorb.

Before you add container-type to an element, ask yourself the following three questions:

Answering these will help prevent a housemate from completely changing the furniture layout right from under you.

The third housemate is the CSS Cascade. Every shared apartment has house rules about who gets the last word, and a new tenant can arrive with more say than anyone expected.

The :has() selector and native nesting let us write short selectors in CSS. But short selectors can match more and weigh more than they appear.

Say we want to remove paragraph margins from card components that have a hero image. This might look like it does the job:

See the Pen [Scenario 3: The Selector That Reached Too Far [forked]](https://codepen.io/smashingmag/pen/pvexpRd) by Shoyombo Gabriel Ayomide.

This card has no hero image. Yet every paragraph in it loses its margin.

If you inspect the comment’s paragraph in DevTools and look at the “Styles” pane, you will find two culprits:

It could get even more complex and difficult to catch because the state changes at runtime. Let’s say a user posts a comment that includes an avatar. The body text of the card above it loses its spacing. Good luck reproducing that from a bug report that says “spacing looks off sometimes.”

The fix is to constrain the selector with child combinators:

This helps the selector get more specific about what it is yelling about.

Native nesting has the same habit. MDN’s guide to nesting and specificity says the & selector takes “the largest specificity in the associated selector list,” exactly like :is(). Look at this adaptation of MDN’s example:

A .button link inside a .card is blue, even though the #featured ID is nowhere near it. The usual reaction is a more specific .button rule, then an exception to that rule. Before you know it, you are in a war of specificity you never knew you had declared.

The way out is to keep the selector list and drop its weight by wrapping it in :where():

Plain links inside a card are still blue, and the .button link is red again. Again, another way to quiet the loud housemate.

Don’t be fooled into thinking specificity is the whole story. MDN’s introduction to the cascade puts origin, importance, and cascade layers ahead of specificity, and notes that “normal layered styles have less precedence than normal unlayered styles.” In my test, a layered rule with a specificity of 0-4-0 lost to an unlayered .title (0-1-0). So if your new component styles go into @layer components while the legacy stylesheet stays unlayered, the legacy rule wins every conflict, however carefully you wrote the new one. The exception is when the !important keyword is used, because it actually reverses the cascade order.

Miriam Suzanne’s original proposal for cascade layers describes exactly the pain layers were meant to remove: having to “be very careful with matching specificity to layer.” Layers do remove it, but only when the rules on both sides of the conflict are layered. A half-layered codebase just gives you one more ordering to keep in your head.

None of these battles started with :has() or nesting. In an r/webdev thread titled “CSS-in-JS: What’s the biggest performance drop you actually felt?”, one of the most upvoted answers isn’t about performance at all. It is about the cascade:

This is about the “order of appearance,” which is the last step in the cascade that ends up deciding a conflict nobody knew existed. Another commenter in the subreddit adds that the injected styles can land in a different order in development and production builds: “Who doesn’t love debugging something that works locally but not in prod.”

Not everyone in the thread agrees on who is to blame. TheThirdRace replies, “It’s a coding issue, not a technology issue.”

I think both are right, and that is exactly the point. CSS-in-JS is a different technology from the native features we are looking at, so take this as a neighboring example, not as proof. But the shape of the problem is the same: the tool did what it promised, and the trouble arose from how it complied with the existing rules.

“We moved it to CSS, so it’s faster now.” You have probably heard this in a code review, and maybe said it yourself. In my last article, I warned that it isn’t always the case. Let’s test it.

Moving a behavior from JavaScript to CSS changes who does the work. It doesn’t tell you how much work there is. The last housemate, the browser’s rendering engine, is the one who gets the bill. Everyone in the apartment shares it, and nobody looks at it after the move.

Take :has() as an example. Browser vendors were worried about its cost from the start. In a CSS Working Group issue about :has() restrictions, Emilio Cobos Álvarez noted that implementations wanted to ship it with limitations “in order to prevent performance footguns and complexity.” Today, MDN’s :has() page has a whole section on performance with two pieces of advice:

I wanted numbers, so I built a list with 5,000 rows. The test toggles an .is-selected class on the same row 300 times, so the row’s selected state changes on every toggle, and forces a style and layout update each time. I ran it in Chrome 153 on a Mac in October 2026, and recorded a trace in DevTools to count how many elements each toggle restyles.

See the Pen [Scenario 4: The Rewrite Nobody Measured [forked]](https://codepen.io/smashingmag/pen/XJMxVgP) by Shoyombo Gabriel Ayomide.

First, the “bad” selector was fine. Rule 2 is exactly the shape MDN warns against: a :has() anchored to body. It cost about a millisecond and a half more than the script, and it restyled a single element, the toolbar. A frame at 60Hz lasts 16.7ms, so I would take that trade for deleting an event handler any day.

Second, two harmless rules were not harmless together. Rule 3 styles the rows, and its :has() condition remains unchanged throughout the test. On its own, it costs nothing measurable. Put it in the same stylesheet as the second rule instead, and every toggle restyles all 5,000 rows plus the toolbar. That is about what it costs a script to restyle every row on purpose.

Let’s keep this in proportion. Since 7ms still fits inside a frame, nobody would notice it on this page. However, the browser is now restyling 5,001 elements to change just one, and neither rule says so. Also, the cost grows with the list. When I set the demo to 20,000 rows, the two rules together restyled 20,001 elements and took about 31ms per toggle, which is nearly two frames.

I can tell you what Chrome does here, but not why. Changing .is-selected on one row restyles every element that the other :has() rule targets, even though that rule never mentions .is-selected and its result never changes. I have filed this with the Chromium team as an issue, with a reduced test case, and I only tested Chrome. Whatever the cause, neither rule looks suspicious in a code review, and the cost applies only when both are met.

That is system complexity in a single table. And please take the pattern from it, not the numbers: this is one page, one browser, and one machine.

The same goes for animation. The animation guide on web.dev recommends restricting animations to opacity and transform where possible, and warns that “an animation that moves an element using something other than transform is likely to be slow.” MDN’s CSS performance guide lists the properties that trigger layout when animated, such as width, height, margin, top, and left. A CSS transition on left runs layout on every frame, while a JavaScript animation of transform skips it. The property determines the cost, regardless of which language you write it in.

To be fair, CSS often wins. A script that reads layout, writes styles, and reads layout again forces the browser to do layout several times in one frame, and a container query hands that whole job to the browser. But that win is something you can measure, so measure it:

Each of these scenarios is small on its own. The trouble is that a real migration could hit all four scenarios at once. You have already seen three of them. They are the three bugs in the “after” demo at the top of this article. Add a long list, and you get the fourth. One move-in, four housemates disturbed.

A team replaces a card’s JavaScript-driven responsive layout with a container query and :has(). The pull request adds twelve lines of CSS, deletes one script, and misses another. Within a month, three bug reports land, and there’s a chance a performance trace turns up as the fourth problem:

None of these reports means that container queries or :has() were the wrong choice. Each bug lives in the meeting point between the new CSS and the system it moved into. Still, one small pull request has sent a developer through the JavaScript, the page layout, the cascade, and the profiler.

And the way out is not a return to JavaScript. Here is the same page once more, fixed.

See the Pen [demo (fixed PR) [forked]](https://codepen.io/smashingmag/pen/wBJYpPJ) by Shoyombo Gabriel Ayomide.

It still uses a container query and :has(), with three changes:

This gap between a clean start and a real codebase shows up in the same r/webdev thread. The person who started it, JRM_Insights, opens with an observation that could describe any of our four scenarios:

Further down, mq2thez describes moving an old CSS-in-JS codebase to a newer pattern: “In isolation or greenfield maybe it wouldn’t be a huge issue,” but in practice, it meant rewriting “whole component trees.” TheThirdRace, who had been defending the technology, answers that they had only ever evaluated it “from a greenfield project perspective,” and that it was “very enlightening to see this through the lens of a spaghetti CSS migration.”

That is the honest version of this whole article.

Most of what we read about a new feature is written from the greenfield perspective. Most of what we ship is a migration.

Before you replace JavaScript with a modern CSS feature, or adopt one in an existing codebase, answer these six questions. Think of them as the conversation to have with the housemates before the new tenant gets a key. I suggest putting the answers right in the pull request description.

Modern CSS is great and a huge improvement to styling as a whole. In particular, container queries and :has() let us express things that used to need a script, a dependency, or a hack. But a new capability does not hand us a simpler system by default. In every scenario we looked at, the new tenant did something that didn’t sit well with others, and that was because nobody introduced them to the housemates’ ways of doing things. We get a simpler system when the new rule has one clear owner, a scope we have checked, a place in the cascade we chose on purpose, and numbers showing it helped.

So, the next time a pull request deletes more than it adds, enjoy the red lines. Then ask where the complexity went, and run the six questions. If you can’t answer one of them, the migration isn’t finished yet.

Love in the Time of Dinosaurs

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Every month, I spend hundreds of hours and thousands of dollars keeping The Marginalian going. For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. If it makes your own life more livable in any way, please consider aiding its sustenance with a one-time or loyal donation. Your support makes all the difference.

You can also become a spontaneous supporter with a one-time donation in any amount:

Partial to Bitcoin? You can beam some bit-love my way: 197usDS6AsL9wDKxtGM6xaWjmR5ejgqem7

Need to cancel a recurring donation? Go here.

For an act of resistance to the tyranny of algorithms, try the Marginalian newsletter—undistracted notes on the search for meaning, free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human since 2006. (Here is an example.)

In 1824, studying a set of large fossil bones discovered in a quarry near Oxford two centuries earlier, the English theologian, geologist, and paleontologist William Buckland kept looking at the fragment of the lower jaw. Something didn’t add up. As he examined it more closely, he realized these were not the remains of an elephant, as previously thought, but of an enormous carnivorous reptile with no analogue in the fossil record or in the living world.

He christened it Megalosaurus — the first named dinosaur — and set out to compose a complete account of the ancient beast.

Because nobody knew what dinosaurs looked like, he needed an exceptional scientific illustrator whose unbridled imagination and disciplined draughtsmanship could enflesh the bones of the mysterious creature. Taken by a particularly excellent drawing of the jaw of an extinct hyena, he obtained a letter of introduction to the artist, tucked it into his coat pocket, and boarded a coach across Devonshire, hoping to be received.

William took one book to accompany him on the long ride — a weighty new volume by paleontology founding father Georges Couvier, summarizing his pioneering research on the fossil bones of quadrupeds. With gaping disbelief, he watched another passenger take out that same book and begin paging through it. The conversation was inevitable and the realization quick: She was the artist he was traveling to meet. Cuvier himself had sent her the book in gratitude for her contribution.

Perhaps because she had lost her mother while still an infant, Mary Morland had grown up fascinated by deep time, by the slow-burning beauty of minerals, by the way fossils immortalized the ephemerality of the flesh. After devouring Couvier’s books as a teenager, she taught herself draughtsmanship and unlearned what her culture had told her she could be: Determined to become an artist of science, she sent her specimen illustrations to Couvier himself.

The venerable scientist was so taken with the young woman’s work that he published it alongside his studies. Word spread across the scientific community and soon she was illustrating other books and modeling rare specimens in leather.

Unlike most women of her time, whose standard path to subsistence was marriage, Mary could now make a living by her own work. If she married, it would only be for love. Her love was the science of time: She kept drawing paleontological specimens, grew an impressive mineral collection, became a fossil geologist herself.

By the time she met William that day on the coach, she was an old maid in her late twenties and one of the era’s most esteemed scientific illustrators. They started on the dinosaur work immediately.

Within a year, Mary Morland was Mary Buckland. Their home became a miniature Alexandria of science books, light pouring in from candle-holders made of ichthyosaurus vertebrae, fossils lining every sideboard. Their dining room housed a menagerie of frogs and snakes. Guinea pigs ran regularly across the table. A pony occasionally rode past, a laughing child on its back.

One of their surviving children would later recount that Mary not only illustrated William’s studies but, with her “great mental powers, habits of perseverance and order, tempered by excellent judgment,” she “gave to them a polish which added not a little to their merit.”

Despite their fourteen-year age difference, she outlived him by just over a year, channeling her bereavement in studying marine bryozoans — coral-like saltwater creatures — with her daughter. Hours before her death, Mary was still bent over her microscope, entranced by a new specimen.

Mary and William were buried alongside one another, their entwined fossils to be discovered by some paleontologist of the far future: What is love if not the mutual belonging of our passions and our bones.

For two decades, it has remained free, ad-free, AI-free, fully human and alive thanks to patronage from readers. I have no staff, no interns, not even an assistant — a thoroughly one-woman labor of love that is also my life and my livelihood. At This Resort, the Owner is Driving a Snowcat, Turning Wrenches, and Fixing Broken Fences

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

I’d never ridden a ski lift with the person who owns the mountain below us before, so I’m not sure if this is common.

But as we chugged from the Ski Santa Fe’s base toward its 11,075-foot peak, Ben Abruzzo noted a chairlift returning to the base that sounded squeaky, a groomer that needed tending, and an orange safety fence that had fallen over. Abruzzo—whose day was charged with showing this writer around the mountain his family has owned since the mid-1980s—interrupted his responses to any of my questions on more than a few occasions, apologizing before speaking into a small walkie-talkie mounted on his chest. He instructed this person or that to address the various tiny issues that must pop up a million times a day on a mountain, acting more like a manager than the guy who owns the mountain.

Over the course of a single lift ride, it became clear how much Abruzzo not only cared about the experience of the roughly 250,000 visitors who enjoy his family’s mountain every year but also about the mountain itself. It makes sense once you realize that Ski Santa Fe is, in a way, woven into the very fabric of the person Abruzzo is.

“It’s all I’ve ever known,” said Abruzzo, as the lift neared Ski Santa Fe’s peak, the mountain he described as his “lifelong backyard.”

Owners and CEOs often love to say that their businesses are like a family. However, for the Abruzzos, Ski Santa Fe is not only their family business, but it’s their ancestral home.

Abruzzo is the third-generation owner of Ski Santa Fe, following in the footsteps of his grandfather, his father, and two of his uncles, who finalized the purchase of the mountain in 1985. It was hardly a lark, as the elder Abruzzo purchased the Sandia Peak Ski Area in nearby Albuquerque in the late 1950s.

Their familial sensibility permeates much of Abruzzo’s staff. His grooming manager has worked at the mountain for thirty years. His operations manager as well. The mountain’s ticketing manager bests them both, with 32 years of service.

“I think it’s because we treat our folks right,” Abruzzo said, pointing out that when he says ‘we,’ he doesn’t just mean his father, his late grandfather, his uncles, and any other Abruzzo who’s spent time working the mountain. “Our mountain manager, our operations manager, all of our full-time staff; they’re all invested because they’re all part of this community.”

Abruzzo started working on the mountain when he was 12, building mountain bike trails in the summer and working in the rental shop during the season. After commissioning as an infantry officer in 2001, Abruzzo deployed to Afghanistan with the Army’s 101st Airborne Division during the height of the Global War on Terror. Abruzzo returned home in 2005, working as an area manager at Sandia Peak. He relocated to Ski Santa Fe in 2010, eventually taking over the mountain shortly before the Covid-19 pandemic.

And even though he owns the ski area, Abruzzo still drives snowcats and still turns wrenches when chairlifts need tending. And he requires the same of all the mountain’s senior staff.

“We expect all of our managers to be well-versed in the blue-collar side of things,” he said. “And that includes me.”

Sitting high in the Sangre de Cristo Mountains, Ski Santa Fe is about a 30-minute drive from the heart of the famously artsy city in northern New Mexico. Of course, snowy days and weekends might impact that commute time, Abruzzo admits, despite the fact that the mountain has its own dedicated road crew charged with plowing the two-lane road leading up to Ski Santa Fe. Still, as Ski Santa Fe is the only ski area in the vicinity, it’s a drive that never feels out of reach for locals and visitors alike.

In a more corporate environment, that dearth of options could provide owners an excuse to rest on their proverbial laurels. However, Abruzzo sees that scarcity as an honor to uphold. If people only have one option, it’d better be a good one.

With 88 total trails, almost evenly split across blacks, blues, and greens, a trio of terrain parks, and 1,800 feet of vertical drop, all serviced by a double lift, two triple lifts, two quad lifts, two surface lifts, and a high-speed detachable lift, the mountain is far bigger and more diverse than you might think when you hear “family-owned ski area.”

But beyond all that, Ski Santa Fe is known by locals and regulars for two main reasons: first, its off-piste skiing. And second, its snowmaking capabilities.

On the day we spent together, snow fell from the sky in big, thick flakes. It was one of the 20 or so powder days Ski Santa Fe gets each season. The tops of tall pine trees, buried in some 200 inches of snow, poked out of the thick layers of powder. It was a relatively unfamiliar sensation to this lifelong East Coaster who is far better versed in riding ice than powder.

Still, Abruzzo assured, even when it’s human-made, Ski Santa Fe’s snow is some of the best and most consistent there is.

“We make very dry snow,” he said before pointing out how the mountain’s 42 snow guns and five-million-gallon storage tank allow them to cover most of the mountain in snow throughout the season. “It’s dry to the point where we have had snow gun companies write us a custom setting. And what you get is a much nicer surface to ski on that stays nice longer.”

A little shy of halfway down one of our day’s runs, Abruzzo cut from one groomer to another and, suddenly, a rustic, two-story shack stood surrounded by a small sea of skiers and snowboarders, each of whom seemed to have a beer, a cocktail, or a hot chocolate in hand.

At 10,500 feet, on-mountain restaurant Totemoff’s sits higher than the peaks of many ski areas in North America.

And while there was no entertainment beyond some music playing over the speakers, Abruzzo assured me that Totemoff’s often plays host to live music. Above the bar, apartments for Abruzzo’s family and staff really hammer home the idea of how deeply entwined the Abruzzo family is 0n this mountain.

As we sat chatting over a pair of light beers, the talk eventually came back to the Abruzzo family, their lifelong marriage to this mountain. I ask him what it’s been like having three generations of Abruzzos run a mountain together. He quickly corrects me.

“Both of my kids work here,” he said with a laugh. “So, it’s actually four generations.”

Menopause Blood Protein Signature Tied to Dementia Risk

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

A multi-institutional proteomic investigation published in Nature Medicine reveals that menopause is not merely an isolated endocrine shift, but a systemic biological transformation that reshapes molecular signatures of aging. Researchers from UC Santa Barbara, UC San Francisco, and international collaborating centers analyzed plasma proteomes across thousands of women, identifying 16 specific proteins tied to neuroinflammation, neuronal maintenance, and Alzheimer’s disease risk that surge across the perimenopausal transition.

Crucially, these molecular alterations correlated with menopausal stage rather than chronological age. When evaluated across four independent cohorts totaling nearly 12,000 older women, higher baseline levels of this protein panel predicted poorer cognitive performance and a modestly increased risk of Alzheimer’s dementia up to 16 years later.

Source: UC Santa Barbara / Nature Medicine

For generations, the transition into menopause has been framed predominantly through the lens of reproductive cessation and vasomotor distress. Symptoms such as hot flashes, night sweats, insomnia, mood volatility, and cognitive “brain fog” have frequently been treated as transient inconveniences of middle age. Yet, two-thirds of all Alzheimer’s disease patients are women, a clinical disparity that cannot be explained solely by female longevity.

Because women spend roughly one-third of their lifespans in the postmenopausal state, understanding how midlife endocrine remodeling interacts with the central nervous system has become a paramount objective in preventive neurology.

Now, a multi-institutional study published in Nature Medicine demonstrates that menopause triggers a distinct molecular fingerprint detectable in peripheral blood decades before cognitive impairment manifests. Led by neuroscientists at UC Santa Barbara (UCSB), UC San Francisco (UCSF), and Sunnybrook Research Institute in Toronto, the team identified 16 blood-borne proteins that elevate across the menopause transition and correlate with long-term brain aging and dementia vulnerability.

“Menopause is not an isolated reproductive event,” explained Dr. Emily Jacobs, professor of psychological and brain sciences at UCSB and a principal investigator on the study. “It is a whole-body biological transition. Now we can begin to see a molecular fingerprint of that transition in the blood and ask how it relates to brain health decades later.”

To isolate the molecular shifts unique to this transition, the investigators leveraged the Jacobs Lab’s Healthy Aging Study, profiling thousands of circulating proteins across premenopausal, perimenopausal, and postmenopausal women.

The screen identified a discrete network of 16 proteins whose abundance altered substantially as ovarian function declined. These biomarkers mapped directly to key neurobiological pathways:

Critically, when controlling for participant age, statistical modeling confirmed that these protein elevations correlated significantly with a woman’s clinical menopausal stage rather than chronological years. Cross-referencing data with more than 2,800 women in the UK Biobank verified that the menopause transition directly aligns with accelerated biological aging profiles across multiple organ systems.

To determine whether this midlife proteomic signature translates into measurable late-life cognitive consequences, the researchers examined longitudinal health records from four independent cohorts totaling nearly 12,000 older women.

The findings showed a consistent trajectory: women who exhibited higher circulating levels of these 16 target proteins demonstrated poorer cognitive outcomes on standardized neuropsychological testing in later life. In the largest longitudinal dataset analyzed, harboring this molecular signature during midlife was associated with a modestly elevated risk of receiving an Alzheimer’s dementia diagnosis over the subsequent 16 years.

The ultimate clinical vision is to adapt this signature into a routine blood-based diagnostic panel, akin to standard lipid and cholesterol panels used to stratify cardiovascular risk, allowing clinicians to pinpoint neurodegenerative vulnerability in midlife when lifestyle, hormonal, or pharmacological interventions are most effective.

The authors emphasize that these molecular shifts do not represent a guaranteed diagnosis.

“The findings do not mean that menopause causes Alzheimer’s disease,” stated senior author Dr. Kaitlin Casaletto, associate professor of neurology at the UCSF Memory and Aging Center. “Most women do not develop Alzheimer’s, and the clinical significance of individual Alzheimer’s-related protein changes detected during midlife remains uncertain. Rather, the study highlights the importance of menopause as an opportunity to study brain aging, and eventually identify factors that promote both risk and resilience.”

Dr. Casaletto noted that midlife represents a crucial pivot point: “Midlife is a critical window for both men and women where we can really set up the brain to be more resilient or more vulnerable to dementia in later life. For women, menopause may be this especially powerful time to intervene.”

To determine how hormonal fluctuations, proteomic cascades, vasomotor symptoms, and neurostructural changes unfold in real time, the UCSB and UCSF teams, supported by the Ann S. Bowers Women’s Brain Health Initiative and the Wellcome Leap program, are launching the nationwide Longitudinal Menopause Project, actively recruiting participants and investigators to chart the neurobiology of female aging from midlife to senescence.

Funding: The work was supported in part by the Ann S. Bowers Women’s Brain Health Initiative, Wellcome Leap, the National Institutes of Health and the Noyce Trust.

Blood proteomics of menopause map to brain aging and dementia risk

Menopause is a hallmark process in biological aging that has been implicated in later neurodegenerative risk, but the pathways underlying this connection remain unclear.

Here we used blood proteomics data from several cohorts to identify biological changes associated with menopause and its links to brain aging.

In n = 80 rigorously staged (STRAW+10) pre-, peri- and postmenopausal women (aged 43–58 years) with serum NULISAseq proteomics, we show that spontaneous menopause is characterized by dysregulation in inflammatory, synaptic, metabolic and Alzheimer’s disease biologic processes, which tracked more strongly with hormones than with age.

Validation analyses in age-matched pre-/peri- and postmenopausal women (n = 2,814) with plasma Olink proteomics replicated the observed proteomic shifts and revealed broader menopause-related upregulation of inflammatory and catabolic processes plus accelerated organ and cell aging, including brain aging.

In four independent cohorts of older women (average age, 60.7–72.1 years; total n = 11,925), higher menopause proteomic scores associated consistently with cognitive aging and dementia risk. The molecular signatures of menopause may inform the selection of biomarkers or therapeutic targets for brain health in midlife women.

South Korea eyes rocket debut and China lines up four launches

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

On Monday, Nov. 2, Rocket Lab launched The Sea God Surveys on an Electron rocket from its Launch Complex 1 on New Zealand’s Māhia Peninsula at 11:41 a.m. EST. Aboard was a QPS-SAR satellite for private Japanese company iQPS It marked Electron’s 10th flight for iQPS. The company is building out a fleet of synthetic aperture radar (SAR) satellites with the goal of handing customers worldwide radar views in close to real time.

On Monday, Nov. 2, CAS Space — the commercial launch company founded by the Chinese Academy of Sciences — is targeting 7:10 p.m. EST for a Kinetica 2 launch from the Jiuquan Satellite Launch Center in northwestern China, according to Next Spaceflight. The payload has not been disclosed, but the only prior Kinetica 2 launch carried a batch of SpaceSail satellites. SpaceSail, also known as Qianfan or “Thousand Sails,” is a Chinese broadband megaconstellation designed to beam internet service from low Earth orbit (LEO).

On Wednesday, Nov. 4, SpaceX will launch the classified USSF-432 mission for the U.S. Space Force from Vandenberg Space Force Base in California at 8:28 p.m. EST. After stage separation, the Falcon 9 first stage will aim for a landing on the droneship Of Course I Still Love You, stationed in the Pacific Ocean.

On Wednesday, Nov. 4, South Korea’s Agency for Defense Development (ADD) may attempt a demonstration flight of its Mir rocket from Jeju Island, off the country’s southern coast. That date comes from Next Spaceflight’s tracking; South Korean officials have said only that the launch will happen before the end of the year. ADD, the military research arm of South Korea’s defense ministry, has been developing the solid-fueled rocket since 2021. The military plans to use it to launch small military reconnaissance satellites into orbit. The demo was originally set for June 30 but slipped over safety concerns.

On Thursday, Nov. 5, China’s state-owned launch provider, the China Aerospace Science and Technology Corporation (CASC), has two launches scheduled, according to Next Spaceflight. First, a Long March 2C is slated to lift off from the Xichang Satellite Launch Center in southwestern China at 11:00 a.m. EST with an undisclosed payload. Then at 4:15 p.m. EST, a Long March 12 will launch from the Wenchang Space Launch Site on Hainan Island, also with an undisclosed payload.

On Friday, Nov. 6, Chinese commercial company Galactic Energy will launch a Ceres 2 rocket from Jiuquan at 6:35 p.m. EST, according to tracking from Next Spaceflight. The payload has not been disclosed. This will be the second test flight for the Ceres 2 after the rocket suffered a first-stage failure and fell short of orbit on its first flight in January.

On Tuesday, Oct. 27, SpaceX launched Starlink Group 15-16 from Vandenberg at 3:25 a.m. EDT, with the booster landing on Of Course I Still Love You.

On Friday, Oct. 30, the Indian Space Research Organisation (ISRO) launched the NVS-03 navigation satellite, also known as IRNSS-1L, aboard a GSLV Mk II rocket from the Satish Dhawan Space Centre in Sriharikota. The satellite joins NavIC, India’s regional satellite navigation system — the country’s homegrown counterpart to GPS.

SpaceX followed early Saturday, Oct. 31, with Starlink Group 15-18, lifting off from Vandenberg at 12:04 a.m. EDT. Its booster also touched down on Of Course I Still Love You.

No earlier than Friday, Nov. 13, United Launch Alliance (ULA) is targeting the launch of Amazon Leo (LV-01) on a Vulcan rocket from Cape Canaveral Space Force Station in Florida. The flight will loft a batch of satellites for Amazon Leo, Amazon’s broadband constellation formerly known as Project Kuiper.

No earlier than Saturday, Nov. 14, Rocket Lab plans to fly The Mountain God Rises from Māhia — Electron’s 100th launch. The milestone flight goes to a familiar customer: another QPS-SAR radar satellite for iQPS, bound for a low Earth orbit.

How To Spot a Flood-Damaged Car Before You Buy It

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Learn the subtle signs to look for to avoid buying a flooded car.

With the average cost of a new car approaching $50,000, it can make more financial sense to buy used. But, while the lower price point is appealing, mechanic David Martin says to shop carefully and to check any used car for signs of flooding. “Engine computers, sensors, electrical connectors and wiring are vulnerable to corrosion, expensive to fix and could endanger lives,” he says. Not all cars that have been in a flood will be given a flood title, and unscrupulous sellers will mask this fact from would-be buyers. Ahead, learn how to tell if you’re buying a flood-damaged car, with expert-backed advice from Martin.

Use your nose to detect water damage inside the vehicle. “If the car smells musty or moldy, watch out,” Martin says. “Also, pay attention to cars that have strong air freshener or detergent scents. They could be used to mask flood-related mildew.” Additionally, look for silt or mud in glove compartments or under seats where it may have been overlooked during cleaning. Finding it is a tell-tale sign the car has been flooded. Also look carefully for condensation behind instrument panels and interior and exterior lights.

Check for any rust or flaking metal on the car’s underside, especially on brake lines and around the fuel tank, Martin says. Lift the hood and look for corrosion on the battery terminals and engine components, then open the car doors and inspect the door hinges for signs of rust.

Vehicle upholstery and carpeting can reveal signs of flooding. Feel for dampness and look for stains, as well as loose, wrinkled or buckled carpeting. Additionally, pull seat belts all the way out to check for stains, mildew or mud and look for mismatched or recently replaced carpeting and upholstery.

Turn on the car and do a thorough test of the car’s electrical components. Check the power windows, door locks, seat controls, and backup camera. “Make sure all lights, the air conditioner, heater, wipers, turn signals, stereo and navigation systems work,” Martin says. “And test them more than once.” Even if an electrical component is working, check to make sure it’s working properly. Flickering lights and intermittent functioning can also be signs of water damage.

Flood-damaged cars may be moved across state lines and retitled, so always get a Carfax report, which tracks a vehicle’s complete history regardless of location. Additionally, run the vehicle’s VIN number through the National Insurance Crime Bureau. “The service can identify whether a car has been reported stolen or labeled as salvage by insurance companies due to flooding,” Martin says. “But, while these reports can be helpful, they don’t cover everything.That’s why having a certified mechanic inspect a used car before you buy is a smart investment.”

Even if you don’t suspect a car has been flooded after looking it over yourself, a professional mechanic could catch subtle signs you missed, as well as any other mechanical issues with the vehicle. Ask them to specifically check for signs of flooding.

Yes, a flood-damaged car can still have a clean title and show no record of flooding in its history.

No, not if you intend to drive it. Issues can continue to arise years after a flooding event that can impact the vehicle’s functioning. However, you could buy a flooded car with the intention of using it for parts.

Mestiz Installs Monumental Día de Muertos Altar in NYC

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

For Día de Muertos, marigolds are more than decorative flowers. Their vivid orange petals and distinctive scent are believed to guide the spirits of loved ones home, making them an essential part of the Mexican tradition of remembrance. In New York City’s Flatiron District, artist and designer Daniel Valero has transformed the familiar flower into something architectural: a monumental, seven-tier altar constructed from handwoven floral forms.

Titled Twenty Petal Flower, the installation was commissioned by the Flatiron NoMad Partnership and created through a collaboration between Valero’s Mexico-based design studio, Mestiz, and Brooklyn-based fabrication organization Powerhouse Arts. On view in Flatiron North Plaza through Nov. 3, the public artwork reimagines the traditional ofrenda, or altar, as a gathering place where visitors can leave mementos honoring loved ones who have passed.

Rather than focusing exclusively on remembrance, Valero wanted the installation to recognize the people whose work makes the tradition possible.

“An altar usually honors someone we love who has passed away,” explains Valero. “This time, I wanted to honor the many hands behind the cempasúchil, from the people who grow it to those who transform it into an ofrenda.”

The installation takes its name from cempōhualxōchitl, a Nahuatl word meaning “20-petal flower,” from which the Spanish name cempasúchil is derived. Its design traces the flower’s journey from the fields of Michoacán, Mexico, to the artisans who transform it into the elaborate floral arrangements associated with Día de Muertos.

For Valero, the challenge was translating something delicate and ephemeral into a large-scale structure without losing its cultural significance or handmade character. “I’ve always been fascinated by the idea of flowers becoming architecture,” he says. “With Twenty Petal Flower, we used weaving and scale to transform something delicate and temporary into a monumental structure, creating an architecture of celebration where people can gather, remember, and connect.”

The oversized flowers were handcrafted primarily from wicker using traditional weaving techniques. Their rounded, organic forms are arranged across the altar’s seven tiers, creating a dense composition of orange petals illuminated by warm, candle-like lighting.

The Mestiz team developed and produced the woven elements at its fiber workshop in Tequisquiapan, Mexico—but bringing those intricate forms to a busy Manhattan plaza presented additional structural challenges. Powerhouse Arts worked with Mestiz to develop a supporting framework durable enough to withstand the demands of an outdoor public installation.

The collaboration involved frequent exchanges between Mexico and Brooklyn, with the fabrication team sharing photographs of CNC-cut samples, texture tests, and paint color matches to ensure that the structural elements remained faithful to Valero’s design. One of the most important decisions was how to support the flowers without making the underlying framework visually dominant.

Daniel Valero in his Mexico studio, Mestiz. Photography courtesy of Mestiz.

“We wanted the internal structure to support the woven flowers without overpowering them,” says Brittni Collins, director of public art at Powerhouse Arts, “so we built a steel frame with a slim profile and color-matched it to the cempasúchil orange so it could hide behind the weavings. The flowers were secured with stainless steel zip ties that hold up to wind and weather.”

The installation was also designed with its future in mind, Collins explains. Rather than building a temporary structure intended for a single celebration, Powerhouse Arts developed a modular system that can be disassembled, stored, and reinstalled for future celebrations.

Despite its size, the finished installation is intended to feel welcoming rather than monumental in a conventional sense. Visitors are encouraged to approach the altar, spend time with it, and contribute their own remembrances.

“I love the idea of someone walking through Flatiron NoMad and suddenly discovering this joyful, vibrant, handmade monument,” Valero says. “Bringing an ofrenda into a public space allows it to become part of the city’s everyday life, a place where people can stop, remember someone they love, and leave an offering.”

Women elements were produced at Mestiz’s fiber workshop in Tequisquiapan, Mexico. Photography courtesy of Mestiz.

Creating that kind of experience was central to the Flatiron NoMad Partnership’s vision for the project. “The scale and openness of Flatiron North Plaza gave us an opportunity to create something immersive and unexpected in the middle of the neighborhood’s everyday rhythm,” says James Mettham, president of the Flatiron NoMad Partnership. “What is so exciting about this project is that it does more than occupy the plaza. It changes how people move through it, invites them to stop and look more closely, and introduces them to the stories and traditions behind the work.”

For Valero, bringing the ofrenda to New York also creates an opportunity for cultural exchange, allowing people familiar with Día de Muertos to honor an important tradition while introducing others to its meaning. “I hope it brings a little piece of home to the Mexican community while inviting everyone in New York to share in the beauty of Día de Muertos,” he says.

Twenty Petal Flower is on view in Flatiron North Plaza in New York City through Nov. 3, 2026.

Court will hear arguments in January on six-person juries, religious zoning dispute

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Type to search articles, cases, and authors.

The Supreme Court will hear oral arguments on Jan. 13 in a Florida man’s challenge to the state’s use of six-person juries; one week later, it will hear a religious zoning dispute between a Catholic church in northern Kentucky and a couple who live across the street and oppose the church’s plans to build a shrine to the Virgin Mary. Those cases, Kian v. Florida and Missionaries of Saint John the Baptist v. Frederic, are among the highlights of the court’s January argument calendar, which was released on Monday afternoon. The justices will hear six arguments over five days, from Jan. 11-13 and again from Jan. 19-20.

15 Cozy Holiday Shows For Bingeing With An Oversized Blanket & A Mug Of Cocoa

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

From very merry matchmaking to an absurd amount of buttercream, these series will put you in the holiday spirit.

Here are the cozy holiday shows I’m queuing up this season. Come live in the twinkle lights a little while with me, won’t you?

A very merry… homicide? OK, I know, but hear me out. In this Hallmark series, Sarah Drew (yes, April from Grey’s Anatomy) plays Emily Lane, a newcomer to the quaint town of Fletcher’s Grove. There, she runs an impossibly charming Christmas shop — and keeps stumbling into murders. But trust that it is full-on cozy between all the twinkle lights and flirty banter with Peter Mooney’s Sam. Plus (!), Emily has a mysterious past of her own that just seems to get juicier every season. Season 3 premieres Thursday, Nov. 5, and Good Witch queen Catherine Bell joins the fun, so now’s the time to catch up.

Where to stream: Hallmark+, Netflix (Seasons 1 and 2)

Is there anything more quintessentially holiday-esque than baking? The GBBS holiday specials give you everything you love about the lovely culinary competition (The tent! Paul Hollywood! Desserts that make you drool!), but bring back fan-favorite bakers from past seasons to whip up festive showstoppers. I love that the stakes are blissfully low here; no one’s going home in tears. It’s the TV equivalent of a warm mince pie, and the rare show the whole fam will actually watch together. Bonus: You may be inspired to bake! If you’re anything like me, you will not succeed… but you’ll be inspired, nonetheless.

This cutie-pie series has only one season, with just eight half-hour episodes. That’ll get you through a marathon night of gift wrapping, won’t it? Cynical Dash (Austin Abrams) and endlessly sunny Lily (Midori Francis) trade dares in a red notebook they leave for each other all over New York City at Christmastime. I mean, come on, you can’t ask for much cozier than that. It’s like a scavenger hunt crossed with You’ve Got Mail, minus the corporate bookstore villainy. It’s swoony and gorgeous and features the historic Strand bookstore, and I’ll never fully forgive Netflix for canceling it after one season.

I realize this isn’t technically a holiday show, but I just feel like nothing says “cozy winter” quite like this sitcom about a fish-out-of-water New York doctor stuck in the tiny, quirky, snowy town of Cicely, Alaska. It’s peak ‘90s comfort TV, aka weird in the best ways. I highly recommend watching the whole series from the start. However, if you just need a quick fix, start with the Christmas episode “Seoul Mates” from Season 3 and then just keep going until spring.

One of my favorite Christmas shows to come out of the last few years, this series sees Mae Whitman (forever Amber from Parenthood) as Kate, a perfectly content single textile designer in an English village whose best friends sign her up for a holiday matchmaking program. The gist: 12 themed dates leading up to Christmas. It’s all very Bridget Jones by way of a Christmas market with (bonus!) Jane Seymour and Mary McDonnell along for the ride, not to mention a sweet B-plot about Kate’s dad finding love again. Six episodes with a satisfying ending and no cliffhanger? Total bliss.

Since we’re on the subject of Hallmark holiday series, might I put you on to Hallmark’s official first-ever holiday series? This one follows six families on one cul-de-sac as they navigate the chaos (and, yes, joy) of the season. It’s a totally stacked cast, too, thanks to Ian Harding, Erin Cahill, Rachelle Lefevre, and Dennis Haysbert in the mix. Basically, it’s a warm ensemble-cast hug of a show that’ll have you weirdly ready to head home for the holidays and straight into your family’s distinctive brand of holiday dysfunction.

Anyone who’s ever fielded “So… are you seeing anyone new?” over holiday dinner will relate to our heroine here: Johanne, a 30-something nurse who panics under family pressure and claims she’s bringing a boyfriend home for Christmas. Ah, classic setup! That means she has 24 days to find one, so the race is on. This Norwegian rom-com is funny and real and snowy and absolutely worth the subtitles.

I never thought I’d be so into a reality series about glassblowing, but here we are! Here, glassblowers make holiday ornaments, snow globes, and festive sculptures out of molten glass and, I gotta be honest, it’s oddly soothing. It’s a solid choice when you need the kind of background TV to put on that the kids won’t complain about.

Dennis Quaid is in peak Dennis Quaid form as an intense family patriarch who meets his youngest daughter’s musician boyfriend over the holidays. It’s got that classic multicam sitcom feel, laugh track and all, that nails the delightful agony of spending a holiday week trapped in your parent’s house. It’s only one season, making it an easy binge.

There’s just something about a show that takes place in a small town where everybody’s all up in your business that feels somehow inherently holiday-esque. So no, this one’s not specifically a Christmas series, but it feels right at home on the list. Anna Lambe stars as Siaja, a young Inuk mom who walks away from her marriage and tries to build a new life in the tiny Arctic town of Ice Cove. It’s a super-snowy series that manages to come across incredibly warm, not to mention laugh-out-loud funny. Season 1 swept the Canadian Screen Awards, and Season 2 lands on Netflix Nov. 5.

Was there ever any doubt? Stars Hollow in the snow is the blueprint for cozy TV. Lorelai’s love of wintry weather (“I smell snow”), the town’s over-the-top seasonal festivities, Luke’s Diner, enough coffee to kill a horse… it’s like a holiday show that happens to be year-round. Whether you’re watching for the 20th time or finally indoctrinating your teen, there’s truly no wrong time to go back to Stars Hollow. There’s definitely a right time, though, and it’s now.

How do I love this show? Let me count the ways: 1. Tina Fey, 2. Will Forte, 3. Colman Domingo, 4. Steve Carell, 5. Kerri Kenney-Silver, 6. Marco Calvani, 7. Erika Henningsen… OK, OK, you get the point. Which is that there are many reasons to love this Netflix series about a group of longtime friends who vacation together four times a year. Every season of the show includes a winter getaway, complete with cozy cabin vibes. Season 2 dropped this spring and Season 3 is in production, so you’ve got 16 episodes to work through for now.

Ugh, my heart! In this lovely series, the late, great Treat Williams stars as a big-shot New York neurosurgeon who uproots his grieving kids to a tiny mountain town in Colorado. Cue snow streets and small-town eccentrics and lots of earnest WB-era feelings! In full transparency, this one might make you cry into your hot cocoa. At four seasons long, it makes for the perfect long December binge.

I cannot stress enough how much any self-professed devotee of cozy shows should be watching this adaptation of James Herriot’s books. All Creatures Great and Small follows a young vet in the Yorkshire Dales, and every season ends with the exact kind of Christmas special your holiday-loving heart hopes for, between snowy farmhouses, roaring fires at the pub, and loved ones gathered around the table at Skeldale House. Season 7 doesn’t hit PBS until Jan. 17, 2027, so you’ve got this final stretch of the year to get all caught up.

Where to stream: PBS app (with PBS Passport) and PBS Masterpiece on Prime Video

Host Jonathan Bennett (aka Aaron Samuels) and judge Melissa Peterman put 10 aspiring leading men through festive challenges in this Hallmark holiday competition. Think Santa auditions, gingerbread builds, and scene work with Hallmark royalty like Tyler Hynes and Alison Sweeney. The goal? To crown one the next star of a Countdown to Christmas movie. It’s cheesy in the way that all the best holiday stuff is, and will actually hit you right in the feels when you least expect it.

Get your Christmas-print jammies out; it’s time to start your cozy holiday binge sesh.
