Mamdani and Hochul Visit Queens Night Market to Get Out the Vote

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Zohran Mamdani wanted to share with the governor, but the vendor at the Taiwanese Yummy tent wasn’t having it. Cheng Liu insisted the New York mayor try his own bowl of beef noodle soup—the chef’s way.

Instead of trying a bite from Governor Kathy Hochul, Mamdani watched and waited as Liu arranged slippery, wide noodles underneath pickled mustard greens, herbs, and slices of succulent brisket that had been cooked for eight hours, all ladled with a delicate broth. Mamdami started to dig in with his chopsticks when Liu rushed over to spoon in a generous amount of homemade chili oil.

“The mayor is not scared of spicy food,” Liu said via his son’s translation. “We wanted to give him Taiwanese cuisine.”

At the Queens Night Market, Mamdani and Hochul struck an unusual pair: a New York mayor and governor who get along.

Three days before Election Day, the political allies arrived at Flushing Meadows Corona Park in a motorcade of black SUVs to pose for photos and get out the vote for the Democratic party. Hochul has made the city part of her final push in a reelection campaign against Republican Bruce Blakeman, the Nassau County executive endorsed by President Donald Trump.

In a joint interview with Hochul, Mamdani told Bon Appétit that he was showing up for the governor because she was showing up for immigrant New Yorkers. Earlier this year, Hochul signed legislation to ban local and state law enforcement from cooperating with federal immigration operations, and to stop police at all levels from covering their face when interacting with the public.

“The governor has stood up and called for public safety to actually be public safety and not just be an assistance and an accompaniment of ICE,” Mamdani said. “That has been part of what makes people feel like they can be part of this city.”

The mayor also mentioned that the Queens Night Market was a favorite with constituents because of the price. Every dish is capped at $6. Hochul, running on affordability, points to her expansion of free school lunch statewide. “As a mom, this is personal,” she said. “I made a lot of peanut butter and jelly sandwiches over the years for kids.”

Representing different generations and factions of their party, Mamdani and Hochul recognize what they stand to gain together. Mamdani needed Hochul to expand free childcare and greenlight plans for a second-home tax. Hochul needs the enthusiasm of the Democratic Socialist mayor’s voting bloc.

Hochul said she and Mamdani are aligned on lowering the cost of living and supporting the small business owners that make the food and nightlife scene so special.

“It’s a rare day in New York for this kind of relationship,” Hochul said, “but when we talk to each other, we don’t disagree on these issues.”

The pair of politicians approached the night market in a coordinated operation that included advance teams, extra security, and business casual ’fits: Hochul in jeans, Mamdani tie-less in a quarter-zip. The mayor—who has made his appetite a key part of his public identity—appeared less stiff in his interactions with food vendors, but Hochul gamely grabbed chopsticks and posed for selfies.

John Wang, who launched the Queens Night Market in 2015, said politicians are a regular presence here. “Every election season, they come out,” Wang said. “Even if they’ve never been here before, they’re like, ‘Yeahhh! I love the Queens Night Market.’” He said some (2020 presidential candidate Andrew Yang, for instance) get feted like Mamdani, and others (N.Y. Sen. Chuck Schumer) draw a tamer response. Representative Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez used to come a lot, Wang said, and would always get swarmed by supporters.

For the last market of the season, which fell on Halloween, Mamdani might as well have been a member of BTS. Vendors and visitors alike stared slackjawed, squealed, and uttered “oh my God!”

A young man named Asad showed up in a Mamdani costume with no idea he would meet the mayor, let alone pose for photos that brought him to tears.“This is Bruce Blakeman’s nightmare!” the mayor said of his Mamdani double.

“It was surreal, emotional a little bit,” said Asad, who declined to share his last name. Asad said he was born in Karachi, Pakistan, became a U.S. citizen in 2022, and voted for Mamdani after moving to New York from Florida.

At the Ella Spice stand, Akera Phillip represents Grenada with breadfruit croquettes, fried cou-cou, and to-go pouches of sorrel. Phillip, who named her business after her grandmother, felt “elated” and “blessed” to serve her food to the leaders of the state and the city.

“As a small island girl, it just made me feel big,” she said.

Akera Phillip holds breadfruit croquettes.

At the Epis by Steve tent, Hochul told Steve Lahens she could handle “Buffalo spicy,” a nod to her hometown, rather than admit to any trouble with his Haitian pikliz. Lahens and his wife fixed up plates of griot (fried pork) with diri djondjon (black rice) and makawoni au graten, a Caribbean mac and cheese he makes with bell peppers, “evaporated milk and a few little things I can’t talk about.”

Lahens said he was born in Haiti but came to the U.S. in 1988. A workplace injury pushed him toward cooking, “and it changed my life, for the better.” He began cooking out of his house but now works out of the Mangrove shared kitchen at the Flatbush Central Caribbean Marketplace. He was excited to hobnob with the governor and the mayor, but Lahens also said New York small businesses need more help.

“The rent could be lower so people like me could get a spot,” Lahens said. “Right now I’m stuck in catering. I love it, too, but my whole plan and goal is to get a storefront.”

Plant Your Holiday Porch Pots Now – These 7 Beautiful Plants Create a Spectacular Christmas Display

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

What Your Jade Plant Needs in October – 7 Tips to Keep It Gorgeous and Glossy Through Winter

These 7 Plants Need Pruning Before Winter – November is Your Last Chance to Make the Cut

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

Why settle for decorations that only last a few weeks? Create beautiful seasonal containers that can brighten your entrance well into winter.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Holiday porch pots are a cheerful way to decorate your home's entrance for the festive season. These outdoor winter arrangements look beautiful and welcoming when placed near front doors, steps, or entryways to greet guests during the holidays.

As October ends, the holiday season approaches. Families make Thanksgiving plans, starting a season that lasts right through the new year. This is the ideal time to start putting together natural holiday decorations that bring bright colors and greenery to your home's entrance.

If you are ready to jump into a creative gardening project for winter, we’ve got the ideas to get you started. The basic idea is to combine beautiful plants and greenery to make spectacular seasonal containers. But you should plan to have some fun while you are doing it.

The best plants for DIY holiday porch pots combine appealing structure with festive cut boughs. They should be hardy enough to survive cold weather and add winter curb appeal.

Why not start with an evergreen that nobody else on your block is likely to display? This silver Korean fir cultivar is one of a kind: a curious, fascinating dwarf evergreen with twisted, curled silvery needles. The twisted needles of ‘Ice Breaker’ Korean fir (Abies koreana), available at Nature Hills, have frosted undersides, making it look as if it is perpetually snow-covered. These little beauties grow very slowly, getting to 2 feet tall after 10 years. They are hardy down to -20°F.

It’s easy to find evergreen foliage plants without red berries, but harder to find red berries without evergreen foliage. That’s exactly why winterberry holly (Ilex verticillata) in holiday porch pots is totally intriguing. You can order a best-selling Berry Poppins winterberry holly from Plant Addicts.

These native hollies – growing in eastern Canada through the southern United States – are pretty plants all year long, but they become stunningly beautiful at the dreariest time of the year. They lose their leaves in autumn, leaving only the amazing crop of bright berries to light up your winter pots. They are hardy down to USDA zone 3.

Sign up for the Gardening Know How newsletter today and receive a free copy of our e-book "How to Grow Delicious Tomatoes".

Ornamental cabbages and kales (Brassica oleracea) are cold-hardy foliage plants that provide exceptionally vivid white, pink, red, and purple winter colors for holiday pots. They are technically edible but are not grown for eating. Rather, they are cultivated for their bright, rose-like center rosettes.

Ornamental cabbage and kale plants maintain their bright structure and color well into freezing winter months. For a festive container, they can be combined with winter-hardy pansies, berried twigs, chrysanthemums, or ornamental grasses.

Pick up a four-pack of Song Bird Red Ornamental Kale Plants from Burpee.

You may know these stunning plants as hellebores (Helleborus niger), or as Christmas roses. Whatever you call them, they make fantastic additions to holiday porch pots, with their shining white flowers and strong, dark green leaves. The only issue with hellebores is that they usually don’t bloom until January – past the heart of the holiday porch pot season. But some cultivars bloom just in time for the holidays, such as ‘Christmas Carol Early’, 'Jacob', and 'Snowbells', available at Plant Delights Nursery. The Christmas rose is usually hardy to USDA zone 3.

This dense, naturally round shrub is a perfect little ball of soft green needles. Its small size and low-maintenance nature make Little Gem Norway spruce (Picea abies 'Little Gem') a popular addition to your holiday porch pot. You can order a plant from Magic Valley Gardens at Walmart.

Norway spruce grows slowly, adding only about an inch or two in size every year and topping out at about 3 feet tall and wide. Don’t worry about winter weather – ‘Little Gem’ is hardy down to -40°F. The new growth emerges soft and bright lime, but by Christmas it will be a handsome forest green.

The flowers that give Heuchera its common name of coral bells only appear in spring and summer. By the time the holiday season rolls around, you’ll appreciate this plant's incredible foliage. Coral bells offer a wide range of leaf colors beyond green, including apricot, bronze, pewter, burgundy, and deep purple. The foliage often adds even more enchantment with scalloped, wavy, or veined edges. Select ‘Caramel’ or ‘Peachberry Ice’ cultivars, both available at Plant Addicts, for foliage with rich tones and holiday warmth.

Many trailing “spiller” plants work well in holiday plant pots, but few are better than the classic evergreen trailing English ivy (Hedera helix), available in an ivy variety pack from Plant Addicts. This hardy vine softens the edges of holiday planters and offers all-weather resilience. It stays vibrant throughout the season and works well with flowers or small evergreen shrubs to create a festive feel. The cascading leaves create a rich, full look, balancing upright or mounding holiday plants.

If you’re ready to create gorgeous holiday plant pots of your own, a few practical tips will help. Let’s get down to the basics of how to put it all together.

The base of it all is the pots or containers you select. Choose pots in materials that can handle winter temperatures, like metal, wood, or even plastic. Avoid materials that will crack when the temperatures drop. Get pots that are big enough, but not too big. Generally, front door holiday plant pots should be between 12 and 30 inches in diameter and between 12 and 18 inches deep.

You’ll want to use potting soil in the containers. Leftover summer potting soil works just fine, but it’s best to buy fresh, like Miracle-Gro Organic Outdoor Potting Mix, from Amazon.

Next, you’ll want to put in the plants, and I suggest using a classic thriller, filler, spiller design. Start with the thrillers. These are the tall, eye-catching focal plants that make guests admire the pots. You can use one of the taller plants you have selected – like winterberry holly or ‘Ice Breaker’ fir – or you can use cut boughs after trimming the stems.

Get ready to layer in the fillers. This is the place for medium-height evergreens or foliage plants. Coral bells or ornamental cabbage work well in this role. Alternate textures and shades.

Last, add the spillers, plants with draping or trailing foliage. These go around the bottom edge of the container so that they cascade gracefully over the edges of the container.

Creating your plant pots is fun, but you’ll also have to put in a little effort to keep them going strong through the season.

This starts with care immediately after you set out the pots. Give each of them a deep watering, enough to saturate the entire soil. Excess water should run out of the drainage holes in the bottom of the pot. Make sure that’s the case, or your plants will die of root rot. It helps to add pot feet to elevate the containers off freezing patios.

Don’t worry too much about freezing weather. It is normal for the moisture in the patio pots to freeze. Keep checking the soil and add more water if the top inches are dry. Mist the evergreen branches during dry winter days to rehydrate the foliage. As the weather cools, cluster the containers close to the house wall to shield them from winter winds and create a microclimate.

Teo Spengler is a master gardener and a docent at the San Francisco Botanical Garden, where she hosts public tours. She has studied horticulture and written about nature, trees, plants and gardening for more than two decades. Before turning her focus to gardening, she worked as an attorney and legal writer, experience she also brings to her role as our Gardening Lawyer. Her extended family includes some 30 houseplants and hundreds of outdoor plants, including 250 trees, which are her main passion. Spengler currently splits her life between San Francisco and the French Basque Country, though she was raised in Alaska, giving her experience of gardening in a range of climates.

How to Strengthen Your Arms and Wrists With Upward Plank Variations

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

In Yoga Journal’s Archives series, we share a curated collection of articles originally published in past issues beginning in 1975. This article about Upward Plank Pose variations first appeared in the June 2007 issue of Yoga Journal. Find more of our Archives here.

What direction do you face when you practice yoga? Probably whichever way your usual practice space dictates. But the old yogis were very clear that we should face either east or north when practicing, per the classical yoga text the Gheranda Samhita 5.48. Why?

Yoga scholar Alain Danielou writes that the four cardinal and four intermediate points of the compass have special significance. The thunderbolt-wielding Indra, lord of all the protectors and prototype of courage and power in Hindu mythology, guards the east.

By facing in a certain direction when we practice, we acknowledge and draw on energetic properties for support and inspiration. So again, why east and north? East is the quarter of the rising sun, which Hindus revere as the “breath of creatures” or the source of all earthly life.

The sun represents the embodied Self, its dawn the emergence of the “light” of spiritual self-awareness and insight. North is the quarter of the pole star, called dhruva (pronounced DREW-vah) in Sanskrit, meaning “fixed, eternal.” Its constancy amid the ever-wheeling night sky is considered the embodiment of willpower, certainly a quality all serious yogis covet. In India too, north is the realm of the majestic Himalayas, where Hindu tradition places the mythic mountain Meru, thought of as the “navel” of the earth as well as the bridge to the celestial world of the gods and goddesses.

What compass directions would you assign to the back and front of your body? According to the yogis, your back body is the west, your front body the east. In Sanskrit, paschima means both “back” and “west,” while purva means both “front” and “east.” So this terminology gives us the names of two common and related poses: Paschimottanasana (Intense Stretch of the West Pose or, more commonly, Seated Forward Bend), which stretches your back body, and Purvottanasana (Intense Stretch of the East or Upward Plank Pose)—pictured here—a slight backbend and front-body stretch that provides a nice counterpose to its western opposite.

Purvottanasana stretches you all the way from your ankles through the thighs, groins, and belly, up to the shoulders and throat. It also strengthens your wrists and the muscles on the backs of the upper arms (triceps) and firms the buttocks—which, if you believe popular media claims, is a primary benefit of yoga. It will also help improve your sense of balance and calm. And it’s a great counterpose to the commonly practiced Chaturanga Dandasana (Four-Limbed Staff Pose).

If you have serious wrist, shoulder, or neck problems, practice this pose only under the guidance of an experienced teacher. You’ll need a chair, a block, and a wall to practice some of these preparatory poses.

Sit on the floor with your legs extended straight in front of you in Dandasana (Staff Pose). Rotate your thighs inward, big toes in, heels out, and press your palms to the floor slightly behind your buttocks with your fingers pointing forward toward your feet. If your shoulders protest, you can turn your fingers outward.

Bend your elbows and sink or hollow your front torso, spreading your scapulas as far away from your spine as possible. Rotate your upper arms inward and widen your elbows, letting the heads of your upper arm bones (humeri) jut forward. Enjoy the stretch between the scapulas for a minute or so.

Then straighten your elbows and lift your chest while leaving your lower body on the floor. Often, students simply squeeze their scapulas together and shove their lower front ribs forward, which hardens the front torso, restricting the free flow of breath, and compresses the low back. Instead, keep your scapulas wide and front torso soft.

Press the bases of your index fingers into the floor and hug your triceps against your humeri. As your elbows straighten out and your chest lifts, maintain the inward rotation of your upper arms, which will help the scapulas stay broad. Release your head back slightly, but don’t fully extend the neck. Trace the stretch along your front torso from your pubis to your sternum (breastbone).

Imagine directing your inhalations into the space behind your sternum (“imagine” because there’s no lung there, just your heart), increasing the lift of your chest. With each exhalation, reaffirm the opposing movements of the inner hands (down against the floor) and scapulas (up against your back). Hold for a minute or two. Repeat two or three times, until you feel confident you’ll be able to recreate the inward rotation of the thighs and arms and the actions of the hands and scapulas while maintaining the soft front torso when called on to do so in the more challenging circumstances of the full pose.

Next, sit on the front edge of a chair with your knees bent, heels directly below your knees. Wrap your hands around the back edge of the seat (for this first part of the exercise your upper arms will naturally rotate outward), thumbs pointing outward (Figure 1).

Inhale and slowly lift your buttocks away from the seat until your thighs, torso, and head form one long diagonal line, with your arms more or less perpendicular to the chair seat. Rotate your thighs slightly inward, and push (but don’t physically move) your knees away from your torso. The thigh rotation will tend to make your tailbone sag toward the floor, collapsing your belly, so firm your tailbone against your back pelvis and lengthen it. Hold this position for a minute, then sit back on the chair. On an inhalation, release the back edge of the seat and return to sitting upright. Take a few breaths.

If you like, repeat this exercise with your fingers turned forward, palms on the seat, and your upper arms rotated inward. Hold for one minute, release on an exhalation, and rest.

If you want to take this exercise to its limit, lift your buttocks again and extend your legs. Do your right leg first. Inhale and press out through your heel to straighten your knee, but suspend your foot a few inches off the floor. Then rotate your thigh inward, point your toes, and bring your sole to the floor. Just as the bases of your index forgers press the seat, so the base of your big toe presses on the floor. Repeat with the left leg. Once again, hold for a minute and release on an exhalation.

If you want to continue, brace the back legs of a chair against the wall and lay your block on the seat (if you’re using a wood block, cover the seat with a sticky mat to keep the block from sliding). Set the block on either its lowest or middle height near the front edge of the seat.

Sit on the floor right in front of the chair, knees bent, feet on the floor, heels 12 to 15 inches away from your buttocks, and lean back against the front edge of the seat. Press your hands on the floor with your fingers turned either forward, to the sides, or back—you choose—just in front of the feet of the chair legs.

Inhale and do what you did for the first part of the first exercise: Lift your thighs and torso parallel to the floor (heels below the knees), and then release your head back onto the block to take any pressure off your neck. If the block is too high or too low, come down and readjust. If you are having trouble opening your chest, it may help to reinforce the firmness of your palms against the floor, your scapulas against your back, and your triceps against the humeri. Then again, it may not.

Don’t get discouraged. Just practice this exercise every other day or so for the next few weeks, and you’ll be surprised how quickly things improve. Whatever happens, hold the pose for 30 seconds to a minute, then release to the floor on an exhalation. Smile.

If you can’t boost your chest, though, trying to extend your legs will probably make matters worse, so I don’t recommend it. But if your chest is reasonably lifted, then it’s acceptable to extend your legs.

Having trouble getting the balls of your feet, especially the bases of your big toes, on the floor? Position a rolled-up sticky mat on the floor where the balls of your feet will be when your legs are extended and press against that.

Sommelier-Approved Thanksgiving Wines

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

The challenge of picking wine for Thanksgiving is multifaceted. Your selections must be lively enough to enjoy as guests work their way through charcuterie, balanced enough to play well with turkey, and versatile enough to rub shoulders with dessert. It’s a tall order for anyone tasked with bringing the bottles to their holiday meal. To help, five beloved wine-heads from across the country weighed in on the bottles that everyone around your holiday table will adore.

Here are the crowd-pleasing offerings sure to liven up your Thanksgiving.

For Adam Robins, beverage director at State Bird Provisions and The Progress in San Francisco, there’s no better way to start a festive meal than with Champagne. Bérêche & Fils delivers classic brioche notes tempered with fruit like Bosc and Asian pears, brought together with a snap of acidity to prime the palate for the feast ahead

Les Cigales dans la Fourmilière Escarpolette Blanc 2024

Los Angeles’s Lasita is beloved for its natural wine selections and its menu of kicky Filipino favorites. Co-owners Chase and Steff Valencia adore this Languedoc orange wine, which offers notes of bold fruit—peach and apricot—and a bright acidity that can cut through even the butteriest of mashed potatoes.

Peter Lauer Riesling Schonfels Kabinett No. 111

“More people should be drinking Riesling,” says Matt Turner of Lei, one of Bon Appétit’s Best New Restaurants of 2026. He points to this German stunner with direct minerality and notes of stone fruit, which sips just as well alongside a cheesy cauliflower gratinée as a garlic-studded beef tenderloin.

At the James Beard–nominated Wild Child in Lafayette, Louisiana, natural wine rules supreme. Ian LaCour, who helps curate the bottle list, gravitates toward this muscular red. Thanks to a silky body and dusky notes of stewed red fruit, it plays well with the “heartier proteins on the Thanksgiving table.”

You’d be forgiven for being unfamiliar with macvin, a niche style of fortified wine made in France’s Jura region. But the oaky fresh apple and cider notes in this punchy bottling—akin to a less-oxidized, sweeter oloroso sherry—make for a divine digestif. “It fares well with every dessert,” says Nick Arone of Detroit’s Bar Chenin.

This Climber Just Broke Dean Potter’s Half Dome Speed Record By Free Soloing ‘Snake Dike’

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Michael Reese on the summit of Half Dome after breaking the trailhead-to-summit speed record (Photo: Courtesy of Michael Reese)

On October 28, 32-year-old American climber Michael Reese set a new fastest known time on Yosemite’s Half Dome, breaking an 11-year record set by the late Dean Potter. Reese climbed the 2,000-foot batholith from the Mist Trailhead to its summit in 1 hour 12 minutes 58 seconds, following Potter’s original line up Grizzly Gully and free soloing Snake Dike (5.7 R; 800ft). He broke Potter’s previous record from May 3, 2015, by about six minutes.

Potter, who famously competed with Hans Florine to set the speed record on the Nose (5.9 C2; 3,000ft), saw the Grizzly Gully to Snake Dike line as a step toward blending trail running and climbing; he was aiming for a sub-two-hour time from trailhead to trailhead. He died 13 days after setting the record in a wingsuit accident at Taft Point.

Reese grew up in Boulder and came to climbing from running and triathlon. In September, he set a new speed record on the First Flatiron, at 7 minutes 51 seconds. After interviewing him last week in Boulder for a deep dive on the culture of free solo speed climbing, Reese told me he was headed for Yosemite the following day. I wished him luck. Having climbed both the First Flatiron and Snake Dike, I knew the latter would be a different game. The grippy, coarse sandstone of the Flatirons is a far cry from the slick, polished granite of Yosemite.

A few days after his Half Dome lap, I caught up with Reese to talk about his extensive training, the day itself, his safety margins on route, why he’s hoarded the same shoes as Potter since 2021, and why he thinks Potter’s record has stood for over a decade.

The following has been lightly edited for concision and clarity.

Michael Reese: Since I got into climbing in 2014, Dean Potter has been one of my idols, probably the one who I looked up to the most. The reason for that is he’s a hybrid athlete, you know? I come from a background in triathlon. I really like to mix cardio and strength. So Dean Potter was such an amazing individual because he had a professional-level aerobic and running fitness in addition to being one of the best free soloists, one of the best speed climbers, and one of the best BASE jumpers and highliners in the world. So I said, “Wow, how amazing is this individual to mix disciplines into territory no one’s ever seen?”

In 2021, one of Dean Potter’s good friends told me about this as a potential record to try. He became one of my main climbing partners. Of course, you know, this is 2021. Dean Potter had passed away years before. I went in 2021. I got totally destroyed because the approach was way too technical. I got lost, turned around in Grizzly Gully. It’s just such technical terrain. I went back on occasion, and started to learn the very complex approach of Grizzly Gully of alternating slabs, mazes, trees.

Bit by bit, I learned every year. I first went for a speed attempt in 2023. I went again in 2024, but I was really far off. I did 1 hour 37 [minutes] both times because it was really hard to figure out where to go. I wasn’t in as good of shape as I am in now, so I needed more fitness; I needed more practice.

Finally I came back this year, being in a lot better shape, spent two weeks practicing the route, then gave it a go.

Reese: Sixteen times before going for the record. Eight times free solo. Eight times with ropes and practicing the moves.  I practiced the crux 10 times in a row each lap, going back and forth across memorizing holds.  I didn’t really tick anything.  I jumped up and down until I purposefully would slip [while being clipped in]. I’d purposefully slip and find, “Okay, it took this amount of mistakes to actually cause a fall.”

Reese: Yes. The [complexity] of the Grizzly Gully approach, which is probably a low fifth-class approach with a lot of slabs. It has everything. It has running, it has steep, fourth-class dirt. That’s why I consider it the premier of technical mountain running, mixed running, and climbing, because you’re constantly switching gears.

Reese: We always did it a combination of simul-climbing and pitching it out. We would basically simul-climb to the crux section or sections. And then on those crux sections, I would be leading, and I would stop and [built a] traditional anchor and belay them up. That way they could give me a good belay for practicing the crux section.

Reese:  There was a lot of things that almost made it where I wouldn’t have been able to get the record. For example, I had gotten very sick just from being out in the cold. I had a cough. I had rolled my ankle two days before. So I was trying to nurse all these things, and I was just right on the fence of being like, “Am I healthy enough to go through this?”

Reese:  I’d scouted it out two days previously. Then I had a nice rest day where I just went for a picnic. I tried to ice my feet and I taped up my ankle.

My girlfriend started hiking a few hours before me to get in position because I wanted her to get some footage at the base. I hung out in Curry Village and rested. I did my typical routine where I drink a tablespoon of matcha powder to wake myself up and get amped up. I drank a ton of electrolytes because I knew that dehydration could be an issue. It was a very sunny day.

I went to the parking lot, I got on my shoes, and I put on gaiters to try to prevent a rock from going in[to my shoes in] the very dirty terrain. I had on just my watch, shoes, and shorts. No shirt because it was getting warmer. So then I did my typical warm up. I jogged for 15 minutes. I went up Grizzly Gully for a little bit to warm up my steep hiking muscles. I did some 30-second strides to get my heart rate elevated.

I  sipped some water, but tried not to overdo it, did some more strides, and then I meditated for a few minutes just to get in a good head space and say, “This is what I’ve been training for. This is my opportunity and it’s time to live in the moment.”

Reese: Not particularly. People looked at me like I was kind of crazy. Why am I sprinting back and forth at the trailhead? Why do I have no shirt when it’s 40 degrees out?

Reese:  Yeah. I connected my watch to GPS and began the effort from the Mist Trailhead on the pavement there. I had to swerve around some tourists for a minute and then I turned into Grizzly Gully. I didn’t see anyone [else] until I got to the base.

Reese:  Around noon. It was a pretty chill there, about 40 degrees. I wouldn’t mind it to warm up a little. And also because I needed to be very careful about other parties on route. Most places I could pass a party, but in a few places, like those first few pitches in the crux section, it could have been devastating to have a party on the way there.

Reese: I figured starting later would improve my chances of other parties at least being out of the crux section and a little bit further up, and, and that did luckily work out like that.

Reese:  Yeah, that’s a good question. A little bit of both. There’s a lot of places where the beta would vary so much I can’t rehearse everything. On the other hand, as you know, there’s a couple sections that the dike splits into two sections, and I do have each section rehearsed. So for the most part, I have options, and it would be okay besides that crux section. That would be pretty catastrophic.

Reese:  That’s a really good question. I do always prioritize safety. So if I came across a party and they were both in the anchor, or for example, if there was maybe just a just a follower that was securely on a tight top rope and it wouldn’t add any risk to them … and I was on record pace, I would be pretty keen to find a way to make it work, and maybe ask them just not to move, and I could tiptoe around. If there was a case where there was like a leader on the crux who was not looking solid, and I thought that going through would add risk to them or me … I would maybe bail on the attempt. Safety does come first. So yes, I would bail.

Reese:   I was going really hard, but I was saving just enough gas that I knew I wouldn’t, like, completely buckle on the crux. Then I hit the climb and felt really well. I instantly knew it was gonna be a good day on the rock. I was just kind of detecting every minuscule dish, kind of nailing the beta. And there were the people filming me at the crux section, cheering. I’d rehearsed it so much, it felt like a treat just to get across the crux and nail everything and feel really solid there. I still didn’t know if I would get it because I was gonna have to climb it faster than I ever had for the record still.

But it just flowed so well. So I did Snake Dike in 10 [minutes] 55 [seconds]. There was a special moment where I knew I would get the record because I knew I needed to finish them in, you know, 16 minutes or something. I knew I had done them in about 12 or 13 before. [That day] I did them in about 11 minutes. That Death March—that was the crux. As you know, that steep stuff after the Snake Dike, I was starting to kind of buckle, and I had a headache from dehydration. My knees and arms were starting to kind of buckle from lactic acid. But I knew I’d get the record, but I also just wanted to do the best I could and make a time that would last. So I pushed to the summit, sprinted across to the true summit, past the cables to the highest end on the north end. There was a party hanging out there.

I ran and tagged the summit, breathing hard. They were like, “Geez, dude,” like, “What did, what did you do?” I couldn’t even… My response was just dry heaving ’cause I’d gone so hard.

Reese:   The Five Ten Tennies. I bought a big stockpile of them before they went out in 2021 because I knew they were some of the best shoes and may not be made again. It’s the same ones that Dean Potter used on his attempt.

Reese:    I think the length of time honestly was kind of borderline. I think anything longer, it would be important to have something. Anything shorter, you don’t really need it. You do have a good point there. This actually was kind of a borderline scenario . Everyone’s different. For me, this one was right on the fence of bringing anything or not. And it depends on the day. If it’s a hot summer day, maybe I would change and bring something. Under the same conditions, I’m happy with not having anything.

Reese:    I think it’s because of how unique and special the line is. The magic is that most peaks, the fastest way is gonna be running around to the summit. On El Cap, the impressive nose record, you know, an hour 50… you can run around El Cap faster than that. You could run around the side and get up there in under two hours.

On Half Dome, it’s interesting, because from the Mist Trailhead to the summit, you summit up the cable. Most people go up the cables in the Subdome. So that requires running around everything, whereas Snake Dike allows a very direct line to the top.

One reason I think it’s lasted so long is Dean Potter was such an amazing athlete with his combined running and climbing. He had this high level of running combined with being able to solo Snake Dike, obviously a very easy free solo for him. So there are people that may be able to run a little bit faster, but they can’t solo Snake Dike. There are people that can solo Snake Dike, but they have nowhere near the fitness.

There have been some amazing athletes like Nick Elson from Canada.  He was able to tie Dean’s time by taking the shortcut through the forest and running around, splitting Liberty Cap and Broad Nick, shortcut through the forest to cut the switchbacks, and then up the cables.  Now everyone, including myself, starts at the Mist Trailhead. Which is an extra 60 seconds. [Editor’s note: Elson’s trailhead-to-summit time was 1 hour 20 minutes 57 seconds. He currently holds the round-trip speed record on Half Dome, with 2 hours 3 minutes 52 seconds].

When people said, “Oh, is Dean Potter’s vision really the best to go up Grizzly Gully and Snake Dike?” I think what I did proves that Dean Potter had the vision because Nick Elson, being one of the best trail runners… He’s extremely fit, one of the best athletes. I wouldn’t be able to beat his time by eight minutes to the summit off of pure fitness. So the eight-minute difference shows that Dean Potter’s line, the Grizzly Gully-Snake Dike, is just faster than the shortcut through the forest and going up cables.

Reese:    The honest answer is I don’t know. Maybe someone will get really motivated and beat it sooner rather than later. Maybe it’ll be another really long stretch. I tried to put enough of a difference where it’s gonna be a little bit hard.

Records are made to be broken. I’m not gonna be surprised if it’s broken one day. I don’t know when that’ll be. The thing is, it’ll probably have to be beaten by Grizzly Gully-Snake Dike, because it’s already been shown that some of the best trail runners can’t go under 1 hour 20 minutes by any other means running around. I think and hope it’ll have to be done by someone comfortable soloing snakes, like a runner-climber hybrid. Which makes Half Dome so special. The fastest way to the top is by climbing. It’s gotta be done by a climber.

Beyond the Iconic Potato Dish at New NYC Restaurant Somssi

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

There’s plenty of creative dishes coming out of this West Village kitchen

You’ve probably heard of the Potato Potato Potato dish at Somssi, the newest restaurant from Na:eun Hospitality. The triple-layered potato dish is the most labor-intensive dish on the menu but has also become the most popular order, with the kitchen going through up to 100 pounds of potatoes each week. The whimsical, caviar-topped dish really encapsulates what the restaurant is trying to achieve: “We should have serious food, serious hospitality, but we shouldn’t take ourselves too seriously,” says managing partner Ahris Kim. Here’s how the Somssi kitchen preps to create the newly famous potato and plenty of other dishes before a night of service.

Yukon gold potatoes are put through a ricer to become a cream and butter-heavy puree that is set in molds in the freezer. After being toasted in breadcrumbs, the logs of potato are wrapped in russet potato ribbons before being fried up to order.

Next is the ox tongue, which is brined for just over 16 hours before being slow-cooked in herbs, aromatics, and beef tallow for four hours. Huge cuts of tongue are carefully scored so they will get a great sear when they hit the grill. It’s served on a romesco sauce with just a bit of wasabi on the side.

Finally, pork loin is coated in ground shrimp for a creative schnitzel that is reminiscent of shrimp toast. The schnitzel is topped with a sweet-and-sour plum sauce that balances out the thick, crispy fried exterior. Scallop crudo is made up of pressed scallops surrounded with a strawberry sauce and topped off with gooseberries and peppers.

Watch the team at Somssi create creative dishes like a triple-potato bite and a show-stopping grilled tongue dish in this episode of Mise En Place.

‘Please Help Me.’ A Hiker’s Desperate Text Led to Her Dramatic Rescue on a Famed Colorado Trail

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

A Black Hawk helicopter. (Photo: patty_c / Getty Images)

A desperate text to 911 turned into a dramatic rescue in the Colorado high country after an early-season storm trapped a 37-year-old solo hiker in thigh-to-hip-deep snow on the Four Pass Loop on October 30.

The Four Pass Loop is a popular but rugged 27-mile route that travels through the Maroon Bells-Snowmass Wilderness while gaining about 8,000 feet of elevation. Most hikers tackle the route as a backpacking trip over the course of two to four days.

According to a release by Crested Butte Mountain Rescue (CBMR), the ordeal began when Pitkin County Emergency Dispatch received a text to 911 that read “please help me.” Before calling for help, the hiker had texted a family member that her phone’s battery was down to 10 percent. Shortly after that initial call for help, emergency services lost contact, leaving them with only a single coordinate to guide their search. With temperatures dipping into the teens, CBMR reached out to CareFlight of the Rockies to coordinate air support. The team also dispatched a ground team, reasoning the elevation and mountain conditions could make landing a helicopter impossible.

By 11 p.m., the helicopter team located the hiker attempting to climb over Frigid Air Pass, but they were unable to make a safe landing to extract her from the scene.

About four hours later, the first two party members of the CBMR ground team reached the pass and located the hiker struggling to make it over the pass near where the helicopter had spotted her. But neither air support nor ground support could reach the hiker: “The steep terrain, deep snow, and darkness presented legitimate avalanche concerns,” CBMR wrote. “The first two rescuers initially attempted to descend toward the subject but encountered concerning conditions. Recognizing the potential hazard, they made the decision to wait for the remaining team members before committing to the descent.”

When the remaining members of the CBMR ground team reached the pass at about 5:30 a.m., two team members descended somewhere between 300 to 400 feet to reach the hiker. Upon their arrival, they realized that her condition was potentially life-threatening since she was no longer ambulatory, she had cold-related injuries, and was showing signs of hypothermia. The rescue team placed the hiker in a bivy before realizing that the best way to extract her would be via a helicopter hoist rescue.

CBMR coordinated with the Colorado Search and Rescue Association (CSAR), who contacted the Colorado Army National Guard to dispatch a UH-60 Black Hawk helicopter from Gypsum. A helicopter lowered a rescue technician to the hiker, then hoisted both party members into the helicopter.

After the hiker was successfully transferred to the airport and flown to the UCHealth Burn and Frostbite Center in Aurora, the National Guard returned to the Frigid Air Pass to remove the remaining rescuers due to the pass’s dangerous conditions.

In the wake of the incident, CBMR reminded hikers to take extra precautions before heading into the backcountry since high-altitude rescues can be dangerous for rescuers, too. (“So throw that extra puffy in your pack!” the team wrote.)

The team noted that rescue came at the end of a particularly challenging week for CBMR, which responded to three incidents, two of them fatal.

→ Baca artikel asli

The JWST May Have Found Where Heavy Black Hole Seeds Formed

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Astrophysicists think that the supermassive black holes (SMBH) we see in the modern Universe are the result of a long drawn-out process. That process is referred to as the hierarchical black hole mergers framework. It explains how some SMBH can reach such staggering masses. Some of the most massive appear to have billions of solar masses.

The idea is that these SMBH start out as seeds, and those seeds are stellar-mass black holes. Two processes create them. One is when massive stars between about 8 and 50 solar masses reach the end of their lives of fusion. They either explode as supernovae, and their cores collapse, forming black holes, or they collapse directly into black holes. The other is when two neutron stars merge.

In between stellar mass black holes and SMBH are intermediate mass black holes (IMBH.) These black holes range in mass from about 100 solar masses to 100,000 solar masses. The observational evidence for IMBHs is still thin, but it's persistent.

A 2024 paper focused on Omega Centauri, a globular cluster on the outskirts of the Milky Way, as the site of an IMBH. A group of fast-moving stars in the center of Omega Centauri can only be explained by the presence of an IMBH with about 8,200 solar masses, according to that work.

Gravitational waves from a black hole merger are also evidence of an IMBH. A 2020 paper presented the only direct evidence of an IMBH when the waves showed that two black holes, with 85 and 66 solar masses, merged to create an IMBH with about 142 solar masses.

But what are the birthplaces of these IMBH? Some must have formed in the early Universe to act as seeds for the SMBH we see today.

Research titled "Massive star clusters detected by JWST as natural birth places to form intermediate-mass black holes" may have the answer. It's published in Astronomy and Astrophysics, and the lead author is Viola Bocchi. Bocchi is from the Sapienza University of Rome.

"The James Webb Space Telescope (JWST) has revealed a population of dense stellar systems at high redshifts, including the âCosmic Gemsâ arc (zââ¼â10.2) and the âFirefly Sparkleâ (zââ¼â8.3). With masses in the range 105 Mââ107 Mâ and half-mass radii in the range â¼0.4â15 pc, these systems are ideally suited to form intermediate-mass black holes (IMBHs) via collision-based models," the authors write.

The Cosmic Gems arc is a gravitationally lensed galaxy, and the JWST observed its light when the Universe was only about 460 million years old. The JWST found five dense star clusters there.

The Cosmic Gems was first discovered by the Hubble during its Reionization Lensing Cluster Survey (RELICS) and was identified as a powerful source of radiation during the Epoch of Reionization. In 2024, the JWST took another look and found the five Young Massive Clusters (YMC), the first discovery of such ancient clusters.

The Firefly Sparkle is another gravitationally lensed early galaxies, imaged only about 600 million years after the Big Bang. The JWST found 10 YMCs in the Firefly Sparkle.

This image shows the Firefly Sparkle galaxy, where the JWST found 10 YMCs. It also shows a pair of companion galaxies. Image Credit: NASA, ESA, CSA, STScI, Chris Willott (National Research Council Canada), Lamiya Mowla (Wellesley College), Kartheik Iyer (Columbia University)

This research shows that not only were all these YMCs sources of ionizing radiation, they may have been the birthplaces of IMBH.

Due to the density in these YMCs, n-body simulations weren't viable. Instead, the researchers used a model based on runaway collisions and mass-loss by stellar winds to estimate the resulting IMBH masses. They then validated these results against direct N-body simulations of compact star clusters.

"With masses in the range 105 Mââ107 Mâ and half-mass radii in the range â¼0.4â15 pc, these systems are ideally suited to form intermediate-mass black holes (IMBHs) via collision-based models," the authors write. "Our estimates yield typical IMBH masses in the range â¼102 Mâ to â¼4âÃâ103 Mâ, implying typical formation efficiencies on the few percent level."

The clusters in the Cosmic Gems yieled the heaviest IMBH, with masses ranging from 1600â2700 Mâ. "The extreme compactness of the Cosmic Gems clusters (Rhââ¼â1 pc) facilitates the formation of black hole seeds with high masses of 1600â2700 Mâ," the authors write. Rh â¼â1 pc is the radius of the sphere that contains half of the clusterâs total mass and measures how compact a cluster is.

"The low metallicity (Zââ²â0.02âZâ) is a critical factor for retaining the seed mass despite stellar winds," the authors write.

These young clusters also have low metallicities because not enough stars have lived and died in them to generate higher metallicity. Their low metallicity played a critical role in forming IMBH. Low metallicity limits mass-loss due to stellar winds, and that let the objects keep growing.

The results from the Firefly Sparkle are different. "In contrast to the Cosmic Gems, these clusters exhibit significantly larger radii (Rhââ¼â4âââ6 pc) for typically lower total masses (105âââ106âMâ)," the authors write. That creates cores that are less massive, limiting the potential for the runaway collision process to create IMBH. The black hole masses in Firefly Sparkle range 1.8âÃâ102âââ8.1âÃâ102âMâ. That's about 180 to 810 Mâ, which represents the lower boundary for IMBH masses.

This artist's illustration shows what the Firefly Sparkle galaxy looked like about 600 million years after the big bang if it wasnât stretched and distorted by gravitational lensing. This illustration is based on images and data from NASAâs James Webb Space Telescope. Image Credit: NASA, ESA, CSA, Ralf Crawford (STScI). Science: Lamiya Mowla (Wellesley College), Guillaume Desprez (Saint Mary's University)

Overall, the lower range for IMBH masses in this work (~100 solar mases) is comparable to black holes formed by massive Population III stars, the Universe's hypothetical first stars, which were likely extremely massive. But, "the upper bound extends significantly higher, allowing the most massive clusters to produce objects firmly in the âheavy seedâ regime," the authors write. "Forming such heavy seeds is highly beneficial to form SMBHs at high redshift."

These results aren't observational in the sense that the JWST didn't observe any IMBH. But they do show a plausible environment where IMBH could form and potentially lead to the SMBH we see in the centers of galaxies like ours.

These results could also explain the JWST's Little Red Dots. Their spectra are often interpreted as being powered by SMBH. "Our results suggest that dense massive star clusters could provide possible massive seeds from which the SMBHs in the LRDs may have grown," the researchers write.

The researchers state some caveats, based on some acknowledged weaknesses in their work. Many properties of these clusters are unknown, including their rotation and their internal structure, which could change the outcomes and lower the IMBH masses.

The initial mass function also isn't known, but it could be top-heavy. "This possibility, along with a possible primordial mass segregation, would favor the formation of massive IMBHs, though we did not explore it here in detail," the authors explain.

Researchers will no doubt dig deeper into these young massive clusters and try to fill in some of the blanks that remain. But this research shows that they could be the source of the heavy seed IMBH that eventually form SMBH.

"Our results suggest that these dense, metal-poor clusters are viable factories for heavy seeds that are capable of growing into the supermassive black holes observed in the early Universe," the authors conlude. .

Evan Gough is a science-loving guy with no formal education who loves Earth, forests, hiking, and heavy music. He's guided by Carl Sagan's quote: "Understanding is a kind of ecstasy."

Mortgage Rates Drop From September Highs as Midterms and Jobs Data Loom

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

In A Nutshell: Rates have fallen about 30–40 basis points since the peak at the end of September and there’s little reason to believe the volatility will slow down.

The Fed meeting last Wednesday was the highlight of the week, with the committee voting to hold rates steady as expected. There were two dissents, with one coming from a surprising source, Governor Lisa Cook, who is usually seen as someone who favors lower rates. The statement and press conference reiterated a desire to keep hiking, making a December hike more likely than not if the economic data comes in as expected.

The Fed has been highlighting elevated inflation and strong economic growth as its reason for hiking. And Q3 GDP, in fact, came in at a brisk 3.4% annual rate. Real business fixed investment spending grew at a 12.7% annual rate thanks to rapid growth in AI spending. And real consumption grew at a 3.8% annual rate as people spent out of savings and wealth increases rather than income growth. The question now is what happens to growth in Q4. Real-time data is pointing to a potential slowdown as consumers pull back.

It’s another big week with both the midterm election tomorrow and employment data arriving throughout the week. Democrats are expected to comfortably gain a majority in the House of Representatives with control of the Senate also favoring Democrats, but less certain. Expectations for government spending, which has been a factor for rates, could certainly change with a more divided government. But the near term economic impact might be felt most acutely as we near government funding and debt limit deadlines. The closest budget deadline is December 11, which might get pushed to Q1. And we’re likely to hit the debt limit again in Q1 at which point the Treasury will take “extraordinary measures” until we truly run out of options sometime before October 2027.

Since it’s the first week of the month, we’ll also get the typical employment reports, including job openings and turnover on Tuesday and the official jobs report on Friday. With the Fed squarely focused on inflation, the jobs report mostly serves as a check on whether the Fed needs to turn its attention back to recession risk, which would reduce the need for the rate hikes that markets have priced in. So far this year, the answer has been “no,” with the labor market on solid enough footing and that is not expected to change with this week’s data.

Finally, Fed officials are out of their blackout period following last week’s FOMC meeting. With Chairman Warsh typically reluctant to offer much forward guidance, markets have been relying more on surrogates to forecast the outcome of the next meeting. There are numerous speaking engagements on the calendar, including Cleveland Fed President Hammack and Dallas Fed President Logan on Thursday, and New York Fed President Williams on Friday. Forecasters will be looking for clues to support the “hike every other meeting” thesis.

Chen Zhao is the head of economics research, where she produces research on the housing market for public and internal audiences. Previously, she was an executive director leading housing finance and financial markets research at the JPMorgan Chase Institute. Prior to joining JPMCI, Chen was an economics consultant at Analysis Group, Inc., where she worked on financial litigation cases and led teams conducting health economics and outcomes research on behalf of pharmaceutical companies. While in graduate school, Chen was with the Center for Economic Studies and the Social Economic and Housing Statistics Division at the US Census Bureau, where she conducted applied microeconomics research using large scale restricted-access linked survey-administrative data. She started her career at the White House Council of Economic Advisers, where she focused on labor and health economics.

Matt Robertson Wins $1.25 Million in the Inaugural WBE The Champions Bass Tournament

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Matt Robertson overcame a gauntlet of obstacles this past week to win the inaugural WBE The Champions tournament and take home $1.25 million—the biggest prize in professional bass fishing history. He bested 61 other anglers, outmaneuvering and out-fishing them in what turned into a chess-vs.-checkers matchup. Early on, Robertson chose to fish below Old Hickory Dam, and he stuck with that game plan for the duration of the tournament. He caught only two non-keepers below the dam during practice, but knew enough to gamble his entire tournament on that small stretch of water. On the final day, the gutsy call paid off again with a 20-pound, 2-ounce bag, enough to take home the top slot and $1.25 million.

One of Robertson’s biggest challenges throughout the tournament was navigating the lock connecting Old Hickory Lake and the Cumberland River. Even Robertson said he was shocked he could lock down and back all three days without issue. Normally, filling the lock with water to raise a boat to the same level as Old Hickory takes about 30 minutes. Locking down goes a little quicker. All in all, it could take about an hour for the lock to complete one full rotation. And that’s just with a small boat or two. With a barge, it could take much longer. Surprisingly, Robertson locked up and down all six times throughout the event, maximizing his fishing time at his premier spot.

Below the dam, Robertson targeted smallmouth, which must be 18 inches to keep, compared to the 12-inch spotted bass and 14-inch largemouth regulations. In three days of fishing, not a single spot or largemouth was caught below the dam. If you were going to get a bite there, it would be a smallmouth. That meant it had to be a dang good one to keep—and Robertson took advantage of his opportunities. His Day 1 bag (20 pounds even) and Day 3 Bag (20 pounds, 2 ounces) were the two biggest bags of the tournament by far.

The current below Old Hickory Dam was the magic sauce. It positioned the fish on cover and got them to bite. Robertson was fortunate to have moving water below the dam each day, which isn’t always a given. The current shifted dramatically day to day as different turbines fired at different times, but Robertson adjusted and still caught big bronzebacks.

For the armchair quarterback, it probably looked like a no-brainer to fish below the dam this week. But there was a lot to consider, and a half dozen things could have easily gone wrong and ruined Robertson’s run. But the Kentucky native defied the odds, avoided the catastrophes, and came out on top. He adjusted when the current shifted; he waited patiently to lock; he was efficient when he got bit. All of that added up to a handful of quality fish whose tails fell on the right side of the line. The end result was Robertson collecting the biggest purse in bass fishing history. And he did it in dramatic fashion.

Shaye Baker is a bass fishing content contributor for Field & Stream. He has more than a decade of experience covering professional bass fishing tournaments. Highlights Education Baker graduated from Auburn University with a bachelor’s degree in Spanish and International Trade. However, the education he nows relies on in his daily occupation came by way of working alongside many of the greatest tournament coverage professionals the industry has ever seen, learning to shoot, write, edit, and add commentary in the real world. Experience Baker spent his first few years after college simultaneously pursuing a professional bass fishing career, as well as a career in outdoor journalism in the bass fishing space. After mixed success on the semi-pro fishing trail, Baker decided that working in the industry for a guaranteed paycheck was better than fishing for a possible one. Baker’s clientele base gradually grew to the point he could transition to full-time content contribution for the likes of FLW Outoors, Bassmaster, Wired2Fis,h and several other local, regional, and national publications. He now enjoys working full-time in the industry, but has also returned to his roots, regularly participating in local tournaments with his father and friends. Fun Fact Though Baker grew up in (and loves) Reeltown, Alabama, the epitome of small town USA, he is (somewhat) fluent in Spanish. He has also spent time in Ecuador, Peru, Spain, Italy, Portugal, and France, and has been privileged to see over half of the United States thanks to his career. Notable Work

Review: Emirates 777-300ER “Game Changer” First Class Dubai To Brussels

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Seven years after my first flight in Emirates’ fully enclosed Boeing 777 first class suite, I returned to the same route and same flight, EK183, from Dubai to Brussels. The “Game Changer” suite remains extraordinary, but this flight demonstrated that even a superb seat cannot compensate for disappointing service, though perhaps my exceptions were too high? My latest review follows, with the original 2019 review preserved below for comparison.

I first reviewed this product in 2019 and came away impressed. Returning in 2026, I was curious whether it would feel quite as special after experiencing more of the world’s leading first class products. The suite certainly did. The experience around it was less consistent, especially compared with my excellent Emirates A380 flight from Mumbai to Dubai.

As I explained in the introduction to this trip report, I booked Mumbai–Dubai–Brussels for 102,000 Emirates Skywards miles and approximately $700 in taxes and fees. That included my A380 flight the day before and this flight to Brussels on a Boeing 777-300ER featuring Emirates’ “Game Changer” first class suites.

I took an Uber from my hotel to DXB. Emirates has a dedicated terminal for business and first class check-in and within area, there’s a posh first class check-in lobby.

However, with so many first class passengers, there is not the sort of personalized ground service you might experience with Air France or Lufthansa. After checking in, I was on my own to get through security, passport control, and to the lounge.

I visited both the Concourse A and Concourse B Emirates First Class lounges before my flight.

I reached the gate (further than I thought) about 30 minutes prior to scheduled departure and the flight had fully boarded, with a “final call” announcement from the gate agent and on the monitors.

I settled in onboard, noticing the cabin was booked full. What war?

The first class cabin has six suites in a 1-1-1 configuration with the middle suites having virtual windows (with a live feed of the world outside).

I’ll post a video review of this flight at a later point, but I hope from words and pictures and you can appreciate how unique this first class compartment is in terms of privacy…it’s quite a revolution from the older product on the A380 and 777 (as nice as those products are).

My suite, 1K, included a large table, screen, closet, cabinet for hand luggage, and an oversized, plush “dentist” chair. Sounds simple enough, but the accents were tremendous and I loved that Emirates’ first class cabin has ditched most (but not all) of the gold bling for a more refined look…it’s just so odd that after all these years Emirates has only retrofitted nine jets with this product.

There’s also a flip-up cabinet with a mirror that houses the amenity kit skin products and a cool souvenir notebook and pen.

The suite has universal A/C, USB-A, and USB-C power (plus HDMI for those still living in 2012).

There are also plenty of lighting options.

Within the suite, you can customize your experience not only via adjusting the seat from upright to reclined to fully-flat, but you can adjust the temperature and lighting (even the color of the lighting).

I didn’t need to sleep on this daytime flight to Brussels, but I ended up taking a 4-hour nap: the bedding is superb and the 79-inch bed very comfortable in 180º lie-flat mode.

In terms of the hard product alone, this seats beats out the JAL A350-1000 suite. But of course a flight is about more than the seat, especially in first class….

Emirates’ “ICE” entertainment system includes hundreds of movies, TV shows, live TV, music, and games…more than enough for a journey around the sun, let alone a quick trip to Europe.

Noise cancelling headphones are from Bowers & Wilkins.

While this flight did not have Starlink Wi-Fi, the OnAir internet was complimentary and worked reasonably well, even if it felt more like dial-up than high speed.

It was also a lovely day just to look out the window, especially as we neared Brussels.

Upon boarding, I was offered a refreshing glass of Champagne and chose Dom Rosé 2010.

Menus were distributed prior to pushback, with a la carte breakfast and lunch choices available throughout the flight. along with an exceptional collection of wine and spirits plus a “movie snack bar” menu.

I thought the breakfast choices were limited. My omelet was fine, but I’ve had much better omelets on other carriers and even had a better one out of Mumbai on Emirates the day before.

Service began with fresh fruit, a cappuccino, orange juice (with ice cubes?), rhubarb yogurt, and delicious bakery items like a cinnamon bun and almond croissant.

The omelet had beans, potatoes, and cooked spinach with it…utterly forgettable.

After waking up from extended nap, it was time for the main feast. While my meal was being prepared, I was offered mix nuts and hydrated with some sparkling water.

The caviar course was excellent and I had more Dom Pérignon Rosé with it.

Next came fabulous Arabic mezze with humus, grape leaves, tabbouleh, falafel, kibbeh, and fattoush salad..everything was great.

With time running out, I skipped the soup and went right for my main course: pan-fried lamb loin and cutlet, served with olive caramel sauce, tomato fondue, beetroot jus, potato pavé with pistachio dukkah-crusted kale, and blanched sugar snap peas.

Sadly, it was so overdone that I did not finish it. However, I did enjoy some excellent garlic bread and red wine (a Bibi Graetz Colore 2015 from Tuscany) with it.

Lunch ended with a cheese course and chocolate truffle cake (with roasted hazelnuts and pistachios). The cheese included:

The “new” 777 has two lavatories in the front of the cabin, shared by crew. They are spacious, though not like upstairs on the A380:

I was never proactively offered an amenity kit. When we landed, I asked about it and was told, “They are on request only.” So be aware of that…

Slippers and pajamas (what Emirates class loungewear) was proactively offered. I took size medium, which fit me (I’m 6’1″) and was very comfortable.

As I mentioned above, skin products from Byredo were present in the suite.

I’ll repeat what I wrote in my first impressions of this flight.

After breakfast, my dishes sat for over 20 minute untouched. Not the service I was expecting.

I woke up a couple hours before landing for the meal and was totally ignored for almost 30 minutes (and my suite door was open). I realize that I could have just pressed the call button (and eventually did), but I don’t think that should be necessary in first class…I think that the FAs should have been far more proactive in monitoring the cabins.

When I did call the FAs to begin meal service, there was 90 minutes left in the flight, which in theory should have been time for everything. I provided the full order and the caviar came quick, but once again the crew was not attentive. I finished the caviar and waited. Finally, the mezze came and I finished it…and waited. I waited and waited for the main course and it came (having been left in the oven far too long).

I waited again. The captain announced our descent into Brussels. My glass was empty, my plate was pushed way, but no one was coming.

Do I really have to push the call button after I finish each course? That’s not the kind of first class service I like.

With 40 minutes left in the flight, I finally flagged a FA down and asked her to bring the cheese and dessert at once. She seemed very agitated, not in the hostile sense, but because she was so stressed…because it wasn’t only me trying to finish my lunch, but the other four in the cabin (it was booked 5/6).

She brought over the cheese and dessert and I ate it…it was fine…but I felt too guilty ordering coffee with so little time left…again, not a feeling I should have in first class. Drink refills were not offered.

The pre-arrival service could have been so much better executed. The service flow was so perfect on my A380 flight because the flight attendants took charge and proactively executed service. Not so on this flight and it wasn’t because the FAs were too busy: they were chatting in the galley.

So was it was the best flight ever? Nope. The A380 flight the day before was perfect…this one was not.

I think it’s always appropriate to mention here that I am incredibly grateful to fly first class on Emirates and this sort of “first world problem” is not meant to condemn Emirates or gripe so much as it is to offer an honest comparison between this and other premium cabin products. Comparatively speaking, Emirates fell short here.

Despite the questionable service, what a pleasure it was to fly on this product and enjoy the beautiful suite and multi-course meals. I look forward to doing this again. Next time, though, I think I’ll skip breakfast and just have one meal in the middle of the flight.

The review below was originally published on June 7, 2019, and covers my earlier flight from Dubai to Brussels.

I booked my ticket from Dubai to Brussels a couple night before the trip using Emirates Skywards. The points transfer on a 1:1 basis from American Express Membership Rewards and the trip cost 85,000 miles and about $420 in taxes/fees. The same trip would have been 75,000 miles with Qantas, which has now been added as an American Express transfer partner.

I began the day with a 4:00 A.M. cab ride to Dubai International Airport from the Park Hyatt. Although I tired to sleep, I was too excited for the upcoming journey. Pulling up to the Emirates premium class check-in area Dubai, a line of uniformed staff were waiting to assist with luggage, but today I was traveling only with hand baggage.

Inside, I  found myself the only passenger in a huge terminal…it felt a bit surreal. After checking in, I proceeded down a long hallway toward the security checkpoint and passport control.

After a couple hours in the first class lounge, I headed to the gate for boarding, which began ahead of schedule about an hour prior to takeoff.

B1 is used for aircraft departing from remote stands and passengers were being funneled down a single escalator and onto a waiting bus. I tried to board but was told to wait and that first class boarding would begin “soon”. So I waited. And waited. And waited. 25 minutes passed. I was getting antsy. So were the three other first class passengers. We were the only ones left in the gate area.

Finally, we were invited to head downstairs where our “first class” bus was awaiting. I don’t know if “first class” was the right word, but the bus was very comfortable. We soon began what turned out to be a 15-minute bus ride to the aircraft…which seemed to be parked in Abu Dhabi (I jest…). Upon reaching the aircraft, we were invited to carry our own bags up the fairly steep air stairs onto the 777-300ER that would shortly be departing for Brussels.

Onboard, three FAs were waiting at the door. After a warm welcome, one escorted me to my suite.

I was grinning ear-to-ear as I stepped into the beautiful six-seat cabin. Wow, this was spacious! The cabin is arranged in a 1-1-1 configuration with four window/aisle suites and two suites in the middle, one with an entrance on aisle one and the other with an entrance on aisle two.

My suite, 2K, included a large table, screen, closet, cabinet for hand luggage, and an oversized, plush chair. Sounds simple enough, but the accents were tremendous and I loved that Emirates’ latest first class cabin has ditched most (but not all) of the gold bling for a more refined look.

What I appreciated most about this new cabin was the attention to detail Emirates placed in making it practical. For example, my rollerboard carry-on fit perfectly in the cabinet, which was so much more convenient and aesthetically pleasing than having to leave it under the ottoman (like in Singapore or Etihad First Class) or store it in a closet (Lufthansa First Class) or overhead bin (Air France First Class).

There was also a closet that easily accommodated my garment bag and clothes on a hangar and smaller storage cabinets along the window.

A power port and USB charger was conveniently located within arm’s reach, as was a USB-C charger (which my laptop has…).

Adjacent to my seat was a touch panel that allowed for control of the lighting (including customizable mood lighting) and the temperature of the suite. The armrest also had a four-button seat control.

To the left and right of the IFE screen were cabinets housing snacks and drinks, sort of like a glass cabinet in a kitchen. There was also a privacy divider which could be raised to fully enclose the cabin. Notice some of the “leaves” of tree look different. Those provide the only means by which flight attendants can check on you when your suite is closed.

In addition the drink cabinet, there was a basket with snacks sitting on the table along with beautiful orchids:

But the attention to detail went even further. The careful stitching on the seat and embroidered Ghaf (prosopis cineraria) tree above the window further showed how much thought Emirates put into this cabin. And the overhead lights were shaped like diamonds.

Each suite had binoculars to look out. These were not souvenirs; FAs collected them as we approached Brussels.

The suite across the aisle from me stayed vacant, so I had a chance to check out the “fake” windows”. Kudos to Emirates for trying, but I was not impressed.

I structured the flight around a nap…not because I wanted to sleep through a pricey flight, but because I slept poorly the previous two nights and wanted to try out the bed and bedding.

Thus, shortly after my post-takeoff breakfast flight attendants prepared the bed for me while I changed into my pajamas. The bedding is simply tremendous, with a satin-like finish that was soft and so comfortable.

Window shades lower by button (sheer blinds and blackout) but Emirates also added real curtains. While I figured these were purely for aesthetic reasons, light seeped through one of my three windows so it turns out the curtains were quite practical.

The only annoying thing was that I could not figure out how to shut off the small band of blue light overhead:

I slept for about 3.5 hours, asking the FAs to shake me awake with about two hours to go until landing.

Prior to pushback, I was offered a drink of choice and chose orange juice, which was freshly-squeezed. Later on in the boarding process, I was also offered Arabic coffee and a choice of dates.

After takeoff, I examined the menu for today’s flight:

Having had steak for “breakfast” I was actually in the mood for some eggs, but only a light continental breakfast was offered. No problem…I wasn’t all that hungry. The purser, Ashwin, came by to introduce himself and took my breakfast order. I just wanted some fruit and yogurt along with a cappuccino. Almost immediately, my table was prepared by Tetiana and I was offered more orange juice along with a basket of warmed breakfast breads.

The table folds out from the console in front:

Mixed nuts were also offered and I enjoyed a glass of Dom Pérignon 2008 Champagne.

I do try to stay away from these fatty carbs, but the croissants smelled so good I ended up eating two of them…and some danish. So much for my light breakfast…

The fruit, yogurt, and cappuccino (notice the chocolate powder stencil art is the Emirates logo?) were all tasty and the whole breakfast service took only about 20 minutes. I still had plenty of time for sleep and a big feast prior to landing.

I was presented with a hot towel after my breakfast dishes were cleared.

After my nap, Tetiana again set up my tray table for lunch.

Hot bread from the oven and my favorite Isigny Sainte-Mère butter from France were offered.

I started the meal off with some more Dom, though this time some Dom Pérignon Rose 2006. It was served at the perfect temperature and was a spectacular accompaniment to the meal which would follow.

I began with caviar, admittedly more so for the trip report than because I felt like it. Still, I ate it and enjoyed it. I appreciated that Emirates warmed the blinis.

While I was eating my soup, Maria brought over my next course, a green salad with French dressing.

For my main course, I ordered a beef filet with asparagus and boiled potatoes. The dish was beautifully presented and the asparagus with truffle hollandaise sauce was spectacular…even better than Lufthansa First Class.

But the beef filet? Not so much…it was served beyond well-done and I ended up only nibbling at it. Such a shame that it was served overcooked. Tetiana was concerned that I did not eat and offered to bring me something else, but by this point I was already full and there were still two courses left.

As you can read in the menu above, the cheese course was quite extensive. I had a sample of each piece, but could not finish it.

Finally, I completed my meal with warm chocolate cake with raspberry sauce and another cappuccino.

Kudos to Emirates for an excellent dining experience in first class.

Emirates does not offer amenity kits on daytime flights. That said, there were several toiletries available in the lavatory and a box at each seat contained Byredo (Swedish) amenities like eye cream and skin toner. The amenities were “hidden” away in a cabinet along the table that folded up to reveal a mirror with lights. A leather-bound notebook and writing pen were also present.

Pajamas were available by request. Emirates uses “breathing” pajamas that are supposed to help the body stay ventilated. While that may just be a gimmick , I loved the soft pajamas and they have become my new favorite airline pajamas…my old favorites were also from Emirates.

Finally, the slippers were also quite comfortable and also large enough for my feet.

Ok, this is what surprised me the most. I knew the flight would have wireless internet, but like all (and I do mean ALL) my previous Emirates flights, I just assumed it would not work. Boy was I wrong. Not only was the internet functional and free (as a Skywards member), it was FAST!

Make sure your Skywards number is associated with your reservation in order to take advantage of the complimentary wi-fi.

On the other hand, the IFE was no surprise. I knew Emirates would offer a comprehensive library of movies, TV shows, live TV, games, and music and of course it did. There were even two cameras to allow for live views from outside.

What did impress me, however, was the wireless remote in the suite and the detachable tablet that served as a secondary IFE device, especially suited for games and music. When detached, it was totally wireless and also controlled the seat, could summon FAs via video conference for service, and easily controlled my customized soundtracks.

Newspapers were offered prior to takeoff.

Lastly, the Bowers & Wilkins noise-cancelling headphones were comfortable and worked very well.
