The only problem with this flight was that it ended so quickly. 6.5 hours was not enough time to fully enjoy the wonderful hard and soft product onboard. I was simply ecstatic throughout the flight…Emirates takes first class to a whole new level.

While the old Emirates suite is still very nice, the new suite is even more spacious, even more comfortable, and even more cutting-edge.

Emirates has room for improvement with its first class ground product, but its in-air product is top-notch.

Everest and Ama Dablam Climbs Resume Despite Cyclone

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

The second cyclone to hit Nepal’s mountains this season caused heavy snowfall last weekend, but its briefer stay has affected climbers and trekkers less. As the sun returned this afternoon, fresh snow hampered smaller teams on 6,000m and 7,000m peaks, but the highly supported expeditions on Ama Dablam are proceeding with barely a hitch.

No cyclone seems enough to deter the Russian team on Everest for very long. Leader Andrei Vasiliev told us yesterday from Kathmandu that he, Sergey Kondrashkin, and Vitaly Shipilov will return to the mountain in one or two days. The trio is well aware of the challenge they face on Everest’s West Ridge, completely alone and without oxygen.

The climbers’ latest foray up Everest’s West Ridge route. Photo: Andrei Vasiliev

Vasiliev mentioned he is under some pressure due to unidentified “unsolved matters” but still determined to tackle the climb. In his own words:

We know what needs to be done; the path is clear, and we must attempt to follow it. We’ve fallen ill. A massive amount of snow has fallen. You adapt quickly to new conditions. Once we’re back in the snow, the cold, and the cramped tent, it will feel as though things were never any other way.

The new snow dump has thwarted some teams attempting Baruntse (7,129m) in the Khumbu. Jan McManus of Ireland was among them, and he has called off his expedition after failing to reach Camp 1 due to deep snow:

It is unclear whether other climbers on the peak may still be trying. There were six large teams totaling 65 foreign climbers with permits for Baruntse.

On Himlung (7,126m), teams hurried to summit and get down and safely away before the cyclone hit. During the previous snowstorm at the end of September, a massive avalanche buried Base Camp, killing 16 staff members. Despite the tragedy, one of the deadliest in Nepal’s mountaineering history, the peak remained open, and guided teams came in large numbers to the mountain a couple of weeks after the accident. A total of 130 foreigners had permits. Teams summited on October 25 and 26, and then left the area.

Ama Dablam Base Camp is also covered in snow, but the commercial teams will nevertheless resume climbing soon. According to Jana Schneider of Germany, climbing with 14 Peaks Expeditions, a Sherpa team is on its way to reopen those sections where fresh snow has buried the ropes. “If all goes well, my summit push begins Nov 4 until Nov 6,” she wrote.

So far, 410 foreigners have applied for a permit to climb Ama Dablam. A Sherpa team first fixed the route on October 17, and dozens have summited since then. Despite the break forced by bad weather over the weekend, summits should continue throughout November.

One climber has perished on the mountain so far, and there have been some close calls. Paul Clarke of Australia and Xenia Santha of Hungary climbed Ama Dablam after attempting Dhaulagiri earlier this season. Their summit push was delayed as they found a 25-year-old climber in a serious situation at Camp 2. Santha and Clarke, who climbed without Sherpa support, gave the climber dexamethasone, spent the night with him, and helped organize an emergency airlift for the following morning. They continued to the summit one day later.

A high camp on Ama Dablam. Photo: Xenia Shanta

Angela Benavides graduated university in journalism and specializes in high-altitude mountaineering and expedition news. She has been writing about climbing and mountaineering, adventure and outdoor sports for 20+ years.

Prior to that, Angela Benavides spent time at/worked at a number of local and international media. She is also experienced in outdoor-sport consultancy for sponsoring corporations, press manager and communication executive, and a published author.

Ask Dr. Paola – HELP! Should I Use An Allergen-Neutralizing Spray? (November 2, 2026)

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Catster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission at no cost to you. Learn more.

Welcome to our "Ask Dr. Paola" series, where every other Monday we bring expert advice straight from Dr. Paola Cuevas (MVZ) to help our readers better understand their cat's health and well-being. Whether you're a new pet parent or a seasoned cat lover, Dr. Paola is here to provide answers to your most pressing questions. From nutrition tips and preventive care to troubleshooting common behavioral issues, Dr. Paola is ready to offer insights that will keep your kitty happy, healthy, and feline fine. Stay tuned for expert guidance on a range of topics that matter most to you and your cat, so you can make informed decisions and provide the best possible care for your furry companion. â

My 1-year-old cat Daisy has allergies. She is on a special hydrolyzed diet. Iâm trying to find an allergen-neutralizing spray to cut back on dust mites in the house. Would you recommend any specific products?

Since Daisy is already on a hydrolyzed diet, it makes sense to look at environmental allergens too, especially if her allergic signs are continuing. Dust mites are common household allergens, and while the goal is to reduce the amount of allergens in her environment, eliminating them completely is not realistic. Regularly washing bedding and blankets, vacuuming with a HEPA filter, and keeping dust to a minimum are still the best places to start.

If you would like to try a spray, Purefy Anti-Allergen Spray works differently from an insecticide. It is intended to neutralize or remove the allergenic proteins associated with dust mite debris, particularly the proteins found in their feces and other microscopic particles that accumulate in household dust and fabrics. Those proteins, rather than the living mite itself, are an important source of the allergic reaction. So the idea is not to kill every mite in the house, but to reduce the allergen they leave behind.

I would specifically avoid routinely using insecticide or acaricide sprays around Daisy, even when a product is marketed as ânatural.â Cats can be particularly sensitive to certain botanical ingredients and insecticides, including some essential oils and pyrethroids. If you use an anti-allergen spray, follow the label carefully, keep Daisy away from treated surfaces until they are completely dry, ventilate the area well, and never spray it directly on her or on surfaces she is likely to lick. Please ensure you also keep it away from food and water bowls.

Think of the spray as an extra layer of environmental control, not a treatment for Daisy's allergy. The unglamorous combination of washing, HEPA vacuuming, and reducing dust is still doing most of the heavy lifting. And if Daisy continues to have significant allergic signs despite a properly conducted hydrolyzed diet trial and environmental management, it is worth investigating other possible triggers rather than simply reaching for stronger household chemicals. Usually, the answer is not a more powerful spray, but figuring out exactly what is making your cat itchy. So, real detective work is often needed for success.

He used to live with two wonderful cats before embarking on his travels around the world. Radim currently lives in Slovakia, surrounded by nature and his neighbors' cat community.

Ask Dr. Paola - HELP! What Happens If Cherry Eye Is Left Untreated? (November 2, 2026)

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

We just sent a welcome email your way with a quick overview of what to expect (including those vital recall alerts).

Dogster is reader-supported. When you buy via links on our site, we may earn a commission at no cost to you. Read about privacy.

Welcome to our "Ask Dr. Paola" series, where every other Monday we bring expert advice straight from Dr. Paola Cuevas (MVZ) to help our readers better understand their dog's health and well-being.

Whether you're a new pet parent or a seasoned dog lover, Dr. Paola is here to provide answers to your most pressing questions. From nutrition tips and preventive care to troubleshooting common behavioral issues, Dr. Paola is ready to offer insights that will keep your furry friend happy and healthy. Stay tuned for expert guidance on a range of topics that matter most to you and your dog, so you can make informed decisions and provide the best possible care for your canine companion.

âHaveâ a âquestion? Send it in here!

My dog, Reggie, has Cherry eye. If left untreated, can it have a negative effect on his vision? Money is an issue for me right now.â

Cherry eye happens when the gland of the third eyelid, which helps produce tears, slips out of its normal position and becomes visible as a pink or red cherry-looking bulge in the corner of your dog's eye. I understand that veterinary expenses can be difficult to manage, and it is important to know what needs attention right away and what can safely wait a bit longer.

Cherry eye does not usually cause blindness directly, but leaving it untreated can put Reggie's eye health at risk. The exposed gland can become irritated, inflamed, or damaged, and its tear production may be reduced over time. Because this gland contributes to keeping the eye moist and protected, reduced tear production can increase the risk of dry eye, a condition that can lead to corneal damage, scarring, and impaired vision if it becomes severe. Think of the gland as part of the eye's built-in lubrication system. Even if the eye looks comfortable today, problems can develop gradually.

If surgery is not financially possible right now, please arrange a veterinary examination to confirm the diagnosis, assess tear production, and check the cornea.

Talk to your vet about a payment plan, or you might be able to find a veterinary teaching hospital or nonprofit clinic that may offer lower-cost care. Your veterinarian may recommend a suitable lubricating eye product while you work toward surgery, but lubrication will not reposition the gland. Please make sure you avoid human eye drops, leftover medications, or steroid eye drops unless specifically prescribed, as some of these can worsen an undiagnosed corneal ulcer. The gland should generally be repositioned and preserved rather than removed. If the gland is removed, you would need to use eye drops to supplement Reggieâs tear production for the rest of his life.

Please seek same-day veterinary care if you see Reggie squinting, holding the eye closed, rubbing it persistently, developing a cloudy or bluish cornea, or producing thick yellow or green discharge. These can be signs of a painful or more serious eye problem. If the eye is comfortable and clear, it may not be an immediate emergency, but I would not leave cherry eye unassessed for weeks or months. The most important first step is protecting the eye while finding an affordable treatment option.

Does Breathing Deeply Feel Hard? Yoga Can Help.

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

In Yoga Journal’s Archives series, we share a curated collection of articles originally published in past issues beginning in 1975. This article about deep breathing first appeared in the March-April 2004 issue of Yoga Journal. You can find more of our Archives here.

With each breath you take, a symphony of muscular activity occurs. As you inhale, some muscles contract while others relax. The result is an expanded space for the lungs, and thus for the breath. As you exhale, the space you need to breathe shrinks—again, through a complex set of muscular actions. For smooth and effective breathing to take place, the muscles involved in the breathing deeply must be fine-tuned and coordinated, just like the instruments of an orchestra.

The lungs don’t have the power to expand and contract on their own: They are inflated by air pressure to the size of their container, the rib cage. The rib cage in turn is expanded or compressed by the surrounding muscles. The breath orchestra includes many different muscles in the chest, back, neck, and abdomen, but none are as important as the diaphragm, which plays a primary role in expanding the breathing space during inhalation and helping control the shrinkage during exhalation.

You can visualize the diaphragm as half a basketball that attaches all the way around the lower ribs. When the diaphragm relaxes, it arches up toward the heart. When it contracts, it becomes flatter, pushing down toward the abdomen. Most of the sensation you feel when you inhale comes from the outward expansion of the belly and lower ribs, a movement created by the contraction of the diaphragm. This contraction lengthens the space available for the lungs and expands the rib cage to the front, the back, and the sides, causing air to be drawn into the lungs. When you exhale, the reverse happens: The diaphragm relaxes, arching up again; your belly becomes flatter; and the rib cage shrinks, pushing the air back out.

Anything that restricts the movement of the diaphragm restricts the breath, especially limiting the ability to inhale fully. There are many conditions that can constrict the diaphragm, including wearing overly tight clothes or a belt cinched too snugly around the waist. Another common cause is slumped posture. Sit up tall and notice how the lower ribs lift up off the abdomen and the adjacent upper belly becomes spacious. Now slump forward, letting your spine round back and your head jut forward. In this position, you’ll feel the lower ribs dig back and in toward the spine, compressing the diaphragm and the upper belly.

Retaining the space your diaphragm needs to move freely is only one of the many reasons it’s a good idea to work on your everyday posture; it’s also a good reason to be conscious of your spinal alignment in seated forward bends. If you work too aggressively to get your head toward the floor in these poses, your spine will round backward and your diaphragm will become compressed.

The movement of the diaphragm can also be restricted by tightness in the abdominal muscles. Pain or fear can lead to gripping in this area; combine that with holding your breath—another common habitual response to anxiety—and you have a recipe for an all-but-immobilized diaphragm. The abdominals can also become overly tight and short through too much focus on abdominal strengthening exercises like curl-ups and crunches.

Each time you curl your head and chest off the floor, you recreate a slumped position and train the abdominals to pull the lower ribs down toward the navel and back toward the spine. If the abs are never given a counterbalancing stretch, they will constantly hold the ribs in that constricted position.

Now don’t get me wrong. I absolutely believe that the ab muscles must be kept strong and conditioned. Their strength is important in supporting the abdominal organs and lower back. It’s also needed to compress the abdomen, assisting in exhalation, and for such forceful exhalations as coughing, sneezing, and fast breathing during exercise.

So the abdominal muscles need to be strong enough to perform their jobs, but they should not be so short, tight, and rigid that they restrict lung expansion and limit inhalation. The upper abdominals, which are strengthened during curl-ups and crunches, are the guilty gang that can put the squeeze on the diaphragm. These upper abdominals include the upper fibers of the rectus abdominus, which sits lengthwise in the center of the abdomen, running between the pubic bone below and the lower sternum and the rib cartilage above. The external and internal obliques, which cross the abdomen diagonally like a supporting girdle, also have upper fibers that attach to the lower ribs and can restrict rib cage movement.

To return to the musical analogy, rigid and short abdominals can overpower the rest of the breath orchestra and limit the contribution that other muscles—especially the diaphragm —can make to the symphony of the breath. Besides disturbing the proper balance between the different muscles involved in breathing deeply, and thus disturbing the breath’s rhythm, tight upper abdominals can contribute to chronic neck pain and headaches. When the expansion of the lower lung space is restricted, we tend to compensate by over-expanding the upper lung space. In this situation, the muscles that lift the rib cage and expand the space between the upper ribs—including the sternocleidomastoid muscles (on the front of the neck) and the upper trapezius muscles (on the back of the neck)—have to do much more than their usual share of the work during inhalation. Neck strain and chronic neck tension are often the result. And the most common cause of headaches is neck tension, especially the excessive pull on the base of the skull where the upper trapezius attaches.

To keep your obliques and rectus abdominus in optimal working order, you must train them to relax and lengthen as well as to contract and shorten. You can begin this process simply by taking a few moments after abdominal strengthening exercises or poses to invite the abs to relax.

Lying on your back, place your hands on your belly, with your fingers spreading out below your navel. When you inhale, your diaphragm will contract and push down on your belly, which will expand under your hands. This upward and outward expansion indicates that the abdominals have released and are lengthening

The abdominal muscles will lengthen even more if you recline over a bolster.

With your hips on the floor, lie back on the bolster so your torso and head are supported, your arms are relaxed out to the sides, and your chest is open. If you position the end of the bolster just at the bottom of your ribs, you’ll lengthen the upper abdominals and create space for the diaphragm. Now take a few minutes to breathe slowly and steadily, feeling the expansion of each inhalation as it gets drawn down into the lower rib cage and upper belly

Along with this exercise, many backbends can help stretch the abs, including Camel Pose (Ustrasana), Upward-Facing Bow Pose (Urdhva Dhanurasana), and Bridge Pose (Setu Bandha Sarvangasana).

If your diaphragm has been limited for years by snug waistbands, tight ab muscles, slumped posture, or habitual breath-holding, it may be too out of condition to contribute much to the breathing process. You can strengthen the diaphragm by providing some resistance to its work. Lying on your back, place some weight on your belly.

The appropriate weight will vary according to your size and ability, from one to two pounds (perhaps a bag of rice) to eight to 10 pounds (a yoga sandbag) or even more. But it should always be something that is soft and can spread, so it will sit comfortably in place. As your belly expands each time you inhale, the diaphragm will have to work harder to lift the extra weight.

Breathe slowly for a few minutes, working for a smooth breath pattern.

With practice, you can create an appropriate balance in the muscles of your belly—a balance that allows them to relax as your diaphragm contracts during inhalation yet keeps them strong enough to support the lower back and assist in exhalation. As your awareness of the diaphragm increases, you may find yourself opting for looser clothes and for seated and standing positions that don’t compress your belly. You may also begin to notice when you respond to stress, anger, and other emotions by holding your breath and tightening your abdomen—and realize that your tension melts away when you release your grip, allowing your diaphragm to move and your breath to flow. After all, the smooth, beautiful music of the breath is not just an important focus in yoga and meditation; it can help you stay centered and calm even as the storms of life rage around you.

Ryanair Weighs Fare Hikes, Pay Freeze As Fuel Prices Soar

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Irish discount carrier Ryanair is bracing for a difficult 2027 as jet fuel prices rise and European oil hedges expire.

In its most recent earnings report, the airline said it is considering temporarily freezing pay for workers, lowering crew ratios, and ramping up use of artificial intelligence. The company did not specify how long it would look to freeze wages, or how it would adjust staffing.

Ryanair has over 27,000 employees across its group.

Speaking to CNBC on Monday, Ryanair CEO Michael O’Leary said ticket prices will rise across Europe as lower-price oil hedges run out.

“The market customers have been insulated for the last 12 months,” O’Leary said. “But those hedges are rolling off. There’s still no resolution in the Middle East. Jet oil today is $170 a barrel. I mean, this is a very significant cost cliff we’re all facing next year. I believe you’re going to see air fares rise next year across Europe by 10 percent, maybe even 20 percent.”

In its earnings report, Ryanair said that while short-haul air fares will increase “materially,” it will not be enough to offset the expected €2 billion increase in fuel costs, necessitating the additional cost-saving measures.

Fuel prices have prompted several major European airlines and airline groups to cut capacity this fall and winter, and to retire some older, less efficient aircraft. Price volatility also pushed Lufthansa to accelerate the shutdown of Lufthansa CityLine, and made the U.K.’s EasyJet amenable to a roughly $7.7 billion takeover by Apollo Global Management.

The price shock has generally been worse in Europe and Asia than in North America, as those regions are more dependent on Gulf oil and lack domestic refining capacity.

Does Fluoride In Drinking Water Affect Intelligence? What The Evidence Reveals

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Could childhood exposure to fluoridated water leave a lasting mark on cognition? Decades of data offer clues.

Could childhood exposure to fluoridated water leave a lasting mark on cognition? Decades of data offer clues.

Fluoride in community drinking water is not linked to lower adolescent IQ or poorer thinking skills later in life, a long-running US study finds.

Researchers followed a generation of Wisconsin school-leavers, examining cognitive performance from adolescence into their eighties.

Those exposed to fluoridated water did not show a consistent disadvantage compared with people whose communities had no fluoridation.

The findings address concerns that adding fluoride to drinking water to prevent tooth decay could undermine intellectual development.

Professor John Robert Warren, the study’s first author, said:

“Utah, Florida and many municipalities have chosen to remove fluoride from drinking water based on flawed studies that considered the IQ effects of exposure to massive doses of fluoride.

Because levels of fluoride added to municipal drinking water in the U.S. are so much lower, almost all prior evidence from those international studies is not relevant to U.S. public policy debates.”

The research draws on the Wisconsin Longitudinal Study, which began with over 10,000 people who graduated from high school in 1957.

Researchers combined school IQ records with historical information about community water fluoridation and naturally occurring fluoride levels.

Following participants into old age allowed researchers to examine whether childhood exposure was associated with lasting differences in cognitive performance.

Comparing communities requires care because their residents can differ in ways that also relate to cognitive performance.

Once those background differences were taken into account, there was no consistent evidence that exposure was associated with either better or worse performance.

The same broad pattern appeared when researchers focused on people who had lived in the same county since early childhood, increasing confidence in their estimates of exposure.

Overall, the findings suggest that growing up with fluoridated community water is not associated with poorer cognitive performance in old age.

The study was published in the journal Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences (Warren et al., 2026).

Denver Air Connection Plans First Florida Service

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Denver Air Connection will launch its first-ever service to Florida, and it will be outside the scope of any Essential Air Service (EAS) contract. The only other place in the carrier’s network that isn’t directly part of an EAS contract is its service to Telluride, Colorado, from both Denver and Phoenix.

Beginning January 9, 2027, DAC will begin nonstop flights from Muskegon, Michigan, to Orlando Sanford, with service operating on Saturdays using the 50-seat Embraer E145XR.

While Muskegon itself only has one other destination, Chicago O’Hare, which is also operated by DAC under the EAS contract, DAC will compete indirectly with Allegiant, as it has a sizable operation and a base in Grand Rapids, Michigan, which is located only about 45 minutes east of Muskegon.

The 1,030-mile route from Muskegon to Orlando Sanford on the E145 will become the longest at-risk flight operated by any EAS carrier, and one of the longest commercial airline flights operated by an Embraer E145 in the country, overtaking the current record holder, United’s 830-mile route from Washington Dulles to Mobile, Alabama, by almost 200 miles.

That record for the E145 is a little bit of a technicality, as JSX does operate a handful of flights over 1,000 miles with its Embraer E145s, but the carrier is not considered a commercial airline, and is instead classified as a “public charter operation.”

Muskegon Airport director Ken Efting did have something to say about this new service: “This is a historic milestone for MKG and the communities we serve…This is exactly the kind of opportunity we’ve been working toward, and it’s a testament to our growing community support and strong partnership with Denver Air Connection.”

This isn’t the first time an EAS carrier has operated at-risk service to the state of Florida from an EAS community. These smaller carriers sometimes begin flights to leisure destinations like Florida and other leisure-heavy destinations to entice higher passenger numbers that boost the communities they serve.

Muskegon will join the small but growing list of Essential Air Service communities that are now connected with nonstop flights to the state of Florida, but they will be the only EAS community in Michigan to have Florida flights.

Automatic litter boxes are not just a serious time saver for cat owners; theyâre also hugely beneficial for cats, particularly those that refuse to use anything but a fresh bed of cat litter. Yet, some cat parents are hesitant about the idea of bringing home a self-cleaning litter box.

For cats accustomed to an airy, open litter tray, a compact, closed-in automatic litter box might seem like too much of a change. But itâs not always just the cat that has to be convinced. For those accustomed to the simplicity of a basic litter tray, the idea of one with a power cord and moving parts may feel a bit overwhelming.

In my experience, there are two big reasons cat parents hesitate to make the switch from a traditional litter pan to a fancy self-cleaning litter box.

The first reason is all about the cat. Many owners fear that their cat wonât be able to adjust to the design of an automatic litter box. While traditional tray boxes are wide open on top and allow plenty of room for big cats to turn around, dig, and stand tall as they do their business, most automatic boxes are more compact.

These boxes typically have hoods or are fully enclosed. Many also have smaller litter areas, which can be difficult for larger cats to navigate.

The second issue that keeps many parents scooping manually rather than investing in a box that can do it for them is about safety.

Automatic litter boxes have a lot of moving parts. Many turn completely upside down while cleaning, so itâs natural to be worried that your cat could be frightened, pinched, or trapped by the mechanism. Of course, these boxes have

sensors and safety controls to minimize the risks of an accident, but no technology is foolproof.

With their new automatic litter box, Neakasa listened to the most common concerns of hesitant cat parents and used them to inspire their design.

For the big or claustrophobic kitty, the M9 offers a wide-open, spacious design. And plenty of headspace means even big kitties like Maine Coons and Ragdolls can stand up tall while they do their business.

For the claustrophobic kitty, the M9 has another trick up its sleeve: the hood and âcabinâ of the litter box are translucent. This means plenty of light gets inside, and your cat has the ability to see all around them while they assume that oh-so vulnerable position.

The Neakasa M9 Automatic Litter Box also has plenty of reassuring features for even the most nervous owner.

This box has many of the same safety sensors that most other automatic boxes do. These work to prevent cleaning while the cat is present and stop cleaning if your cat leaps inside mid-cycle. But what makes the M9 different is that it is structurally designed to be safe even if those sensors fail.

Thanks to the spherical design and âtilt-safeâ rotation, even if the box were to somehow enter cleaning mode while your cat was inside, they would just get a bit of a ride (like a kitty in a hamster ball). Because the M9 cabin is free of pinch points and the opening does not close or shrink during cleaning, it would be virtually impossible for a cat to be harmed.

This is exactly the kind of fail-safe design nervous cat parents need.

Stock market after midterm elections: What S&P 500 history shows

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Markets are sweating it out ahead of the midterm elections, but the event historically heralds strong performance for stocks — regardless of whether it's a red or blue wave, split decision, or status quo in Washington.

Follow CNBC's live coverage of the 2026 midterm elections

The S&P 500 tends to perform well after both midterm and presidential elections. According to a CNBC analysis of S&P 500 returns after elections from 1980 through 2024, the index rose an average of 0.2% after one day and one week, and 1.1% after one month across both presidential and midterm elections.

A year later, the index gained an average of 13.7% after midterms and 13.8% after presidential elections. Returns were positive in 91% of cases. And the S&P 500 was up after all 11 midterms from 1982 through 2022.

Short-term returns were less reliable, particularly after presidential contests, which produced slightly negative average returns after one day and one week.

"There's some anxiousness before these elections," Ryan Detrick, CNBC contributor and chief market strategist at Carson Group, told CNBC. "Once you are through it, it's a big relief and markets tend to do pretty well."

Still, the market usually prefers divided government: one party in charge of the White House and the other holding at least one house of Congress.

Across 10 midterm and five presidential elections that left no single party in charge, the S&P 500 rose an average 15% after one year. One-year returns were positive in 14 out of 15 cases. By comparison, the index gained an average of 11.5% over the year after elections that resulted in single-party control.

"If you have a huge red wave or a big blue wave, markets actually kind of don't like that as much because you have too much spending one way and too much power," Detrick said.

Leaving Democrats in control of Congress with a Republican in the White House — a scenario that may emerge from the elections on Tuesday — was also positive. A CNBC analysis found that after the three midterms (1986, 1990 and 2006) when Democrats controlled both houses and a Republican held the presidency, the S&P 500 averaged a 0.5% decline on the reaction day but was up 3.4% a month later and 10.9% after a year.

Granted, elections may be coincident events to factors that matter far more for market performance.

The S&P 500 plunged 22.1% in the year after the 2000 elections due to the dotcom bust, marking the worst post-election performance since 1980. Conversely, the S&P 500's best post-election year was a 38.3% gain after the 2020 elections, when markets mounted a massive comeback from the COVID-induced selloff.

Investors should bear in mind that Federal Reserve policy and the corporate earnings cycle ultimately matter more than elections, according to Société Générale.

Stocks declined in the aftermath of the 2018 midterms, for instance, when Democrats wrested control of the House during President Donald Trump's first term. More importantly, the Fed tightened monetary policy that year, raising interest rates four times, and the S&P 500 ended the year down 6.2%, though the market was up 11.7% a year after the election.

The big swing factor now is AI and whether declines in tech will continue. As long as the AI wave is still moving and the economy keeps growing, "this bull market is probably still alive and well," Detrick says.

But a Democratic sweep of the House and Senate would almost certainly pressure the AI infrastructure buildout and could lead to more retrenchment.

The bond market is also skittish with yields rising globally and a mini debt crisis in France. And the war with Iran could get worse post-election if President Trump follows through on threats of more strikes or Iran escalates.

"Seasonality isn't destiny, and worries around Iran, AI and France won't mysteriously vanish," said Jim Reid, Deutsche Bank's global head of macro and thematic research, in a note.

If history is any guide, we could see a Santa rally and S&P 500 gains after the midterms. But this is still an AI-driven market, with geopolitics, the Fed, and bond market very much in charge.

— CNBC's Chris Hayes contributed reporting.

Meet the French Soldiers Who Get Paid to Climb Hard Alpine Routes

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Dream jobs come in all shapes and sizes. Some might consider my job at Climbing dreamy. Others might hope to hangboard their way into professional climbing one day. My former colleague Duane Raleigh once made a very serious list of climber “dream jobs” that included roofers, fast food cooks, and snow shovelers.

I am here to tell you that none of these jobs are the best climber jobs. The actual best job for a climber, without any shadow of a doubt, is to be a member of the French military’s 27th Mountain Infantry Brigade. Why?

Allow me to describe the group’s latest work trip.

Last Wednesday, October 14, Sergeant Konrad Piontek and Corporals Amaury Fouillade and Clovis Paulin stood on the 6,654-meter summit of Peak 41 in Nepal. On the 1,400-meter North Face, they had just opened a stunning new route linking long sections of vertical ice with burly rock roofs and pitches of deep snow. They called the line Bon Matin (ED+ WI5+ M6 A1 90° snow), which translates to “Good Morning.” (Not sure what “ED+” means? Check out this article about international alpine grades.)

The three first ascensionists were part of a team of six from the 27th Mountain Infantry Brigade, better known as the Groupe Militaire de Haute Montagne, or GMHM. They had arrived in Nepal in mid-September along with a military doctor. After a period of acclimatization, the group split into two rope teams according to their skill level and how well each had adjusted to the altitude.

The Piontek-Fouillade-Paulin team eyed an attractive, unclimbed smear running plumb down Peak 41’s north side, to the left of a Korean team’s route from 2016. They began climbing on October 11 and summited after “four days of particularly committed climbing,” according to a statement shared with Climbing.

Over the 1,400 meters of the face, the team had to deal with numerous steep and poorly protected pitches, including sections of vertical snow. “We weren’t expecting pitches as steep as that, especially in snow,” said Corporal Paulin in the statement. But they also climbed what can only be described as truly classic mixed terrain: solid rock and squeaky ice, hanging above tons of exposure.

After summiting, the team descended the peak’s southeastern aspect via 15 rappels and significant downclimbing, turning their outing into one massive loop back to basecamp. “Reaching the summit was a real physical relief,” said Corporal Fouillade. “The mental release came later.”

Officially, France’s 10-person GMHM is tasked with “mastering extreme terrestrial environments, particularly vertical terrain and high altitude,” said a spokesperson to Climbing. This fluency in alpine terrain aims to contribute to the development of the entire French army’s mountain capabilities.

“The whole point of the GMHM is to practice high level alpinism,” said the group’s spokesperson. “What is learned regarding mountaineering or cold weather environments is passed on to the French mountain units, but the GMHM has otherwise no militaristic purposes.”

Similar mountain-military units exist across the globe, but—tragically—nothing so geared toward climbing excellence exists in North America. (If you happen to have a French passport and the pre-requisites, I’d be refreshing this recruitment page regularly.)

As far as we know, there is no fixed contract length for GMHM members; some alpinists have been part of the dream team for years and years.

In addition to one high-altitude expedition each year, the group travels to Greenland to teach the Groupement de Commandos de Montagne (literally: “mountain commandos”) about how to thrive in cold weather. They also take minor trips to develop rock and ice climbing skills, whether that’s locally in the French Alps or abroad to Yosemite. In plain terms, that means that 10 climbers get to occasionally don camo and earn a decent government salary to climb full time, around the world.

Did I mention the GMHM is the ultimate dream job?

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

There are a number of reasons cats tend to be picky when it comes to mealtime. Depending on your feline, you may be dealing with one or all of these difficult instincts and sensitivities.

To start, cats have a very sensitive sense of smell. Even quality food can be turned down if the aroma is too unfamiliar. Cats are also very finicky about textures. They tend to develop very specific preferences about how foods should feel in their mouth. This makes it both hard to introduce new foods and to keep your cat from getting bored with the very specific foods they like.

Many cats exhibit neophobiaâa fear of new things. This doesnât just apply to new people, places, and toys, either. Cats evolved to be naturally wary of new food items and prey. This is likely because their ancestors lived in the desert where many little creatures are deadlier than their size might indicate. While modern cat food isnât likely to be venomous, your cat's instincts still tell them to beware anything they havenât eaten before.

Itâs the combination of a desire for the familiar and a tendency to get bored of the too familiar that makes picky cats so hard to feed. They want enough options that fall within a very specific range of flavors, textures, and aromas to keep them both interested and comfortable.

This can make adding moisture to their diets challenging, too, especially for cats who prefer dry food over wet. Luckily, simple additions like savory bone broth can help cat parents incorporate more moisture without introducing an entirely unfamiliar meal.

Cats may not be the ones cooking dinner, but they certainly have opinions about what's in their bowls. Inspired by his own discerning feline companion, a Maine Coon named Nacho, lifelong cat dad and celebrity chef Bobby Flay created Made by Nacho to give cats a menu of their own.

With premium proteins, signature bone broth, and everything from crunchy kibble to tender cuts in gravy and airy lickable mousse, the brand offers cat parents more ways to cater to their pets' individual tastes. For households where mealtime has become a guessing game, that variety can make finding an appealing feeding routine a little easier.

Slow-simmered for hours to develop rich, chef-inspired flavor, the toppers also contain prebiotics to support digestive health and non-GMO apples, carrots, and pumpkin for naturally delicious depth of flavor.

The brand also offers meal toppers featuring freeze-dried chicken breast, functional blends, and lickable mousse treats in tempting flavors like chicken, tuna, and salmon.

For cats who grow bored with the same meals or turn up their noses at familiar food, these extras provide an easy way to shake up the routine. A splash of bone broth or a little mousse can introduce a new texture and boost savory appeal without requiring a complete diet change. For picky eaters, that flexibility gives cat parents more ways to keep mealtime interesting and discover what makes their feline friend purr.

And because hydration doesn't have to mean giving up your cat's favorite flavors, a little added moisture can be a welcome addition to an already successful mealtime routine. Just remember to keep fresh drinking water available, too.

Dear Guest Cafe Brings Art Deco Whimsy to Silver Lake

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

On a busy stretch of Sunset Boulevard, a playful cafe steeped in the aromas of lavender and honey awaits. A quiet departure from reality, Dear Guest in the Silver Lake neighborhood of Los Angeles feels less like a neighborhood bakery and more like the imaginative opening scene of an artistic film.

Designed by Los Angeles-based Studio Jialun Xiong, the 1,100-square-foot interior blends the elegance of 1930s Art Deco design with the dreamlike wonder of Studio Ghibli’s animated worlds, creating a space suspended between nostalgia and whimsy. The interiors are fully customized, which contributes to their welcoming, familiar feel.

“It’s almost like a memory you can’t quite place,” says Jialun Xiong. “We wanted to create somewhere that feels slightly removed from everyday life—playfulness comes through in the colors and shapes, while the dreamlike quality is what ties everything together.”

Drawing inspiration from Parisian boutique hotels and traditional Japanese ryokans, the carefully layered design references create an environment that borrows from hospitality rituals, not just its aesthetics.

Customers enter through an outdoor seating area furnished with custom butter-yellow ombré tabletops and the studio’s new Extrusion Stools, establishing a tone of color, pattern, and play that carries throughout the experience. A concierge-style pastry counter welcomes guests near the entrance, while traditional European crown molding is paired with a reflective silver ceiling to create a slightly surreal effect.

For Xiong, the pastry counter is where the story of Dear Guest comes to life. “There’s something about that sense of arrival and hospitality that I really like,” she says. “The idea of creating an experience beyond just having coffee and pastries. I think it captures the personality of the project quite naturally.”

Geometry and repetition are at the center of Dear Guest. Circles of varying sizes and rounded forms appear repeatedly in mirrors, while grid patterns recur throughout the wall panels, banquettes, lighting, and bar. The result is a sense of intention, with each element thoughtfully curated to advance the story unfolding within the cafe.

Furnished almost exclusively with pieces by Studio Jialun Xiong, the interior showcases the studio’s use of color as a form of creative expression. The soft yellow of the Void Chair contrasts with the cocoa-brown leather of the Skeleton Lounge Chair.

Black glazed tiles and warm gray quartz countertops complement the pearlescent hot-glass tile backsplash that defines the bar. Despite the variety of shades and textures, the space maintains a strong sense of restraint and consistency.

“I think working with a richer palette actually makes proportion, rhythm, and visual balance even more important,” Xiong says. “We wanted the space to feel layered, but not maximalist. There’s still a quiet sense of order that brings everything together.”

A row of custom pendant lights is suspended above the tables, while a patinated bronze, star-shaped joinery detail adorns the cabinet on the opposite wall. In the bathroom, silver walls are embellished with hand-painted illustrations featuring motifs often found in hotels.

“I like mixing things that feel familiar with something a bit unusual or playful,” says Xiong. “It’s more about those little moments you discover as you spend time in the space.”

Malia Agostinelli is a writer and editor blending design and the built environment with the art of storytelling. Rarely found without a pen in hand, she also enjoys exploring new countries, taking yoga classes, and gathering inspiration for her Substack, Collected Elsewhere.

From Drifting Lights to Crystal Grid, Preciosa’s extensive design portfolio brings the craft to customizable crystal.

Collard Greens Vinaigrette

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

For those who might shudder at the thought of eating raw hardy greens like collards or kale, this vibrant side inspired by the French bistro classic leeks vinaigrette is the answer. Simmered past squeaky-raw yet well short of meltingly braised, they offer substantial bite, like al dente pasta. The mustard dressing couldn’t be simpler to make—use it as a base for any salad you might serve this season. It’s punchy enough to stand up to winter vegetables or any sturdy bitter green.

For more Thanksgiving recipes, tips, menus, and inspiration, head to our 2026 Thanksgiving Hub.

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 325°. Toast ½ cup raw walnuts on a rimmed baking sheet, tossing halfway through, until golden brown, 10–12 minutes.

Meanwhile, cook 2 bunches collard greens, ribs and stems removed, leaves cut into 2" pieces, in a large pot of generously salted boiling water until just tender, 9–11 minutes. Using tongs, immediately transfer to a large bowl of ice water and let cool.

Whisk 2 garlic cloves, finely grated, ¼ cup white wine vinegar, 2 Tbsp. Dijon mustard, preferably country-style, ⅓ cup extra-virgin olive oil, ¼ cup (packed) coarsely chopped tarragon, 2 tsp. sugar, and 1 tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt in a medium bowl to combine. Set dressing aside.

Transfer toasted walnuts to a small bowl and drizzle with remaining 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil. Sprinkle with a generous pinch each of sugar and salt and toss to coat. Taste and add more sugar if needed (nuts should be more sweet than salty). Set seasoned walnuts aside.

Drain collard greens and squeeze gently to remove excess water. Transfer to bowl with reserved dressing and toss to coat evenly. Cover and chill at least 15 minutes and up to 2 hours.

Arrange greens in a shallow bowl or on a platter and spoon any dressing remaining in bowl over. Top with seasoned walnuts and tarragon leaves.

Do Ahead: Walnuts can be toasted and dressing can be made 1 day ahead. Store walnuts airtight at room temperature. Cover dressing and chill.

Pear Salad With Honey-Roasted Seeds

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

In the midst of a rich meal, something fresh and acidic keeps your taste buds awake. Where lettuce splashed with vinaigrette can feel like an afterthought, a juicy, savory-slanting pear salad is worth making room for on a plate full of turkey and cornbread. This is the antithesis of the haphazardly chunked together fruit salads you find at the supermarket for a brunch or dessert spread. Keep the focus on crisp-tart pears—apples also work—and it’s a welcome accompaniment to the other hearty dishes. Sliced razor-thin (use a mandoline) and fanned out on a platter, the fruit acts as an edible canvas for wisps of cheese and clusters of honey-roasted seeds. Leftovers (if you have any, of course) are delightful spooned over a well-toasted and buttered slice of multigrain bread the next day.

Pick firm green pears (Bartlett and Anjou are fair game; use Asian pears in a pinch) to ensure they hold their shape once sliced—even a little underripe is better than slightly over. After slicing, have a taste: If they are very tart, add 1 tsp. honey to the dressing; if fairly sweet, skip it.

For more Thanksgiving recipes, tips, menus, and inspiration, head to our 2026 Thanksgiving Hub.

Preheat oven to 325°. Whisk 3 Tbsp. honey, 1 tsp. garlic powder, ½ tsp. freshly ground pepper, 3 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil, and ¾ tsp. Diamond Crystal or ½ tsp. Morton kosher salt in a large bowl to combine. Add 1 cup raw sunflower seeds, pumpkin seeds (pepitas), and/or coarsely crushed raw nuts (such as walnuts, almonds, and/or hazelnuts) and stir to coat. Spread out in a single layer on a parchment-lined rimmed baking sheet; reserve bowl.

Bake seed mixture until golden brown, 12–14 minutes. (Honey mixture will puff up a bit but will deflate when you stir.) Toss honey-roasted seeds, then let cool. Toss again just to break up.

Whisk 3 Tbsp. fresh lemon juice, 1 tsp. Dijon mustard, a pinch of kosher salt, and remaining 1 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil in reserved bowl. Add 2 lb. firm green pears (such as Bartlett and/or Anjou; about 4 large), cored, thinly sliced, to dressing and gently toss to coat.

Arrange pears on a large flat platter. Scatter honey-roasted seeds over and top with 2 oz. Parmesan, aged white cheddar, or Manchego cheese, shaved.

Do Ahead: Honey-roasted seeds can be made 6 hours ahead. Store at room temperature.

Gravy-Braised Turkey

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Cooking a whole turkey is a commitment. Between the schlepping, trussing, and constant babysitting, it’s a serious investment—and one that doesn’t always pay dividends. For a more laid-back affair, and one that practically guarantees success, might we suggest turkey legs cooked directly in gravy? Yes, in the gravy.

This recipe will defy everything you have probably done to cook a turkey until now. The first important step is searing the whole turkey legs for optimal browning. Now you are ready to create a base for the gravy with all those drippings and toasty bits left in the pot. No need to make a full-on roux either: Just sprinkle in the flour over the melted fat, whisk to combine, and slowly pour in the broth. Once the gravy is done, in goes the turkey and aromatics for braising. The rest is you worrying about something else, because that turkey will cook itself.

For more Thanksgiving recipes, tips, menus, and inspiration, head to our 2026 Thanksgiving Hub.

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 325°. Season 4 whole turkey legs (thigh and drumstick; about 6½ lb. total), patted dry, all over with kosher salt and freshly ground pepper. Heat 3 Tbsp. vegetable oil in a large braiser or Dutch oven over medium-high. Working in 2 batches if needed to avoid overcrowding, arrange turkey legs, skin side down, in pot and cook, undisturbed, rotating pan on burner a few times to ensure even browning, until browned underneath and they easily release from pot, 8–12 minutes. Transfer turkey legs to a rimmed baking sheet, arranging skin side up. (No need to brown other side; turkey will still be raw at this point.)

Add more oil if pot looks dry; arrange 2 large oranges and/or lemons, halved, and 2 heads of garlic, tops trimmed to expose cloves, cut side down in pot and add 3 large shallots, peeled. Cook, undisturbed, until deeply browned underneath, 2–3 minutes. Transfer each to baking sheet with turkey as they are done (it’s okay if some fall apart slightly).

Add 6 Tbsp. (¾ stick) unsalted butter to pot and cook until melted. Sprinkle in ⅓ cup (42 g) all-purpose flour and cook, stirring constantly, until no pockets of dry flour remain, about 1 minute. Gradually pour in 4 cups low-sodium chicken broth, stirring constantly to combine. Bring to a simmer and cook until gravy is just starting to thicken, about 2 minutes. Stir in 3 Tbsp. Worcestershire sauce and season with salt and pepper.

Nestle turkey legs into gravy, skin side up, then tuck oranges, garlic, and shallots around, arranging oranges and garlic cut side up. Add 1 small bunch thyme (about 10 sprigs), tied with kitchen twine, and cover pot tightly with a lid or foil. Braise 2 hours. (If your pot is too small to comfortably fit everything, transfer gravy to a 13x9" baking dish before adding turkey legs and aromatics.) Uncover pot and continue to bake until turkey legs are deeply browned, meat shreds easily off the bone, and gravy has thickened a little, 45–55 minutes.

Let turkey and gravy cool slightly before serving. Transfer to a platter if desired. (If gravy has excess fat on top, skim off with a spoon and discard before serving or spooning over legs on platter.)

Buttered Nut Squash

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

We rate butternut the most affable winter squash—relatively easy to prepare, consistently delicious, and available at most grocery stores. The buttered nut dressing is more than just cheeky wordplay. Bold from brown butter and balsamic vinegar and bright from lemon, the crunchy topping is an excellent foil to the sweetness of roasted squash. Cook the squash a couple hours ahead of time and all you’ll have left to do before dinner is make the dressing.

For more Thanksgiving recipes, tips, menus, and inspiration, head to our 2026 Thanksgiving Hub.

Place a rack in lower third of oven; preheat to 425°. Lightly crush 2 tsp. fennel seeds with a mortar and pestle or place on a cutting board and gently crush with a heavy pan. Peel 1 medium butternut squash (about 3 lb.) and halve lengthwise. Scoop out seeds; discard. Cut squash into 2" irregular pieces, aiming for sharp, jagged shapes. Transfer to a large rimmed baking sheet; drizzle with 3 Tbsp. extra-virgin olive oil and sprinkle with fennel seeds, 2 tsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt, and 1 tsp. crushed red pepper flakes. Toss squash to coat, arrange in a single layer, and roast until browned and tender, 30–35 minutes.

Do Ahead: Squash can be roasted 2 hours ahead. Store at room temperature.

While the squash is roasting, cook 6 oz. thick-cut bacon, cut into 1" pieces, in a medium skillet over medium heat, stirring often, until browned and mostly crisp, 8–12 minutes. Transfer to a small plate with a slotted spoon.

Reduce heat to low; combine 1 cup raw pecans and 4 Tbsp. unsalted butter in same pan. Cook, stirring often, until butter browns and nuts have slightly darkened, 6–8 minutes. Remove from heat. Stir in 3 garlic cloves, finely chopped (this will give it a head start to take off the raw edge), then add bacon, zest and juice of 1 large lemon, 3 sprigs sage, and 2 Tbsp. balsamic vinegar and stir to combine. Taste dressing and season with kosher salt if needed.

Arrange squash on a platter and spoon dressing over.

Thanksgiving Cornbread

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Traditionally, cornbread stuffing requires making cornbread, cubing it and letting it dry out, mixing it with stock, eggs, meat, and aromatics, then transforming it into a stuffing (or dressing). This process produces some truly memorable casseroles, but I have an alternative that streamlines the process without compromising flavor: stuffing cornbread. It still smells enticingly like the classic Thanksgiving side as you set the baking dish on the table. I’ve still packed in a medley of woodsy herbs, sweet onions, vegetal peppers, and savory sausage. And it still slices into lofty servings to perch on your plate next to the sweet potatoes and green beans. It just takes half as long to make.

The intentional two-to-one ratio of cornmeal to flour in this cornbread results in a moist, tender crumb that still has enough structure to hold up against the sturdy mix-ins. The sugar might seem like a lot, but it balances out the savory sausage and aromatics perfectly.

For more Thanksgiving recipes, tips, menus, and inspiration, head to our 2026 Thanksgiving Hub.

Place a rack in middle of oven; preheat to 400°. Line a 13x9" baking pan with parchment paper, leaving generous overhang on long sides. Coat pan and parchment with unsalted butter. Melt ½ cup (1 stick) unsalted butter in a large skillet over medium-low heat, then pour into a small bowl.

Cook 1 green bell pepper, halved, ribs and seeds removed, finely chopped, 1 medium white onion, finely chopped, and 2 celery stalks, finely chopped, in same skillet, stirring occasionally with a wooden spoon, until softened, about 4 minutes. Add 1 lb. spicy and/or sweet Italian sausage, casings removed, and cook, breaking up and stirring occasionally, until browned (it may still be a little raw in the center at this point), about 5 minutes. Remove from heat.

Stir together 4 cups (624 g) fine-ground yellow cornmeal, 2 cups (250 g) all-purpose flour, 1 cup (200 g) sugar, 1 Tbsp. plus 1½ tsp. baking powder, 2 tsp. Diamond Crystal or 1¼ tsp. Morton kosher salt, 1 tsp. baking soda, and ¼ cup finely chopped sage, thyme, and/or rosemary in a large bowl. Gradually pour in 3½ cups buttermilk, then melted butter, stirring constantly until combined. Add 4 large eggs one at a time, mixing to incorporate after each addition. Fold in sausage mixture, then scrape batter into prepared pan.

Bake cornbread, rotating pan front to back halfway through, until golden brown and top springs back when gently pressed, 38–45 minutes. Let cool in pan 15 minutes. Using parchment paper overhang, lift cornbread out of pan and transfer to a cutting board. Cut into 12–18 slices; arrange on a platter.

Do Ahead: Cornbread can be baked 4 hours ahead. Store at room temperature; cut just before serving.

NYC’s Hottest New Wine Bar Started With a DM Slide

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

An interview with the team behind El Camarón Peludo.

Now Open is a yearlong series celebrating some of 2026’s most exciting new restaurants. Throughout the year, we’ll check in with teams in Chicago, New York City, and Washington, D.C., to hear what it’s really like behind the scenes of a buzzy opening. Then, we’ll host exclusive meals at these restaurants as part of Dinner Party, presented by Capital One. Read along for challenges, candid reflections, and advice from the proprietors behind some of the country’s hottest new openings.

Giovanni Cervantes and Tania Apolinar spent the last five years slinging tacos at breakneck pace to keep up with the lines at Taqueria Ramírez and Carnitas Ramírez and rowdy World Cup crowds at Socceria. Now the duo is slowing things down a bit for their first full-service restaurant El Camarón Peludo, a playful wine and seafood spot in the West Village where guests linger over mariscos.

At the tiny, walk-in-only restaurant, the menu fuses Mexican seafood dishes with Spanish influences and excellent wine from around the world. For the project, Cervantes and Apolinar teamed up with chef Carlos Barrera, a Mexico City-born chef who has worked in Michelin-starred restaurants in Spain and hosted dozens of popular pop-ups around NYC. When I sat down with Cervantes and Apolinar, I planned to pick their brains about how and why they manage four wildly popular (and very different) New York City restaurants. Then I learned how much El Camarón Peludo was the result of fate: If it weren’t for space constraints, a tapas-fueled trip, and a social media superfan, the duo may have had an entirely different restaurant on their hands.

Pre Shift: Where did the idea for El Camarón Peludo come from?

Tania Apolinar: When we were looking at [the space], we considered making it a taqueria, but because of the [long shape of the restaurant], it wouldn’t have worked for that concept. We knew we wanted to do something different, focused on seafood and wine.

Giovanni Cervantes: I was in conversation with Carlos about this idea to serve mariscos in New York when we saw the space. We didn’t know [at the time], but we were setting up the basis of the menu because we were talking about what we like and what we miss a lot [from Mexico]. We also talked about [our past trips] to Europe. We were really interested in how to match [the style of European tapas and wine bars] with what we do, which is the Mexican approach.

How did you connect with Barrera for the project?

Cervantes: Carlos was one of the first clients that we ever had in Taqueria Ramírez. We met basically through that and through Instagram. He was always commenting a lot...

Cervantes: Exactly. Sending the flame emoji. Then soon enough, we find out that we were neighbors because he lives in Greenpoint, so we started seeing each other more often.

Apolinar: Then we did a pop-up with him serving his famous Trompo Negro, which is like al pastor with [a smoky black spice paste that is widely used in Yucatán and surrounding regions], and that was the first time we actually connected in a working setup. We’re really aligned and happy with the partnership.

Can you tell me about how you balance Mexican and Spanish influences on the menu?

Cervantes: When we found the space, we knew it was the perfect setup to try something different. Then we made a trip to Europe and a trip to Mexico. After we came back from Europe, I think I was feeling very “Europeanized.” I remember having a conversation with Tanya and Carlos about making it even more Spanish, and both of them were like, “What?” And I’m glad they [pushed back], because we definitely found the right balance.

Apolinar: We’re talking about Mexican and Spanish, but in the end, it’s about the seafood and the freshness, the product that we really want to stand out. That’s why [we serve a] very simple gambas a la plancha, and the carpaccio has that approach as well, but then other more Mexican classics [like tostadas topped with tuna or aguachile] have more sauces and all of that.

Cervantes: And it’s not just the food. The influence that we took from different parts of Europe is the setup, the vibe, the size of the plates, the type of space. We want the space to feel cozy and friendly.

How is the service different from your other spots?

Cervantes: At Taqueria Ramírez and Carnitas Ramírez, it’s all about efficiency. Making it right, as fast as you can, and getting people in and out of the place. It’s nonstop volume. The consistency is awesome over there, and that’s [how we] measure that we’re making it right. Here it’s different. It’s more about the experience of the space and the music and the wine. [Our staff are] with these people for an hour or two, so you try to make it personal in a way. It feels different when you have that much time to see how people are enjoying the experience and transporting people to that world through what we’re doing. That’s comforting to me.

Now that you have four different restaurants around the city, how do you balance your time between them?

Cervantes: For Taqueria Ramírez, the very first one, we can sleep very well because almost everybody on the staff has been there since we opened. I’m just mentioning that because it’s all about building the right team because you can only be in one place at a time. Then at Carnitas, our partners [Yvon de Tassigny and Kari Boden] are really great and the plan was for them to be the day to day operators from the beginning. So right now we’re between El Cameron Peludo and Socceria because they’re the new babies and there’s a lot of work to do there.

Tania: It’s tiring, but at the end of the day, I’ve been enjoying it a lot. It’s nonstop but it feels rewarding to see the point of view of customers and make people feel happy and connect to them.

The Best Bike Lights for Triathletes in 2026, Tested and Reviewed

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

If you buy through our links, we may earn an affiliate commission. This supports our mission to get more people active and outside.Learn about Outside Online's affiliate link policy

Bottom Line: Each of the bike lights in our Best Bike Lights of 2026 list below has been tested on long rides by an experienced triathlete for many miles. Our reviewer is unbiased, with no brand relationships, meaning no brand has paid to appear in our list.

It’s that time of year again. You glance at your training plan, realize your post-work ride slot will now be taking place in the dark, and suddenly “bike light” jumps to the top of your shopping list. The good news is that bike lights in 2026 are significantly better than offerings from just a few years ago. You could even say they are light-years ahead (pun intended) of the bike lights from the 20th century. They are brighter, lighter, and – critically for triathletes – easier to mount on your bike than ever before.

But before you order the cheapest bike light on Amazon, there’s a bit more to think through. The cheapest option is far from the best, but you also don’t need to take out a second mortgage to buy a bike light either. Here’s what actually matters when buying lights as a triathlete, followed by the best options on the market right now.

As a light-toting triathlete, your goal isn’t just to see the road ahead of you; it is also to be seen. Public roads are far from perfectly safe, especially in today’s phone-addicted world filled with distracted drivers. But thankfully, having a bike light is one of the best ways to gain the attention of nearby drivers, reminding them to slow down and give you space as they pass by.

When shopping for a bike light, you want to consider two different design priorities, and pay close attention because most lights optimize for one or the other. First, you want a bike light that shines a powerful beam down the road, illuminating your path at speed in the dark. Second, you want a light that is designed to be seen. These lights use wide-angle optics, lateral emission, and attention-grabbing flash patterns to make you visible to cross-traffic and drivers approaching from the rear. Ideally, your bike light(s) do both.

For triathletes specifically, the lighting challenge runs deeper than a single unit on your bicycle. You might actually want three different lights for the corresponding sports. The ideal setup might include front and rear coverage on the bike, a headlamp or chest light for early-morning runs, and some form of visibility for open-water swims, especially those before sunrise. Each sport requires different hardware, but in this guide, we’ll focus on bike lights. Let’s help you figure out how to sort and sift through the hundreds of options on the market.

The standard brightness unit for sports lights is lumens. The higher the number, the brighter the light. But brighter isn’t always better. Some brands love to advertise max lumens at maximum output, but that can drain the battery in under two hours. Always check the light’s runtime at the mode you’ll actually use.

Consider the type of darkness you’ll be riding in, and how much light you’ll need. Not every triathlete will train in pitch darkness, so here is a quick reference of what you’ll find on the light market:

A pinpoint beam aimed straight ahead is great for one thing, and that’s seeing directly in front of you. But if you’re riding in the dark, you also want to have side visibility, not just from your own perspective, but for other riders and drivers to see you. The best bike lights have a wide spread beam that alerts drivers approaching from angles, not just head-on. Some lights now use multiple LEDs, which is worth considering if you ride in the dark year-round.

This is one of the most important aspects of a bike light because if the battery dies while you are riding in the dark 20 miles from home, you are SOL. Most bike lights are USB-C rechargeable, but runtime varies enormously. If you don’t get a light with a low-battery warning (ideally with several minutes of reserve before shutdown), make sure you keep tabs on how long you’ve been running it before it’s time to recharge.

This is where triathletes diverge from road cyclists. Aero bars, clip-on extensions, and non-standard cockpit shapes make standard clamp mounts useless in many setups. When shopping for lights, you’ll want to look for options that come with flexible silicone straps, GoPro-style mounts, or Garmin quarter-turn compatibility. Silicone straps cover the widest range of setups and are easy to transfer between bikes.

In the 2026 bike light market, there are a number of smart features that come with some of the more pristine options. Some of the features are just bells and whistles, but a few are genuinely helpful, especially for triathletes. Radar integration (Garmin Varia), ANT+ connectivity, automatic brightness adjustment, and brake-sensing technology are popular features that help improve function and safety on the bike. These are not marketing gimmicks – radar in particular can dramatically improve your confidence when riding on open roads with traffic.

Front light placement can be the trickiest part of lighting your tri bike. You can choose between below the basebar (the most aerodynamic option), midway down the aerobars (if the light rotates that direction), and arguably the best solution: adding a computer bridge to your aerobars and mounting the light on top or below it. Whatever you choose, test it on a bumpy road during daylight hours before relying on it in the dark. Remember that you’re not only looking for the light to stay attached, but also for the angle to remain stable as the light illuminates the road in front of you.

Rear lights are much more forgiving, with the seatpost being the most ideal spot for a rear-facing bike light. If you have limited seatpost access, you can mount a light on the seatstay. Just make sure your light is pointing backward rather than toward the sky or floor. If you have bottles or other gear mounted under or behind your seat, make sure they don’t block the bike light when they are fully loaded.

Output: 1,300 lumens front; 90 lumens rear

Battery life: 1.5 hrs front; 4.5 hrs rear

The Ion Pro and Flare bike lights do much more than illuminate through darkness. The paired set uses ANT+ to link the front and rear lights together, and you can control both from a compatible Garmin or Wahoo head unit. Up front, the Ion Pro has up to 1,300 lumens, which is more than enough for most low-light situations. It also has a clean beam pattern with solid front and side visibility, with a few hours of battery life across various power settings. In the rear, the Flare can be used with a 16-degree offset mount option that helps solve one of the most common triathlete problems: mounting a rear light on an angled seatpost or seatstay. The mount is sold separately, but it only costs $5.

Pros: ANT+ integration, offset rear mount solves tri-bike seatpost issues ($5 cost), strong front output Cons: Pricey for a set; front light only has 90 minutes of runtime at max output

Output: 130 to 1,000 lumens with six different modes

Battery life: 120 hrs on ECO FLASH; up to 2 hrs at max output

The Blinder has earned its spot on best-of lists for years, and the 2026 version is no exception. IP67 waterproofing and six different light modes make the Blinder 1000 suitable for a wide range of weather and light conditions, including midnight rain-riding. At low outputs, the Blinder 1000 lasts for days on a single charge. GoPro-style and standard handlebar mounts are both included, making it versatile on almost any tri cockpit.

Pros: Fully IP67 waterproof, versatile mounts, solid build quality, side window visibility

Cons: Heavier than some competitors at the same lumen output

Battery life: 1 hr at max output; up to 60 hrs at minimum output

If your friends call you “Nightrider,” then the AMPP 2200 might be the perfect bike light for you. With a max output of 2,200 lumens, the AMPP 2200 obliterates darkness in a way that can make you feel like you’re riding during the daytime. This is the light for those who ride in pitch darkness at speed, or those who live in extreme daylight climates such as Alaska or Iceland. While its top-end output is impressive, the AMPP 2200 includes different light modes that are ideal for riding in the dusk rather than the pitch black. At 500 to 800 lumens, the AMPP 2200 becomes a practical all-night light with sufficient battery reserve. Its included mount is quite secure, even on rough pavement. The trade-off is weight; at 211g, it’s the heaviest option on this list.

Pros: Exceptional max output, wide runtime range, secure mount, high-quality build

Cons: Heaviest light on this list; 2,200 lumens if overkill for those who don’t ride in complete darkness

Battery life: 4 to 80 hrs (front); 3 to 60 hrs (rear)

If you’re only looking for a pair of lights that will help you be seen, the Knog Frog combo could be the perfect fit. The front light output isn’t enough to illuminate a dark road, but not every triathlete wants to ride in the dark. Sometimes, you just want to be seen by drivers and cross-traffic, especially in low-light conditions. The combo’s battery life is fitting for the price point, and the pair is so light that you won’t even notice them. They come with a flexible silicone strap that wraps around virtually any tube shape, including aero bars. While they won’t light up a country road, the Knog Frog combo is great for being seen in the city, on the bike path, or on a foggy training loop.

Pros: Aero-bar compatible, extremely lightweight, very affordable, easy to use

Cons: 40 lumens won’t illuminate a dark road; not a standalone solution for night riding

Battery life: 16 hrs (day flash mode); 6 hrs (solid mode)

The Varia is a rear light that also functions as a vehicle radar. In terms of safety and security, it is one of the best bike lights on the market. When paired with a compatible Garmin head unit or Varia app, the light alerts you to vehicles approaching from behind, before you can even hear them. It is an incredibly useful safety feature, especially if you frequently ride in groups or on busy roads. If you get nervous riding on open roads, the Varia RTL515 could be the best option for you. While the light function itself isn’t anything special, the radar is the reason to buy it.

Pros: Vehicle radar is a game-changer on open roads; lightweight, impressive battery life, pairs with Garmin ecosystem

Cons: Expensive for a single rear light; radar requires a compatible head unit or phone mount

Battery life: 12 hrs constant; 30 hrs flash

In terms of bang for your buck, it’s hard to beat the Gravity Plus, which uses an accelerometer to detect braking and automatically increases brightness when you decelerate. It is effectively functioning as a brake light, and it is one of the cheapest options on this list. For triathletes who ride out of town, train with unpredictable stop-and-go conditions, or frequent group rides, the brake-sensing rear light is surprisingly effective. Runtime is excellent for a budget light, and you probably won’t even notice the added weight.

Pros: Brake-sensing technology, outstanding battery life, lightweight

Cons: 100 lumens is modest; a brighter light is necessary for riding in complete darkness

Crypto Hack Losses Hit $8.74 in 2026, More Than Triple 2025, Yet Without Binance the Year Ran Quieter: How Did Bitcoin and Ethereum React After Major Hacks?

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Crypto hack losses hit $8.74B in 2026 vs $2.51B in 2025. Here’s how Bitcoin and Ether moved a day, a week and a month after major hacks.

Crypto hack losses reached $8.74 billion in 2026 by October 31, more than three times the $2.51 billion lost over the same ten months of 2025, after the October 29 Binance breach alone added $6.36 billion.

Take Binance out and the picture flips. The rest of 2026 comes to $2.38 billion, about 5% less than 2025 at the same point, even though attackers hit 325 targets against 120 a year earlier. More attacks, smaller hauls, and then one enormous one.

The market has its own pattern. Across the ten largest hacks since January 2025, Bitcoin’s move on the day of the attack ranged from -4.1% to +1.9%. A month later, for the nine with a full month of data, the range ran from -15.5% to +19.9%, and it followed whatever the broader market was doing.

The 2025 hacks landed in falling markets, and every one was followed by a lower Bitcoin price a month later. Most of 2026’s landed in rising ones.

So how bad has 2026 really been, and what does history say about the weeks after Binance?

The size of the biggest theft. Through September, 2026 was running behind 2025: $2.24 billion lost against $2.48 billion. Then Binance lost $6.36 billion across ten blockchains in one night, more than all of 2025’s hacks put together.

The two years share a culprit. The FBI tied 2025’s largest crypto hack, the $1.4 billion Bybit theft on February 21, to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group, and Chainalysis now ties Binance to the same unit. The biggest hacks of each year:

The rest of 2026 looks different from 2025 too. There were 325 incidents through October, almost three times 2025’s 120, and five 2026 hacks topped $290 million, against one in 2025. Stolen keys and cross-chain bridges did most of the damage outside Binance.

The gross number also overstates what stays lost. CertiK’s October tally counts $3.03 billion frozen or returned, mostly Binance funds caught on-chain, which cuts the month’s adjusted losses to about $3.47 billion. Trackers also disagree on the year: CertiK puts 2026 at $9.2 billion gross and 2025 at $3.35 billion, while DefiLlama has $8.74 billion and $2.72 billion.

DISCOVER: Binance Hacker Sits on $1.4 Billion in Bitcoin, Triple a Week of ETF Buying

A small dip on the day, then the market’s own trend. The heatmap compares Bitcoin and Ether with their close the day before each of the ten biggest hacks since 2025.

Bybit is the closest match to Binance: a North Korean exchange theft over $1 billion. Bitcoin lost 14.2% within a week of it, with US tariff fears weighing on markets that week too, and it was still 12.4% lower a month on.

Binance is following a gentler path so far. From the October 28 close to Monday:

Neither yet. Bitcoin trades at $77,870 on Binance, flat on the day, and has held above its October 30 low of $76,326 for three days, right on the 200-day EMA (exponential moving average) at $76,512.

After Bybit, Bitcoin lost its floor within a week. After most of 2026’s hacks, it was higher a month later. The line between the two is clear on the chart: the 200-day EMA below, and the 50-day EMA at $80,293 above.

The crypto hack record says the first day rarely decides much. What Bitcoin does at the 200-day EMA over the next few weeks will show which path this one takes.
