31,000 Miles, 28 Years: Karl Bushby’s Walk Around the World by the Numbers

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Karl Bushby walks through a park in Peterborough, England, on October 22, 2026, during the final leg of his 28-year Goliath Expedition. (Photo: Leon Neal/Getty Images)

On November 1, 1998, Karl Bushby left Punta Arenas, Chile, and began walking north. Ahead of him, the 29-year-old British former paratrooper pushed a modified golf trolley filled with supplies and gear that he dubbed “The Beast.” His goal: to walk as far as a person could in unbroken footsteps on Earth, without using anything but his own hands and feet to make forward progress. His planned route for what he called the Goliath Expedition would take him from the southernmost tip of South America to his hometown of Hull, England, a journey he estimated would take him about 12 years.

Exactly 28 years and one day later, Bushby arrived back in Hull’s Queen Victoria Square to cheering crowds at shortly before noon on November 2, capping off a journey that was much longer—and far more complicated—than he could have imagined when he began. Along the way, he navigated shifting ice floes across the Bering Strait and swam the Caspian Sea to dodge geopolitical tensions and the English Channel after Eurotunnel refused to let him walk through the service tunnels connecting France and the UK. Not all the obstacles were physical, either: Bushby decamped to Mexico to regroup after his sponsorships dried up in the wake of the 2008 financial crisis and spent 10 years crossing Russia, stymied by visa requirements and an entry ban. (He finally received the permission he needed in 2014 after walking 3,600 miles across the United States to Russia’s embassy.)

All of that added up to something larger than a hike—a journey that ended up taking almost exactly half of the 57-year-old’s life. (It demanded a commensurate sacrifice, and not just from him. Bushby has a son, now 36, who he’s only seen a handful of times since leaving, and a 12-year-old granddaughter he had never met until finishing his trek; he also had and lost two long-term romantic relationships during the journey.)

For all that, the man behind the Goliath Expedition isn’t inclined to brag. When Backpacker writer Bill Donahue caught up with Bushby in Mexico in 2012, at what would turn out to be the halfway point of his walk, Bushby downplayed his own achievements.

“I’m an average guy,” Bushby said. “I’m less than average. I’m an underachiever. All I’m doing is putting one foot in front of the other.”

Here’s what Bushby’s big walk looked like by the numbers.

The approximate distance that Bushby walked from Punta Arenas, Chile, to Hull, England. That’s equivalent to 14 thru-hikes of the Appalachian Trail, 1.25 times the circumference of the globe, or roughly 13 percent of the average distance from the Earth to the Moon. That distance took him through 30 countries, according to the Associated Press.

The total distance Bushby had to swim during his trip. That included roughly 179 miles across the Caspian Sea in September and October 2024 and 21 miles across the English Channel in October 2026. Bushby did both of those swims in stages, resting on a support boat at night and returning to the coordinates where he had gotten out of the water in the morning.

The amount of time Bushby spent in jail after crossing borders without authorization. That includes 18 days in Panamanian custody after he crossed the roadless Darién Gap from Colombia and 58 days in Russian detention following his crossing of the Bering Strait from Alaska.

Bushby’s average pace across his entire trip. While far slower than other long-distance hikers—the small group of people to have hiked 10,000 miles in a year all had to knock off roughly 28 miles per day—the legal and financial hurdles Bushby faced, as well as his refusal to use transportation to bypass complicated terrain, meant that making miles was significantly more difficult for him.

The number of carts that Bushby used to carry his gear during the Goliath Expedition. The first, the Beast, survived three years before breaking down in Nicaragua. He used its replacement, the Beast 2, for the remaining 25 years. In an interview with The Guardian, Bushby called the cart “my Wilson,” referring to Tom Hanks’ famous volleyball and conversation partner from Castaway.

→ Baca artikel asli

Elon Musk’s Grokipedia is littered with mistakes. But Wikipedia could still learn some things from it.

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

All the latest headlines and insights from the world of digital journalism and publishing. Our daily newsletter is a must-read from newsrooms to executive suites.

We will never sell your address. Prefer a weekly email?

We have sent a confirmation link to . Click it and you are on the list. If it has not turned up in a minute or two, it may be in your spam folder.

Grokipedia shows that AI is actually pretty good at finding tidbits in the far corners of the internet.

When I saw Grokipedia’s articles about me, the memories came rushing back: my 19 years in minor league baseball, including that impressive season with the Montgomery Rebels when I hit 20 home runs. I was lean back then — 6 feet tall and 168 pounds — and earned a career batting average just under .300. I became a minor league coach and managed the Chicago White Sox for a short stint in 1970, which Grokipedia said “highlighted [my] readiness for higher-level responsibilities.”

I then attended Arizona State University, switched to a career in journalism, created the fact-checking site PolitiFact, won a Pulitzer Prize, and now teach journalism at Duke University.

Ah, the memories!…except that I never played minor league baseball, never managed the White Sox, and can only dream about being 6 feet tall. (I’m 5’8″.) Grokipedia, the AI-written encyclopedia of Elon Musk, has confused me with Marion Danne “Bill” Adair, who died in 2002.

That article and another one about “Bill Adair (journalist)” — which really is me — illustrate the many flaws of Grokipedia, which launched a year ago. The articles are littered with mistakes and superfluous details. They are terribly written and ridiculously long. If Grok, the AI engine that wrote this turgid prose, were a student in one of my writing courses, I would give it a D.

Still, the articles provide valuable lessons for Wikipedia editors who are debating how and when to use AI. Grokipedia adds new content and context that Wikipedia often lacks. It shows that AI is actually pretty good at finding tidbits in the far corners of the internet and summarizing them in sweeping prose. It can also provide balance to articles that need it.

Grok can’t write worth a lick, but it shows how Wikipedia can take advantage of AI at a time when overworked editors are trying to keep the encyclopedia alive.

Musk launched Grokipedia as an alternative to Wikipedia, which he said was too liberal. (He called it “Wokepedia.”) Still, he trusted it enough to have Grok copy hundreds of thousands of its articles, which is permitted under Wikipedia’s Creative Commons license, and then add content from elsewhere on the internet. With his usual bluster, Musk said his encyclopedia used a new version of his large language model that was good at “cogent analysis.” He said programmers told it to “cycle through the million most popular articles in Wikipedia and add, modify, and delete.” When Grokipedia launched, Musk said he had big aspirations to overtake Wikipedia, which has 68 million articles in more than 300 languages. He said his encyclopedia would be more complete and more balanced and would make smarter use of images and video. Maybe someday. For now, Grokipedia is a massive collection of overly long articles that are often out of date. Its flaws have been well-documented: Articles are littered with mistakes, peddle conspiracy theories, rely on low-credibility sources, and sometimes skew to the right. And although Grokipedia shows edit requests from readers and whether they are approved, the site is not as transparent as Wikipedia, which reveals details about its editors and every change they make. Last spring, Grokipedia mysteriously stopped updating, according to a report by researchers Renée DiResta and Ronald Robertson. It resumed in late September, still a hot mess, but with a more colorful homepage that always seems to feature its article about Musk. Grokipedia has two articles about me: the conflated baseball player one and “Bill Adair (journalist),” the real one, which is incredibly boring, even to me. Both borrow heavily from Wikipedia’s articles about us and enrich them with details found on assorted websites. Wikipedia’s article about me is nicely concise — just 700 words. It is written in the encyclopedia’s signature dry style and doesn’t contain any errors. The article recounts my career, some of the major news events I’ve covered, the two books I’ve written, and the awards I’ve won. But it highlights trivial events from my career, fails to emphasize larger ones, and lacks any criticism of my work. (I have not tried to improve the article because Wikipedia’s conflict of interest policy prohibits people from editing their own bios.) By contrast, Grokipedia’s “Bill Adair (journalist)” is painfully long — 5,600 words. Its greatest value would be to help someone suffering from a sleep disorder. Like a college student trying to meet a word count, Grok stuffed the article with needless details and redundant descriptions. The article tells you about my work with PolitiFact and then tells you again and again. It explains the site’s rating scale eight times and mentions my academic title four times. Despite labels that say “fact-checked by Grok,” both articles had lots of errors. I spotted 32 in the journalist one and so many in the baseball mashup that I couldn’t count them all. Mistakes are not surprising in AI writing, but I discovered interesting patterns that reveal the weaknesses of large language models. In the article about the real me, only seven of the errors were hallucinations in which Grok seemed to create a detail out of thin air. No, I did not win an award from Manhattan College, and no, PolitiFact did not begin by fact-checking the Tampa mayor’s race. Five other errors showed Grok was a lazy researcher that did not look broadly enough to check a claim. Grok said I am the director of Duke’s journalism program, but I left that job five years ago. The sources cited in the article don’t explain Grok’s mistake, so it seems likely it remembered an old announcement and failed to see a newer one that named my successor. The most prevalent errors were in a broad category I’d all cluelessness. These mistakes showed Grok didn’t fully understand what it read, like someone who skimmed an article too quickly. My favorite in this category was when I got credit for a big scoop in 2000 that George W. Bush had a DUI conviction, which the Grokipedia article said had an impact on “late-election discourse.” I wish! Yes, I was among the many journalists who covered that story, but I had no scoop. Together, the errors reveal that Grok is a knucklehead. It doesn’t know what’s important and what’s not. It is simply mashing together details from many web articles about me. Grok only knows what it found on the internet and it lacks experience and perspective to sort out what’s important. The writing is dreadful. The articles read as if Grok was trained on too many online resumes and corporate memos. Grok uses words and phrases like “commenced” and “transitioned” and “multidisciplinary practicum” and “hypotheses on fact-check efficacy.” A typical sentence: “Adair’s approach in this position reflected a commitment to undiluted sourcing and causal analysis of policy impacts, influencing subsequent innovations in political journalism.” Take a close look at that sentence and you’ll see it’s just a mush of buzzwords. I don’t know what “undiluted sourcing” is and have never used the phrase, nor would I refer to “causal analysis”…of anything. Grok is trying to impress us with an MBA vocabulary but is really just an epic bullshitter. Bernadette Meehan, CEO of the Wikimedia Foundation, which provides money and technical infrastructure for the encyclopedia, has called AI “an existential threat” to Wikipedia. The 25-year-old encyclopedia has long been a source and beneficiary of Google searches. Wikipedia provides the answers and in turn benefits from readers clicking through to read articles. But when people make a query to a chatbot, they often get the answer they need in an AI summary and don’t click through to read the encyclopedia article. That threatens donations because fewer readers see Wikipedia’s fundraising pitches. Wikipedia has banned content generated by LLMs, but that hasn’t stopped many contributors — some well-intentioned, some not — from dumping an avalanche of AI-written content on the site. That has put a huge burden on the site’s “New pages patrol,” the editors who approve articles, because they must weed out the lousy content. (Wikipedia has a helpful guide of LLMs’ favorite words and phrases, such as “tapestry” and “meticulously” and sentences that start with “additionally.”) Still, terrible as it is, Grokipedia offers lessons in how AI can help Wikipedia. Grok’s voracious internet searches sometimes find things that human editors don’t. The long article about me includes a substantive discussion of my research about lying in politics and my work with fact-checkers around the world, neither of which are in the Wikipedia version. Grokipedia also provides some welcome balance. When it offers context, Grokipedia can be a little heavy with hype (“His work has been lauded for innovating transparency in media…”) But it also included a “Criticisms and Controversies” section that details complaints that PolitiFact is biased against Republicans. That section has a healthy back and forth about the critiques and my response. That kind of balance could be helpful for Wikipedia, which has taken heat from Musk and others for being too lefty. Musk’s encyclopedia also has a playful spirit that Wikipedia could learn from. Grokipedia’s homepage features random samples of recent edits that provide a glimpse at how the site is being improved. Another Grokipedia feature reads articles aloud in an AI voice you select: Eve, who sounds like a BBC anchor; Ara, a serious woman with crisp diction; Leo, who might have been a professor at Oxford; and Sal, whose name suggests a sports bar but whose voice is NPR. My big takeaway from Grok is the importance of human judgment in curating knowledge. Grok is a bot trying to behave like a human editor. Its many mistakes and lousy writing show that AI is not ready to replace Wikipedia’s talented volunteers. Still, Grok shows that AI can be a tool that helps them research and write better articles. After seeing an AI encyclopedia up close through my experiences with Grok, I come away with hope. Yes, AI can be a sloppy researcher and an awful writer. But Grok’s performance, shaky as it was, highlighted AI’s readiness for higher-level responsibilities. Bill Adair, the Knight Professor of the Practice of Journalism and Public Policy at Duke University, is writing a book about how Wikipedia editors are adapting to the age of AI.

Musk launched Grokipedia as an alternative to Wikipedia, which he said was too liberal. (He called it “Wokepedia.”) Still, he trusted it enough to have Grok copy hundreds of thousands of its articles, which is permitted under Wikipedia’s Creative Commons license, and then add content from elsewhere on the internet.

With his usual bluster, Musk said his encyclopedia used a new version of his large language model that was good at “cogent analysis.” He said programmers told it to “cycle through the million most popular articles in Wikipedia and add, modify, and delete.”

When Grokipedia launched, Musk said he had big aspirations to overtake Wikipedia, which has 68 million articles in more than 300 languages. He said his encyclopedia would be more complete and more balanced and would make smarter use of images and video.

For now, Grokipedia is a massive collection of overly long articles that are often out of date. Its flaws have been well-documented: Articles are littered with mistakes, peddle conspiracy theories, rely on low-credibility sources, and sometimes skew to the right. And although Grokipedia shows edit requests from readers and whether they are approved, the site is not as transparent as Wikipedia, which reveals details about its editors and every change they make.

Last spring, Grokipedia mysteriously stopped updating, according to a report by researchers Renée DiResta and Ronald Robertson. It resumed in late September, still a hot mess, but with a more colorful homepage that always seems to feature its article about Musk.

Grokipedia has two articles about me: the conflated baseball player one and “Bill Adair (journalist),” the real one, which is incredibly boring, even to me. Both borrow heavily from Wikipedia’s articles about us and enrich them with details found on assorted websites. Wikipedia’s article about me is nicely concise — just 700 words. It is written in the encyclopedia’s signature dry style and doesn’t contain any errors. The article recounts my career, some of the major news events I’ve covered, the two books I’ve written, and the awards I’ve won. But it highlights trivial events from my career, fails to emphasize larger ones, and lacks any criticism of my work. (I have not tried to improve the article because Wikipedia’s conflict of interest policy prohibits people from editing their own bios.) By contrast, Grokipedia’s “Bill Adair (journalist)” is painfully long — 5,600 words. Its greatest value would be to help someone suffering from a sleep disorder. Like a college student trying to meet a word count, Grok stuffed the article with needless details and redundant descriptions. The article tells you about my work with PolitiFact and then tells you again and again. It explains the site’s rating scale eight times and mentions my academic title four times. Despite labels that say “fact-checked by Grok,” both articles had lots of errors. I spotted 32 in the journalist one and so many in the baseball mashup that I couldn’t count them all. Mistakes are not surprising in AI writing, but I discovered interesting patterns that reveal the weaknesses of large language models. In the article about the real me, only seven of the errors were hallucinations in which Grok seemed to create a detail out of thin air. No, I did not win an award from Manhattan College, and no, PolitiFact did not begin by fact-checking the Tampa mayor’s race. Five other errors showed Grok was a lazy researcher that did not look broadly enough to check a claim. Grok said I am the director of Duke’s journalism program, but I left that job five years ago. The sources cited in the article don’t explain Grok’s mistake, so it seems likely it remembered an old announcement and failed to see a newer one that named my successor. The most prevalent errors were in a broad category I’d all cluelessness. These mistakes showed Grok didn’t fully understand what it read, like someone who skimmed an article too quickly. My favorite in this category was when I got credit for a big scoop in 2000 that George W. Bush had a DUI conviction, which the Grokipedia article said had an impact on “late-election discourse.” I wish! Yes, I was among the many journalists who covered that story, but I had no scoop. Together, the errors reveal that Grok is a knucklehead. It doesn’t know what’s important and what’s not. It is simply mashing together details from many web articles about me. Grok only knows what it found on the internet and it lacks experience and perspective to sort out what’s important. The writing is dreadful. The articles read as if Grok was trained on too many online resumes and corporate memos. Grok uses words and phrases like “commenced” and “transitioned” and “multidisciplinary practicum” and “hypotheses on fact-check efficacy.” A typical sentence: “Adair’s approach in this position reflected a commitment to undiluted sourcing and causal analysis of policy impacts, influencing subsequent innovations in political journalism.” Take a close look at that sentence and you’ll see it’s just a mush of buzzwords. I don’t know what “undiluted sourcing” is and have never used the phrase, nor would I refer to “causal analysis”…of anything. Grok is trying to impress us with an MBA vocabulary but is really just an epic bullshitter. Bernadette Meehan, CEO of the Wikimedia Foundation, which provides money and technical infrastructure for the encyclopedia, has called AI “an existential threat” to Wikipedia. The 25-year-old encyclopedia has long been a source and beneficiary of Google searches. Wikipedia provides the answers and in turn benefits from readers clicking through to read articles. But when people make a query to a chatbot, they often get the answer they need in an AI summary and don’t click through to read the encyclopedia article. That threatens donations because fewer readers see Wikipedia’s fundraising pitches. Wikipedia has banned content generated by LLMs, but that hasn’t stopped many contributors — some well-intentioned, some not — from dumping an avalanche of AI-written content on the site. That has put a huge burden on the site’s “New pages patrol,” the editors who approve articles, because they must weed out the lousy content. (Wikipedia has a helpful guide of LLMs’ favorite words and phrases, such as “tapestry” and “meticulously” and sentences that start with “additionally.”) Still, terrible as it is, Grokipedia offers lessons in how AI can help Wikipedia. Grok’s voracious internet searches sometimes find things that human editors don’t. The long article about me includes a substantive discussion of my research about lying in politics and my work with fact-checkers around the world, neither of which are in the Wikipedia version. Grokipedia also provides some welcome balance. When it offers context, Grokipedia can be a little heavy with hype (“His work has been lauded for innovating transparency in media…”) But it also included a “Criticisms and Controversies” section that details complaints that PolitiFact is biased against Republicans. That section has a healthy back and forth about the critiques and my response. That kind of balance could be helpful for Wikipedia, which has taken heat from Musk and others for being too lefty. Musk’s encyclopedia also has a playful spirit that Wikipedia could learn from. Grokipedia’s homepage features random samples of recent edits that provide a glimpse at how the site is being improved. Another Grokipedia feature reads articles aloud in an AI voice you select: Eve, who sounds like a BBC anchor; Ara, a serious woman with crisp diction; Leo, who might have been a professor at Oxford; and Sal, whose name suggests a sports bar but whose voice is NPR. My big takeaway from Grok is the importance of human judgment in curating knowledge. Grok is a bot trying to behave like a human editor. Its many mistakes and lousy writing show that AI is not ready to replace Wikipedia’s talented volunteers. Still, Grok shows that AI can be a tool that helps them research and write better articles. After seeing an AI encyclopedia up close through my experiences with Grok, I come away with hope. Yes, AI can be a sloppy researcher and an awful writer. But Grok’s performance, shaky as it was, highlighted AI’s readiness for higher-level responsibilities. Bill Adair, the Knight Professor of the Practice of Journalism and Public Policy at Duke University, is writing a book about how Wikipedia editors are adapting to the age of AI.

Something potentially explosive could be approaching for Sui crypto (SUI) by January 1, 2027. Anthropic’s Claude AI predicts a potentially unprecedented move for the Layer-1 project based on an intentionally extreme, over-the-top scenario built around a hypothetical landmark catalyst that isn’t completely impossible.

The concept is that a consortium of leading gaming studios, social media platforms, and AI application developers officially chooses Sui as the preferred high-performance blockchain for real-time consumer experiences and autonomous AI agents.

Within this framework, large-scale on-chain games, social applications, and agent-based systems would operate primarily on Sui’s object-centric architecture. This would drive sustained transaction volume and establish SUI as essential infrastructure for the next generation of interactive digital applications.

(SOURCE: Claude AI Predicts an Insane Sui Move)

On the higher timeframes, SUI has been consolidating in the $0.88–$0.91 region after a broader pullback from higher levels earlier in the cycle. Price continues to hold above key intermediate supports while facing resistance around the $0.95–$1.05 zone.

In a standard bull market, a sustained breakout above $1.20–$1.40 would confirm intermediate strength and open the path to higher-cycle targets and a longer-term recovery toward the prior all-time high near $5.35.

Under the extreme real-time consumer and AI-agent adoption scenario outlined above, clearing significant resistance levels would likely trigger powerful measured-move extensions and multi-cycle Fibonacci projections from the longer-term base.

Momentum indicators currently sit in oversold-to-neutral territory with substantial room to run into the extreme overbought readings typical of parabolic advances.

Key nearer-term supports sit near $0.85–$0.88 and $0.80–$0.83; holding those levels would keep the broader recovery structure intact.

Several external developments could enhance this optimistic scenario. Globally, a durable resolution to major geopolitical conflicts, such as the Russia-Ukraine war or a lasting ceasefire involving Iran, along with normalization of the Strait of Hormuz, could reduce risk premiums and encourage capital to flow back into growth and technology assets.

Economically, a clear shift by major central banks towards more accommodative monetary policies, combined with elevated liquidity conditions, would support risk-taking across markets.

In the cryptocurrency space, continued growth in Bitcoin and Ethereum ETF inflows, increased institutional and retail adoption of high-performing Layer-1 networks, rising interest in on-chain gaming and social applications, and renewed enthusiasm for altcoins would create a favorable liquidity environment. This would particularly benefit object-centric, high-throughput chains like Sui.

Under this highly speculative scenario, and assuming a full-blown crypto bull market returns alongside supportive external conditions, Sui (SUI) could reach between $25 and $45, with a stretch target toward $70+ by January 1, 2027.

This remains pure speculation and entertainment, not a realistic base-case outlook. The catalyst would require extraordinary coordination among major gaming, social, and AI developers that is currently highly unlikely.

5 Mobility Moves to Improve Your Bulgarian Split Squats

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Sign up below to receive our newest workout routines, recipes, news stories, and offers from our partners

With the right plan and the right discipline, you can get seriously shredded in just 28 days.

At age 62, "Big Bill" shares his wisdom to dominate one of the ultimate strength marks.

Follow these fit women we're crushing on for inspiration, workout ideas, and motivation.

The Bulgarian split squat is the exercise almost all lifters love to hate. Still, you cannot deny its benefits, including increased quad and glute strength and size and reduced strength imbalances, which can improve bilateral squats.

But it has a funny way of humbling those who perform it because it demands more than strength. It requires hip mobility, ankle dorsiflexion, pelvic control, and single-leg stability.

If one of those is off, let’s hope you’re not on camera.

Here, I’ll cover common BSS performance issues and five mobility exercises to help you get the most out of the most dreaded exercise in gym history.

Before blaming the Bulgarian split squat for all your woes, let’s test whether it’s a mobility issue.

The goal isn’t a perfect-looking rep but finding any performance limitations that are holding back your gains. But before adding mobility drills, let’s see if it’s a setup issue.

Not every Bulgarian split squat problem requires another corrective drill. Before blaming tight hips and reaching for the foam roller, a few small adjustments could make a big difference.

About to perform Bulgarian split squats? Here’s what your body needs to bring to that first set.

A well-performed Bulgarian split squat requires hip and ankle mobility and stability everywhere else. Mobility gets you into position, but strength and stability let you own it.

5 MOBILITY MOVES TO OWN THE BOTTOM POSITION OF YOUR BULGARIAN SPLIT SQUAT

If the adjustments above cleaned up your performance issues, great, you’re good to go. If not, the five mobility exercises below are exactly what you need.

The Bulgarian split squat may be an up-and-down exercise, but your hips must also control side-to-side movement to keep your pelvis and knees stable. That’s where the Lateral Lunge to Box earns its keep. It improves adductor mobility and builds hip strength and control, allowing you to reach the bottom without issues.

Why Lifters Need It: The lateral lunge to box trains the adductors through a lengthened position while developing the hip strength and mobility needed to resist excessive lateral shifting.

Programming Suggestions: You can perform this unloaded as a warm-up for 10 reps per side, or load it up as an accessory movement for your squats and deadlifts for 2-4 sets of 8-15 reps per side.

https://www.youtube.com/shorts/TML8Vqy-ACQ

If your rear hip flexors and quads are tight, it’s hard to get to the bottom without arching your lower back. The Couch Stretch targets the hip flexors and quads that can restrict the rear leg.

Why Lifters Need It: The rectus femoris is unique among the quadriceps because it crosses both the hip and knee joints. If it’s restricted, you may feel excessive pulling in the front of your thigh, arch your lower back, or shorten your range of motion to avoid discomfort. The Couch Stretch can restore length to the rectus femoris and the quads.

Programming Suggestions: Perform two sets of 30-60 seconds per side as part of your warmup.

If you struggle to get your knees over your toes, your heel may lift, your balance may suffer, and your knee may have difficulty moving forward. The Weighted Ankle Rocker helps you build that missing range without any mobility circus tricks.

Why Lifters Need It: During the Bulgarian split squat, your front ankle moves into dorsiflexion as your knee travels forward over it. Sufficient dorsiflexion lets you distribute pressure evenly across your foot as you lower to the bottom position.

Programming Suggestions: Perform two sets of 8 reps per side as part of your warm-up.

4. Front-Foot Elevated Split Squat With Pause

The front-foot-elevated split squat made the list because it promotes deeper hip and knee flexion. You might be able to get into a deep split squat, but if you bounce out of the bottom or lack the strength to hold it, this variation builds the mobility and strength needed for the BSS bottom position.

Why Lifters Need It: If you lack strength at the end of the range of motion, you may rush the eccentric or rely too much on the rear leg. Elevating the front foot lets you practice a deeper split squat position, while adding a pause builds strength there.

Programming Suggestions: Perform two sets of 6 reps per side with a three-second pause at the bottom as part of your warmup.

5. Half-Kneeling Hip Flexor Mobilization With Overhead Reach

There are other hip flexors besides the rectus femoris needed for the Bulgarian split squat. Your iliopsoas, the other hip flexor, also affects how your rear hip extends. This mobility drill strengthens hip extension while maintaining pelvic stability and improves iliopsoas length. The overhead reach and side bend add extra range and intensity.

Why Lifters Need It: When your trailing hip lacks full extension due to short hip flexors, you may compensate by arching your lower back or tipping your pelvis forward as you descend. A few sets of these will have your hips primed for the BSS, even if you are not.

Programming Suggestions: Perform two sets of 8 reaches per side, pausing for three seconds in the stretched position.

The Bulgarian split squat is tough enough without performance issues fighting you on every rep. The gains happen when you reach the bottom, when the muscles are under a loaded stretch. If getting there is a problem, fixing the setup and mobility issues will help.

Mobility gets you to the bottom, and strength and stability get you out. Master both, and this dreaded exercise becomes one of your best tools for building stronger legs.

Utilized correctly, this underrated can add lower-body gains with little injury risk.

Improve hip flexion, hamstring length, and pelvic control so each rep feels smoother and stronger.

Target your inner thighs to fix caving knees, boost hip stability, and add power to every squat.

NASA telescope plummets back to Earth

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Updated Nov 4, 2026 3:59 PM EST

Update 11/4/26 3:55 p.m. EST: The United State Space Force announced the Swift Observatory re-entered Earth’s atmosphere at 7:39 a.m. EST over Indonesia’s North Sumatra region—roughly 3.4 degrees north latitude and 98.6 degrees east longitude. NASA is confident that most of the telescope burned up upon re-entry, although some parts may have survived the descent.A revolutionary NASA telescope is making its way back to Earth—but will not survive the journey. The United States Space Force has honed its estimate for the Neil Gehreis Swift Observatory’s fiery atmospheric reentry. According to the latest calculations, the 2,500-pound orbital telescope will largely burn-up during its descent on or around Wednesday, November 4.

“We’ve known this day would come since before Swift was launched,” Pennsylvania State University astronomer and Swift control lead John Nousek told AP News. “At least in low-Earth orbit, what goes up must come down. And that end is catastrophic.”

Launched in 2004 on what was expected to be a two-year mission, the orbital space telescope ultimately spent nearly 22 years gazing deep into the cosmos. Swift’s contributions to astronomy, particularly the study of gamma-ray bursts (GRBs), cannot be overstated. After more than two decades in space, the observatory examined over 2,000 GRBs and more than 1,400 supernovae. Swift also recorded rarely seen tidal disruption events, in which stars are devoured by a nearby black hole. Most recently, the telescope was instrumental in documenting comet 3I/ATLAS, the third interstellar object ever detected passing through the solar system.

An estimated 7,000 published scientific papers contain data collected by Swift, which averages out to roughly six papers per week during its almost 22-year run.

“Swift leaves us with an extraordinary archive of multiwavelength observations that will fuel new discoveries for decades more,” Shawn Domagal-Goldman, director of NASA’s Astrophysics Division, said in October.

While Swift will ultimately succumb to Earth’s gravitational pull, it nearly received a reprieve that would have extended its mission even longer. In July, NASA and the private space company Katalyst collaborated on an attempt to bump Swift back into a higher orbit using a specially designed robotic satellite. Unfortunately, the device malfunctioned while en route to the observatory, forcing mission engineers to abandon the attempt and leave Swift to its fate.

That didn’t mean the telescope was ready to wind down just yet, however. Astronomers continued to utilize Swift in the months since then, and only recently said their farewells to the intrepid, tenacious observatory.

UPDATE November 2, 1:23 p.m. EST: This story has been updated to reflect the latest prediction from the U.S. Space Force.

Melting permafrost has birthed a new type of large landslide

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

Every print subscription comes with full digital access

Wildfires and water underneath the ground could dislodge large chunks of frozen earth

In 2018, an immense landslide in Canada’s Mackenzie Valley dammed the Redstone River (shown) and created a 3.5-kilometer-long lake. Researchers say the event was a new type of large landslide called a permafrost detachment slide.

This is a human-written story voiced by AI. Got feedback? Take our survey . (See our AI policy here .)

In 2018, a strange and enormous landslide occurred in Canada’s Northwest Territories. A slab of earth roughly equivalent in volume to seven Great Pyramids of Giza suddenly began sliding downhill, leaving behind a barren scar in the forested landscape. But what puzzled scientists was that the slide had initiated in frozen ground that had been glued in place for years.

An analysis of the event reveals that it represents a new class of catastrophic landslides called permafrost detachment slides, researchers report in the Sept. 28 Geophysical Research Letters. The slides may be set in motion by wildfires that occurred decades earlier, destabilizing the ground and opening cracks through which water infiltrated deep underground. Over time, that water may have thawed the frozen layer from below, eventually unleashing the entire frozen layer in a catastrophic slide.

We summarize the week's scientific breakthroughs every Thursday.

Large landslides don’t normally mobilize so much still-frozen material, and when they do begin in thawing layers they usually move slowly, says Dmitry Nicolsky, a geophysicist studying permafrost at the University of Alaska Fairbanks who was not involved in the study. But he says this new research suggests scientists need to better study what’s occurring beneath the surface in permafrost regions, because “we don’t know how [the ground] will move — it might go slowly or it might go catastrophically.”

The inspiration for the research manifested in 2019, when permafrost geologist Joseph Young, based at the University of Alberta in Edmonton, Canada, at the time, was helicoptering to a field site in the Northwest Territories to assist a colleague’s research. In some of the valleys out there, the slopes were “just collapsing with landslides everywhere,” says Young, who is now at an engineering firm in Edmonton. That was surprising because they were in an area where the permafrost — ground that has been frozen for at least two consecutive years — shouldn’t have been thawing much.

Once on the ground, more puzzling details emerged. “Traditionally with permafrost, it melts from the top down, and that releases this slurry of muddy material,” Young says. But at some of the landslide sites, large blocks of still-frozen ground seemed to have been moved hundreds of meters, he says.

Young and his colleagues studied two of the largest slides in the region that had moved big chunks of permafrost. These were the 2018 slide, which mobilized 18 million cubic meters of earth and dammed the Redstone River, and another event in 2017 that moved 4.5 million cubic meters of material.

Field observations revealed that both events had initiated along flat sliding surfaces roughly 20 meters underground, where the permafrost layer’s base was identified. Meanwhile, satellite observations showed that the ground at both sites had been gradually moving meters each year for decades.

Young and his colleagues hypothesized that climate warming and wildfire had somehow played a role in causing the slides. They set up instruments nearby that tracked ground temperature from 2020 to 2022, focusing on spots either with undisturbed forest or where wildfire had burned about 25 years earlier.

They then plugged that data into computer simulations to estimate how the ground would have evolved in the decades after a fire. Based on the results, the researchers concluded that wildfire and extreme summer temperatures can warm and weaken the frozen ground surface, causing it to start creeping and form tension cracks. As these cracks grow, water can penetrate deeper into the frozen ground and eventually reach beneath the permafrost layer, causing it to thaw from below and eventually detach.

More than 150 other slides in the region, identified via satellite observations, may also have been permafrost detachment slides, Young says. This could occur wherever there’s a relatively thin and warm permafrost layer and a rapid warming event to set things in motion, he says.

Permafrost is thawing in cold regions around the world, and these huge slides are very visual manifestations of that, Young says. “This is a direct reality of a changing climate.”

J.M. Young et al. Recent intensification (2004 – 2020) of permafrost mass‐wasting in the central Mackenzie Valley foothills is a legacy of past forest fire disturbances. Geophysical Research Letters. 2022. doi: 10.1029/2022GL100559

AWS Weekly Roundup: GPT-6.1 Terra on Bedrock, Step Functions as agent tools, and more (November 2, 2026)

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

AWS is the world's most comprehensive cloud, enabling organizations to accelerate innovation, reduce costs, and scale more efficiently

Migrate to modernize → Modernize to unlock data → unlock data to run AI

Get started with one of these featured services or browse all

From startups to enterprises, find AWS solutions scaled to your needs. Access tailored resources, pricing models, and support levels.

The agentic launches that stayed with me last week had nothing to do with how capable a model is. They were about reach. AWS Step Functions, which already orchestrates over 220 AWS services, can now be handed to an agent as a tool, so workflows teams already trust became callable without a rewrite. In the same week, the AI blog published how one engagement platform exposed roughly 400 internal API operations the same way. Neither story involves rebuilding anything, only describing what exists and letting the agent find it.

The other thread was cost. OpenAI’s newest Terra model reached Amazon Bedrock priced well below the model beside it, and two post-quantum launches landed for a deadline nobody can put on a calendar yet. Now, let’s get into this week’s AWS news…

HeadlinesOpenAI GPT-6.1 Terra is now generally available on Amazon Bedrock, and what interests me is where it sits rather than what it tops. Terra is the everyday-workload line of the GPT-6 family. In OpenAI’s Agents’ Last Exam evaluation it scored 15 percentage points higher than GPT-5.6 Terra, performed on par with GPT-6 Astra, and completed tasks more than three times faster than both. Those are OpenAI’s figures, not an independent test. The price is what I would check first: input and output tokens are at least 50% lower than GPT-6.1 Sol and GPT-5.6 Terra. It takes up to 1 million input tokens with text and image input, and you can reach it from the Bedrock APIs or ChatGPT Work and Codex.

AWS Step Functions workflows are now available as AI agent tools through Amazon Bedrock AgentCore Gateway, which connects agents to tools over the Model Context Protocol (MCP). The division of labor is the idea: the agent decides when to run a workflow and what input to pass; Step Functions runs each step exactly as you defined it. A support agent can trigger a refund, and the eligibility check, the manager approval, and the credit still happen in that order every time, because the ordering was never the model’s to improvise. Your existing Standard and Express workflows qualify as connector targets, and agents can also look up runs. It’s available in all Regions where AgentCore Gateway is, at standard rates.

Last week’s launches Here are some launches and updates from this past week that caught my attention:

AWS Key Management Service now supports ML-KEM for post-quantum key encapsulation – You can now establish a shared secret with a post-quantum algorithm using AWS KMS keys, with private keys in FIPS 140-3 Level 3 validated HSMs. All three NIST FIPS 203 parameter sets are supported through Encapsulate, which produces a random 32-byte shared secret and its ciphertext, and Decapsulate, which recovers it. The threat is harvest now, decrypt later: an adversary records today’s key exchange and recovers the secret once quantum computers are powerful enough. It’s available everywhere KMS runs except the Middle East (UAE and Bahrain) and China Regions. Amazon Aurora PostgreSQL added post-quantum TLS key exchange the same week, on versions 18 and higher.

AWS IAM Identity Center now supports cross-Region trusted identity propagation – You can now carry a user’s identity with their request to AWS services in another Region. This removes a genuine architectural constraint: trusted identity propagation used to require every service accessing that identity to sit in the same Region, which left two unappealing options, broad IAM roles that cannot distinguish individual users, or consolidating all your services into a single Region. Now the identity travels with the request automatically, so you grant access by user and group membership and audit who accessed what. It’s available at no additional cost everywhere IAM Identity Center runs.

Amazon ECS now supports GPU sharing with NVIDIA Multi-Process Service (MPS) – You can now run multiple Amazon ECS tasks concurrently on a single NVIDIA GPU, the practical answer to work that only ever uses a fraction of one, like small-model inference and multi-model serving. You declare the GPU memory each container needs and can optionally cap its share of GPU compute, and ECS places tasks on GPUs with available capacity as your services scale, with no code changes. It works on ECS Managed Instances and ECS on Amazon EC2, and on Managed Instances it watches the MPS control daemon and replaces the instance if it turns unhealthy. It’s available in all commercial and AWS GovCloud (US) Regions.

AWS Lambda supports Seekable OCI (SOCI) lazy loading for Lambda Managed Instances – You can now have AWS Lambda fetch only the files needed at each stage of startup instead of pulling a whole container image first, which AWS puts at up to 70% off container startup time. You create a SOCI index, push it to Amazon ECR, point the image URI at the indexed tag, and Lambda detects it automatically. The technique also reached Amazon SageMaker AI training and processing jobs, with benchmarks: lazy loading held at 3 to 11 seconds regardless of image size, against a median 3 minutes 42 seconds for a 13.2 GB image, a range they report as 8x to 46x.

Amazon EKS Auto Mode now supports Kubernetes Gateway API – You can now declare how traffic reaches your applications with Gateway and HTTPRoute resources on Amazon EKS without installing a separate controller, and EKS Auto Mode provisions and manages the underlying AWS load balancing infrastructure. You get HTTP and gRPC routing, cross-namespace routing, weighted traffic splitting, header-based routing, and automatic TLS certificate discovery through AWS Certificate Manager. Existing Ingress workloads keep working alongside them, so this is additive rather than a migration. Gateway API support is included with EKS Auto Mode; standard load balancing charges apply.

For a full list of AWS announcements, be sure to keep an eye on the What’s New with AWS page.

Other AWS news Here are some additional posts and resources that you might find interesting:

How Nubank automated disaster recovery with the AWS DevOps Agent – Nubank serves more than 140 million customers across Brazil, Mexico, and Colombia, and classifies over 300 business flows as critical, each tested on a regulator’s schedule. Nubank reports that handing evidence collection and report writing to the AWS DevOps Agent cut a test cycle from about 4.3 hours to roughly 40 minutes, and quadrupled flow coverage in 2026 to more than 160. One execution terminated more than 900 EKS instances under synthetic traffic with AWS Fault Injection Service, producing the evidence its regulator needed.

How MoEngage turned over 400 APIs into agent tools with AgentCore Gateway – Roughly 400 internal REST API operations, built by 25 teams, sitting in private VPCs across seven Regions, with tenant identity that could never become a parameter the model gets to set. MoEngage estimates the managed path saved about six months of platform engineering. The detail worth stealing: a full tool listing would have crossed 20,000 tokens per connection, so agents search for tools by describing a task instead of receiving the catalog.

Reducing petabyte-scale S3 storage costs with evidence-based lifecycle rules – The hard part isn’t deciding to act, it’s knowing which data has gone cold and where it can safely live. This uses S3 Storage Lens groups and Storage Class Analysis to prove access patterns before moving anything. In the engagement described, 5.8 PB of logs in Amazon S3 Standard at approximately $241,000 per month showed an access ratio below 0.1% past 90 days, and a 90-day transition to Glacier Instant Retrieval cut the bill by roughly 70%, a figure the author ties to that workload.

Upcoming AWS events Check your calendar and sign up for upcoming AWS events:

AWS re:Invent – re:Invent returns to Las Vegas from November 30 to December 4, with more than 2,200 sessions. The event catalog is live and reserved seating is open, so register and claim your spots before they fill up.

AWS Community Days – Community-led conferences planned and delivered by community leaders. The 2026 season is winding down as re:Invent approaches, so check the hub for what’s still on.

Join the AWS Builder Center to connect with builders, share solutions, and access content that supports your development. Browse here for upcoming AWS-led in-person and virtual events and developer-focused events.

That’s all for this week. Check back next Monday for another Weekly Roundup!

This post is part of our Weekly Roundup series. Check back each week for a quick roundup of interesting news and announcements from AWS!

Daniel Abib is a senior specialist solutions architect at AWS, focused on generative AI and Amazon Bedrock — and passionate about serverless. He helps startups and enterprises build AI-powered applications and modernize with cloud-native architectures. A three-time Ironman finisher and four-time re:Invent speaker, he brings the same endurance mindset to building cloud solutions.

Big wins for PSV and Twente close gap on leaders Feyenoord

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

See more DutchNews articles in your Google search results

A late equaliser for Fortuna Sittard in De Kuip proved costly for Feyenoord as PSV and Twente racked up huge victories to move within two points of the Eredivisie leaders.

Feyenoord paid the price for failing to finish off the Limburgers, with Luciano Valente’s 59th-minute goal the only reward for their dominance.

Fortuna equalised with four minutes left when Shiloh ’t Zand, on loan from Feyenoord, was allowed to run through to the penalty area and beat Tjark Ernst with a low shot.

Feyenoord coach Giovanni van Bronckhorst made no effort to hide his disappointment. “We know that Fortuna are the kind of team who can score from the few attacks they have,” he said. “If you don’t score the goals, things like this can happen.”

Second-placed Ajax are a point behind after a narrow win at home against AZ ended an 11-match winless streak against the Alkmaar club, as Tolu Arokodare’s early header proved decisive.

Twente recorded the biggest win of the weekend with a 9-0 demolition of Excelsior in Rotterdam, with Wout Weghorst scoring four times and Sondre Ørjasaeter notching up five assists.

Excelsior collapsed following the fifth-minute dismissal of Nesto Groen for a reckless high challenge. The home side were 5-0 down at half-time, with Weghorst claiming a hat-trick in the opening 33 minutes.

PSV bagged seven goals in a one-sided encounter with bottom club Willem II, with all the goals coming from different scorers including Ricardo Pepi, who came on in the second half after a six-week absence through injury.

Telstar opened up a three-point gap at the bottom over ADO Den Haag with a 4-2 win against Utrecht in a painful return to Velsen-Zuid for Telstar’s former coach Anthony Correia.

ADO went down 1-0 in Zwolle, with PEC’s Dylan Mbayo scoring the only goal with a first-time finish on the stroke of half-time.

The match between Cambuur Leeuwarden and Go Ahead Eagles was suspended in the 82nd minute after visiting fans threw fireworks onto the pitch.

The clubs agreed with the KNVB not to play the final minutes and allow Cambuur’s 4-0 advantage to stand as the final result.

Go Ahead’s director Jan-Willem van Dop said the club would take action against those responsible: “I am ashamed on behalf a small group of supporters who ruined things for the club and a large hare of the fans in the away end,” he said.

What’s !important #20: GTA 6 Scroll Effects, Scrollspies, random()

📅 2026-11-02  |  Read Source →

This is the final issue of What’s !important on CSS-Tricks.com. The good news is that it’s coming back in an expanded form as the CSS-Tricks newsletter. I had ya there for a second, didn’t I? Sorry, I couldn’t decide whether to trick or treat, so I went for both. Happy Halloween!

The newsletter officially kicks off on November 10th. That’s after a way-too-long four-year hiatus. What the heck, right? It’s not only high time for it to come back, but for you to get on the list. You’ll get hand-written and hand-curated news, links, and editorials that you can only find there. Join the party with me!

So, let’s go out with a big bang for our “final” issue. It’s packed with some stuff that I’ve been holding onto since before the site came back to life earlier this week, but also some new stuff, and I can’t wait to finally get it out to you.

Scooch over Apple, Rockstar has something they’d like to say about fancy scrolling effects (to be fair, they’ve had 13 years to create them). First, check out the GTA 6 website (which oddly doesn’t work in Firefox), then watch Kevin Powell’s GTA 6 scroll effects walkthrough, which is frankly the most fun that you can have while waiting (and waiting) for GTA 6.

Mojtaba Seyedi demonstrated how to build a scrollspy with modern CSS. What’s a scrollspy, you ask? Well, if you have a navigation that links to different sections of the page, a scrollspy highlights the section that you’re currently on in the navigation.

Brecht De Ruyte showcased and explained some absolutely stunning random() demos.

Lea Verou explained, in detail, the different ways of implementing icons on the web. Many exciting CSS features of the future are mentioned, including Linked Parameters, ident(), @mixin and @apply, the @property shorthand, and more, so it’s a really deep dive with a lot of intrigue.

Kilian Valkhof suggested a universal pseudo-selector. I mean, the universal selector (*) doesn’t select pseudo-elements, which is why almost every website uses the following CSS snippet, right?

But there are way more of them now (e.g., ::backdrop and ::details-content, to name just a couple of common ones in the context of box-sizing: border-box), so Kilian suggests ::*.

Declan Chidlow told us which new HTML and CSS features are useful for design systems. My favorites are text-box, sibling-index(), sibling-count(), @scope, and @custom-media, which are very handy even outside of design systems.

Adam Argyle shared a sweet CSS trick to make
